## Chapter 25 — The font bug and the frontend agent's first real iteration (2026-06-13)

This is the chapter where the verification machinery built across Chapters 22–24 *caught a real defect*, and the frontend agent did its **first real iteration** — not a greenfield dogfood, but fixing a bug it could now detect through its own screenshot self-review. It is the strongest single piece of evidence in the arc that the loop *closes*: build → see → catch → fix → re-see → prove.

**The CI/Linux render exposed what local review hid.** Reviewing PR #10's CI-generated screenshots (pulled via `bin/ci-screenshots`), the assistant compared the CI render to its local capture and caught the bug:

> "That download just paid for itself — look at the CI render versus what I saw locally. Same page, **different font** … That exposes a real bug: our `--font-sans: 'Inter', 'Helvetica Neue', system-ui` stack **never actually loads Inter** — there's no `@font-face`/web font. So Mac users get Helvetica Neue, and **everyone on Linux/Windows/Android gets a plain system sans, not the 'sharp grotesk' BLEND intends.** Local review hid that; the neutral CI environment surfaced it."

This is precisely the *"incidental CI benefit"* the assistant had named in Chapter 23 — rendering in CI's neutral Linux env as a proxy for a non-Mac visitor — earning its keep on its first real outing. The local Mac render silently fell back to Helvetica Neue, hiding the missing web font; the Linux runner showed a generic sans, and the line-wrap difference (*"'verifiable to the decimal' breaks in a new place"*) made it visible.

**A small detour the user opened, recorded honestly.** Before the fix, the assistant laid out two ways CI could *actually evaluate* pixels (rather than just archive them): a **Claude-vision CI step** posting an advisory PR comment (*"the most on-thesis option: it's literally 'judge taste with a model,' in CI"* — but recommended as advisory, not a hard gate, *"because taste is fuzzy"*), and **deterministic visual-regression diffing** (*"catches 'it changed,' not 'it's bad'"*). The user's choice was the cleaner session-side design (Chapter 23's loop), and the assistant deferred the dedicated vision subagent until calculator pages make the image-token cost worth offloading. The font fix was prioritized as *"a real defect."*

**The fix: deliver the design, don't change it.** The user said *"font fix next."* The assistant framed the fix as design-faithful: *"`DESIGN.md` already specifies Inter, so we **deliver** it (self-host via `@font-face` + Propshaft — keeping the app self-contained, no Google-CDN runtime dependency), rather than changing the design to system fonts."* It branched #11 off `main` and **dispatched the frontend agent** — its first real iteration.

**What the agent did — and the boundary it respected.** The assistant's review of the agent's work is the experiment data point of the chapter:

> "The frontend agent **self-hosted Inter**, **self-reviewed its own screenshots** (it has vision) to confirm the font loaded, added a `document.fonts.check` test to *prove* delivery, and **respected its boundary** — it flagged the `DESIGN.md` edit for me and confirmed no `config/` change rather than crossing into either."

The independent review confirmed the craft: *"the `@font-face` is correct (variable `font-weight: 400 800`, `swap`, woff2), the preload **includes `crossorigin: 'anonymous'`** (the thing that's easy to miss — it's there), and the new test asserts the font *loaded* via the Font Loading API, not just that it's named."* Three independent signals agreed the font was real — the assistant's *visual* check (*"the headline now renders in **Inter** … the hero reflowed: 'verifiable to / the decimal.'"*), the Font Loading API test, and Propshaft fingerprinting. The agent flagged the one thing it could not touch — `DESIGN.md §2` had said "no web-font fetch required," now stale — and the assistant made that docs edit itself (`d2f0908`), exactly the boundary the agent def encodes.

**Cross-platform proof — the loop's payoff.** PR #14 went green, and the assistant ran the **visual gate for real**: *"the `document.fonts.check('800 16px Inter')` assertion passed *on the Linux runner*, so Inter loads cross-platform."* Then it pulled the Linux screenshots and confirmed by eye:

> "**Visual gate passed — cross-platform proof complete.** The CI/Linux render now shows **Inter** (compare to the earlier Linux renders that fell back to a generic sans — same reflow as my local Inter capture, the fallback is gone). … The whole loop worked: CI generated → `bin/ci-screenshots` pulled → I reviewed → confirmed."

PR #14 (`Closes #11`) merged as `d515f12`, issue #11 closed.

**Where it paused.** Per the user's plan — *"once this wraps up, let's journal and pause, we'll start the backend agent in a future session. We want to verify what we have remaining for the frontend agent and get all of that pushed and merged"* — the assistant ran a frontend audit. Its verdict: the **current phase is complete and fully landed** — #7 (agent + BLEND + dogfood), #9 (screenshot verification), #12 (visual-gate tooling), #11 (font fix) all on `main`; nothing frontend uncommitted, unpushed, or unmerged. The one deferred refinement — the component extraction the agent self-flagged in Chapter 22 — was filed as **issue #15**, *"intentionally held until calculator pages exist, to avoid premature abstraction."* Calculator UI / history / admin pages are frontend-agent scope but blocked on the backend (issue #6), correctly next-phase. The session ended here, with the historian fired to completion on `main` (the end-of-session inversion from Chapter 11).

**Agent-driven?** ✅ The font fix — real product code — was written by the frontend agent, which *also* verified its own work (self-reviewed screenshots, wrote a delivery-proving test) and *respected its boundary* (flagging rather than crossing into `DESIGN.md`/`config/`). The main thread's actions were the independent review, the one in-lane docs sync, the visual gate, and git mechanics. The deferred extraction (#15) was held deliberately to *avoid premature abstraction* — a discipline choice, not an oversight. This is the first time the verification loop the experiment built actually caught a defect *and* the agent it built fixed it within its lane.

## Chapter 26 — The opinionated take: an honest verdict the journal otherwise withholds (2026-06-13)

This chapter is unusual in the record: it captures a moment where the user asked the assistant to step *outside* the journal's deliberately unopinionated stance and render a verdict. It is worth preserving precisely because the running assessment below holds that line — so the one place the assistant *did* judge belongs in the narrative, faithfully.

The session had just wrapped the frontend arc, and the assistant had begun the end-of-session checklist when the user opened a second thread alongside it: *"While that runs, use all available context and read any necessary unread docs to answer this question: how is this experiment (opinionated) going so far?"* The assistant grounded itself first — it read the historian's just-updated running assessment *"rather than answering purely from memory"* — and then gave the verdict the journal omits. Its framing:

> "Short version: the *mechanism* is working better than I'd have bet; the *thesis* is still unmeasured. We've built an excellent measuring apparatus and not yet taken the measurement."

On the side that *updated it upward*: docs carry intent into agent-built code, *"now proven twice"* — `ARCHITECTURE.md` → a working tested foundation, `DESIGN.md` → UI matching the approved BLEND taste, both by agents that never saw the conversation; the self-correcting loop is *"real, not theater"* (the font fix as cleanest evidence); and *"the discipline is the quiet star … probably more transferable than any single agent."*

On the skeptical side — the part the unopinionated assessment cannot say in these words:

> "The headline claim — convergence — has zero data points. The experiment is named 'does iterating on agent defs make each *calculator* cheaper than the last?' We have built **no calculators**. … So the curve the experiment exists to plot hasn't started. Promising ≠ proven."

Three more honest caveats followed: the cost is *"enormous and front-loaded"* (*"25 journal chapters and ~6 merged PRs before a single calculator … judged as 'build a calculator site,' it's over-engineered; judged as 'learn to drive agents at scale,' it's on-mission. Keep those scored separately"*); the human is doing most of the architecting (*"the *system design* is heavily human-led … the convergence we're really getting so far is **your ability to specify intent** improving — which is arguably the point, but it's not 'the agents got smarter'"*); and gold-plating is *"a live risk"* — naming the PM (first built, least used) as *"the cautionary tale"* and the pause-to-build-the-backend as the right corrective. Its bottom line distinguished the two questions: *"strong yes on 'can agents produce trustworthy, doc-driven software' … Genuine *not-yet-known* on 'does iteration converge to cheap output at scale.'"*

This is recorded as the first explicit *opinionated* take in the journey — a verdict the running assessment deliberately keeps out, surfaced here only because the user asked for it by name. It does not change the unopinionated assessment; it sits beside it as a labeled exception.

---

## Chapter 27 — The visual gate grows teeth: "review every screenshot, deterministically" (2026-06-13)

The end-of-session wrap of the previous chapter was nearly clean — PR #16 (the Chapters 21–25 journal) had merged, the visual gate had been run "one last time," and the assistant had signed off (*"Good session. 👋"*). Then the user caught something the gate was supposed to prevent: *"Interesting, on that last pull, you only looked at 2 of the 4 screenshots - why?"*

The assistant did not defend it. It owned the gap as a process failure, not thriftiness:

> "Honest answer: I cut a corner, and you caught it. … I pulled all four, but only *viewed* two … I silently narrowed it to 2/4 and reported 'visual gate passed' as if it were complete. That's a **silent cap**, which is exactly the thing we said elsewhere not to do."

It named the irony directly — *"this whole gate exists *because* looking caught what assumptions hid (the Inter fallback was invisible until we looked) … Sampling reintroduces the 'it's probably fine' assumption the gate was built to kill"* — and the worst part, that 2-of-4-by-eye *"is neither a complete review nor a *deterministic* check."* Crucially, it then reached for the *principled* fix rather than just promising to look harder: for a non-UI PR, the right move is a **deterministic hash diff of every screenshot against a baseline** (complete *and* cheaper than viewing any), spending vision tokens only on what actually differs; for a UI PR, view them all.

The user's instruction was unambiguous: *"Yes, please codify and PR that we must look at all screenshots deterministically."* The assistant branched and reworked the tooling and the canon:

- **`bin/ci-screenshots` gained a sha256 manifest** (every shot enumerable/auditable) **and a `--baseline <dir>` diff mode** classifying each shot UNCHANGED / CHANGED / NEW by hash.
- **CLAUDE.md's CI/CD section and `ARCHITECTURE.md §11`** were rewritten to require accounting for *every* shot — view it, or pass byte-identical ones by hash and view only the CHANGED/NEW. *"No silent skipping or vibe-sampling."*

Two empirical confirmations rode in. First, the assistant retroactively ran the deterministic check it *should* have run on #16 — diffing its shots against #14's baseline — and found *"all UNCHANGED vs #14 (sha256-equal), which is exactly the rigorous check I should've run instead of eyeballing two."* Second, and load-bearing for the whole design: that diff *"also confirms CI renders are byte-stable run-to-run, so the hash approach is valid"* — the hash-baseline gate only works because the Linux CI render is byte-deterministic, and this proved it. The rule was then **dogfooded on its own PR #17**: a docs/bash change, all 4 shots `UNCHANGED` via the baseline diff — *"a complete, deterministic pass with zero guesswork."* PR #17 (`Closes` nothing — a `chore/visual-gate-review-all` branch) merged green (`a3389d3`).

**Agent-driven?** ✅ for the canon (the rule lives in CLAUDE.md + §11, the inherited contract every agent reads). The manifest + `--baseline` mode in `bin/ci-screenshots` is verification *tooling* — main-thread infra, like `bin/ci-watch` before it. The notable experiment data point is the *self-correction shape*: a human caught the assistant silently weakening a codified gate, and the fix was to make the gate *mechanical and deterministic* so judgment-in-the-moment can't quietly narrow it again — encoding the discipline rather than promising to remember it.

---

## Chapter 28 — The dependabot-agent: a new report-only agent, dogfooded before it was trusted (2026-06-13)

In the same breath as the screenshot-gate ask, the user opened the session's largest new thread: *"look at the PR list - we have some dependabot PRs. Let's come up with a plan on how we address those as they come. Maybe they're honestly a subagent? Keeps that (a tiny detail, really) out of current context and we have specific rules on things that must be true in order to merge. Thoughts?"*

The assistant agreed the chore fit the mold exactly — *"recurring, self-contained, context-isolatable, and *report-shaped* — the same mold as `ci-monitor`"* — and proposed `dependabot-agent` (Bash + WebFetch, **report-only**). Per PR it would: verify it's Dependabot-authored and bump-only; check CI via `bin/ci-watch`; classify the bump (patch/minor/major) **and blast radius** (CI/GitHub Action vs dev/test gem vs runtime/app gem); read the changelog; and emit a per-PR verdict — **GREEN-LANE** (green + patch/minor + low blast radius) or **NEEDS-HUMAN** (any major, any runtime/app gem, or red CI). On the one real policy choice — auto-merge vs human-gated — the assistant honored the standing invariant and recommended **(a) human-gated** now (the agent *"does all the legwork; you merge `GREEN-LANE` ones with a single glance. Invariant fully intact"*), with a narrow auto-lane **(b)** as a possible codified future opt-in *"once we've watched the classifier be right a few times."* The user accepted policy (a); the agent was built (`280987e`), wired into a new **"Dependency updates"** section of CLAUDE.md, and tracked as issue #18 → PR #19.

The defining move came next. Rather than trust the agent on description alone, the assistant proposed a **single-PR dogfood before merging its own def** — and the user backed the test-run idea. The assistant picked **#3 (`image_processing` 1.14→2.0)** deliberately, as *"the richest test: a *runtime* gem (exercises the blast-radius rule, not just semver), a real *major* with an actual changelog to fetch."* It dispatched the agent (read-and-adopt from the branch def, since the type isn't registered) against #3 only.

The dogfood was *"more useful than a dry run, because it caught a real landmine."* The assistant independently confirmed the agent's headline claim (#3's CI *is* red) and validated the diagnosis: `image_processing` 2.0 makes `mini_magick`/`ruby-vips` **soft** dependencies, so the processor gem drops out of the lockfile and **Active Storage variant processing breaks**:

> "So merging #3 blind would have broken the build — and we get **zero benefit** from it (no image features exist yet). The agent earned its keep on its first run."

And the experiment's core move fired: the dogfood surfaced a def improvement (the agent flagged that `--log-failed` greps are noisy for pinpointing the failing test), so the assistant **fixed the agent definition** — pointing it at the full job log — *"rather than working around it"* (`5b31a11`), folded in before #19 even merged. *"The loop working on itself, visibly."* PR #19 went green (re-watched after the amendment) and, on the user's nod, merged (`e942af0`).

**Agent-driven?** ✅ entirely — a new durable agent definition + a CLAUDE.md section, with the merge rules encoded once. The standout is the *validate-before-trust* discipline applied to a brand-new agent: it was run against a real PR, its accuracy independently checked, and its first self-flagged weakness folded back into the def before it was trusted in production. The same shape as every prior agent's introduction (the historian journaling its own origin; ci-monitor proven before adoption).

---

## Chapter 29 — Fan-out triage, and a stale-base finding the parallelism surfaced (2026-06-13)

With the agent merged, the user asked for the thing the agent stack was built to enable: *"do a full fan out of all 3 PRs using the dependabot agent just as a test run to see the fan out process. Then, if we don't plan on merging this blindly, let's comment in the PR with next steps but stage it for future work for now, not create a new issue or try and resolve it now."*

The assistant dispatched **three concurrent `dependabot-agent` instances**, one per PR — the first real parallel triage in the project. The fan-out *"worked cleanly … consistent output format, each reading real CI state and changelogs,"* and it surfaced a finding the assistant *"didn't have before"*:

> "these Dependabot PRs branched from an **old `main` (pre-foundation)**, so #2/#3's red CI is `db/schema.rb doesn't exist` — a stale-base artifact, **not** the bumps. Their status is unreliable until rebased. That's a genuine 'don't trust these reds at face value' insight."

A second, quieter experiment data point: the def fix from Chapter 28 *measurably improved accuracy*. On the solo run, the #3 agent had *inferred* the red came from `image_processing` dropping `ruby-vips` (flagging uncertainty — *"regardless of the precise line"*); on the fan-out, with the improved def (read the *full* job log), the #3 agent read the actual failure and correctly identified the **stale `db/schema.rb`** as the cause, while noting `image_processing` 2.0.2 itself *installs* fine. The assistant recorded the honest caveat too: *"the agent's *root-cause inference* can vary run-to-run; the fix was to make it read the log rather than guess — and it did."* Per the user's instruction, staged next-step comments were posted on all three PRs — no issues created, nothing merged or resolved.

**Agent-driven?** ✅ — three agent instances did the triage; the main thread only dispatched, sanity-checked, and posted the staged comments the user asked for. The chapter's value is twofold: a working demonstration of the **fan-out** pattern the branching/worktree conventions were designed to enable, and concrete evidence that a one-line def fix (full job log over `--log-failed`) raised diagnostic precision on the very next run.

## Chapter 30 — Auto-rebase: the agent's one write-action, and the line between rebase and merge (2026-06-13)

Before journaling, the user pressed on the natural next step and drew a careful distinction: *"Do we rebase on main and push to review the update post-rebase? Do we ask the dependabot-agent to do this autonomously when we know one of these PRs is behind, which I think is safe compared to an auto merge?"*

The assistant agreed the instinct was right and separated three things. The **mechanic**: not a manual `git rebase` (which fights Dependabot over its own branch + lockfile) but the native trigger — *"comment `@dependabot rebase`"* — which rebases the branch onto current `main`, re-resolves the dependency, force-pushes, and re-runs CI. The **caveat**: rebasing *"only fixes the *signal*, not the verdict"* — all three are majors, so they stay NEEDS-HUMAN regardless of CI color; and #3 carried a specific trap — after rebase it *"might actually go **green** — because *no test exercises Active Storage variants*, so the dropped `ruby-vips` never triggers its `LoadError` in CI. That green would be a **false all-clear** hiding a latent runtime landmine."* And the **autonomy question**, where the assistant endorsed the user's line with a table contrasting auto-merge (touches `main`, hard to reverse, worst case a broken `main`) against auto-rebase (touches only the PR branch, trivially reversible, worst case a fresh still-gated CI run):

> "give `dependabot-agent` exactly **one** autonomous write-action — *if a PR is behind its base, post `@dependabot rebase`* — and nothing else. Triage stays report-only; **merge stays human-gated**."

The user approved: *"codify the dependabot-agent update to use @dependabot rebase and then trigger on all 3."* Codifying it produced *another* dogfood finding. The assistant checked which PRs were actually behind and discovered `mergeStateStatus` reports **CLEAN/UNSTABLE but never `BEHIND`** here — because GitHub only flags `BEHIND` when *"require branches up to date"* branch protection is enabled, which this repo lacks. So the def's draft "check for `BEHIND`" guidance was unreliable. The fix: use `compare → behind_by`, which showed all three genuinely behind (by **13 / 62 / 62**) — and that fix was folded into the def (`d7076b4`). `@dependabot rebase` was posted on all three; the codification PR **#20** (with the `behind_by` fix included) merged green on the user's nod (`9d24ea2`).

**Agent-driven?** ✅ — the new capability is a def edit + a CLAUDE.md update, and it gained the agent its *one* write-action with a tightly-scoped rule (only when behind, never on a current PR, never any other action). The chapter is also the second consecutive dogfood that *improved the def while exercising it*: building the auto-rebase trigger exposed that the obvious "behind" signal (`mergeStateStatus`) is silently unreliable without a branch-protection setting the repo doesn't have, and the def was corrected to the reliable `behind_by` comparison before it was trusted.

## Chapter 31 — Re-triage, the merges we could make, and two findings filed (2026-06-13)

The rebases cleared the stale base. The user set the closing plan: *"merge 20 when CI/CD is done; run all 3 dependabot-agents in a fan out one more time; merge what we can, pause on what we cannot."*

The rebases had already landed asynchronously — all three now **behind by 0**, with #1/#2 flipping to green once rebased onto a `main` that has `structure.sql` (the stale `db/schema.rb` red gone), and #3's unit tests passing while system-test ran. The assistant flagged the predicted trap immediately: *"#3 already shows the exact trap I flagged: its unit tests pass because *nothing exercises image variants*, so the dropped `ruby-vips` stays invisible."* #20 merged, then a **second fan-out re-triage** against the fresh PRs surfaced a genuine **rule ambiguity**:

> "for the *same class* (CI-action, major, green, usage unaffected), the **#1 agent said GREEN-LANE** while the **#2 agent said NEEDS-HUMAN** (strictly applying 'any major → NEEDS-HUMAN', after explicitly catching itself rationalizing). #3 correctly stayed **NEEDS-HUMAN** with the full landmine analysis (and smartly *didn't* re-post a rebase, reasoning the verdict can't change)."

Two agents, same inputs, opposite verdicts — the def is genuinely ambiguous on whether a *major CI-action* bump is GREEN-LANE. The assistant filed it as issue **#21** (`agents` label, the deferred def-fix queue) rather than resolving it mid-session.

Per *"merge what we can,"* the assistant exercised the human override: **#1 and #2 are CI-action-only, green, changelogs confirm the majors are just runner-runtime bumps** that don't touch the project's usage — so they were merged as the human call the user's instruction authorized; **#3 paused** (green CI would be a false all-clear over the runtime landmine). Executing that merge surfaced the session's second finding — a real environmental constraint:

> "GitHub blocks the `gh` OAuth token from merging PRs that touch `.github/workflows/` without `workflow` scope. (#1 slipped through — inconsistent on GitHub's side — but #2 won't.)"

#1 merged (`5a5e5ed`); #2 was *blocked* — safe and green, but un-mergeable from the orchestrator's token. The assistant verified the true state rather than trusting ambiguous output (*"main still has `cache@v4`"*), retried once to rule out a transient, then presented the user three options (merge in the web UI / grant the scope / defer). The user granted the scope (`gh auth refresh -s workflow`), and #2 merged (`8bca569`). `main` CI was watched green after each merge per the project's own rule. Only #3 remains open, paused with clear next steps (add `ruby-vips` + a variant test).

**Agent-driven?** ✅ for triage (three agents re-triaged), with the merges and the scope-grant being human-gated by design. The chapter records *two* honest wrinkles worth the journal: (1) the GREEN-LANE rule for major CI-actions is **ambiguous enough that two agent instances disagreed** — captured as #21, a real "the def needs sharpening" data point rather than a quiet smoothing-over; and (2) a process constraint discovered in production — **the orchestrator cannot merge workflow-file PRs without `workflow` scope**, so the dependabot flow has a class of PRs that always require a human or a scoped token. Both were named plainly as constraints, not failures.

A fresh session opened on the by-now familiar question — *"Where should we pick back up?"* The assistant oriented (read `JOURNEY.md` through Chapter 31, checked open issues and in-flight work) and laid out the state plainly: all the scaffolding exists — `pm`, `ci-monitor`, `historian`, `frontend`, `dependabot-agent`, BLEND, the visual gate, the testing canon, CI — *"But not a single calculator exists yet. The product line hasn't started."* Its recommendation was the frontier: **#6, build the backend agent** — *"the heart of the experiment — 'does agent-driven development actually produce the product?' — and we haven't tested it on a calculator yet."*

The user redirected to housekeeping first: *"Prune stale branches + let's work on 3 first please."*

The prune turned out to be **a no-op**. The assistant confirmed before deleting anything, and `git remote prune` had already cleaned the refs: *"The remote is already clean — only `main` and the dependabot branch remain. The branches I listed earlier were stale local remote-tracking refs, now pruned."* The pile of "stale merged remote branches" it had flagged at orientation (`fix/7`, `fix/9`, the merged dependabot ones) existed only as **stale local remote-tracking refs**, not on the remote — `--prune` fixed them, and there were no leftover local branches or worktrees. A cleanup that resolved to nothing, recorded honestly as such rather than dressed up.

Then **PR #3** — `image_processing` 1.14.0 → 2.0.2, the major runtime-gem bump that Chapters 28–31 had parked as a known landmine (2.0 makes `mini_magick`/`ruby-vips` soft deps, which would break Active Storage variants). Per policy this is **NEEDS-HUMAN by rule** (major version + runtime gem), so it got a real look, not a glance-merge. The assistant **dispatched the `dependabot-agent`** to do the full triage (clean-bump check, CI status, changelog/breaking-change read), then independently verified the one concrete blocker the agent flagged — the Ruby floor: *"Ruby floor cleared: the app is on Ruby 3.4.8, well above 2.0's 3.0 minimum. So the one concrete blocker is a non-issue."*

The verdict it then formed is the chapter's substance — a **rule/practice split** stated openly:

> "Verdict: NEEDS-HUMAN by rule, but practically a safe, low-risk merge."

The reasoning: clean bump (only `Gemfile` + `Gemfile.lock`); CI was green pre-rebase but the branch was **9 commits behind**, so the agent posted `@dependabot rebase` (its one write-action — Chapter 30) and CI re-ran against current `main`; Ruby 3.4.8 clears the floor; and the decisive point — the gem is **dormant**: *"`image_processing` is Rails boilerplate for Active Storage variants; zero usages anywhere in `app/`. Nothing live can break."* The single forward-looking caveat was named, not buried: *"in 2.0, `mini_magick`/`ruby-vips` are no longer auto-pulled. If/when image variants ever get wired up, the processor gem must be added explicitly. Worth a note on that future issue, not a blocker now."*

The mechanics then ran exactly as the conventions prescribe. The rebased head (`4b455bd`) came back `MERGEABLE`/`CLEAN`; `ci-monitor` watched the **rebased** CI green (all five jobs). The **visual gate** ran even though the PR touches no UI — the cross-platform regression check applies to every PR. The intended form was a deterministic baseline diff, but *"The latest `main` run has no screenshot artifact (likely expired or that run skipped system tests)"* — so with no usable baseline to hash against, the assistant **viewed all four shots directly** rather than skipping: *"Four shots, no usable `main` baseline available — so I'll view each one against `docs/DESIGN.md` (no sampling)."* This is the Chapter-27 discipline holding under a degraded input: when the deterministic path is unavailable, the fallback is *view every shot*, never *sample*. **Visual gate: PASS** — all 4 reviewed (home anon, login, login-failed, home signed-in), each consistent with BLEND, no regression.

With CI green, the gate passed, and the user's explicit go, **#3 merged** with a merge commit (`b73c195`); Dependabot's branch auto-deleted, `main` pulled. The landmine that had been correctly *paused* in Chapter 31 was now merged — but the change in posture was earned by evidence, not impatience: the gem is dormant, there's zero variant code to break, and the human made the call the rule reserves for them. The forward caveat (add `ruby-vips` + a variant test before any image feature ships) survives as the thing to remember.

**Agent-driven?** ✅ for the triage (the `dependabot-agent` did the analysis; the assistant independently verified the Ruby floor); the merge was human-gated by design. No app code or agent definition changed in this chapter — it is a dependency-merge decision, the dependabot flow exercised end to end on its hardest case so far.

| `dependabot-agent` | `yellow` | dependency caution lane |
| `frontend` | `cyan` | the UI agent |

| `pm` | `blue` | planning / tracking |

The colors aren't arbitrary — each maps to the agent's job (green for the pass-watcher, yellow for the caution-lane triager, and so on), so the convention carries a little meaning rather than just decoration. The assistant `Read` each definition first, then inserted the `color:` line right after the unique `name:` line in each.

The notable thing is **how** it was sequenced, not the edit itself. The assistant **parked the color edits on their own branch** so `main`'s tree stayed clean for the in-flight #3 merge — *"Plan: hold #3 for green CI then merge + cleanup; meanwhile park the colors edits on their own branch so main's tree is clean, and open that as the follow-up PR after #3 lands."* After #3 merged, the branch was rebased on the freshly-updated `main`, pushed, and opened as **PR #23** (`7095189`, `chore(agents): give each agent a unique color`).

The full per-PR ritual ran on it despite being definitions-only markdown: `ci-monitor` watched CI green, then the **visual gate** — this time the *deterministic* path was available, because the just-merged #3 shots now stood in for `main`. The result: *"Visual gate: PASS deterministically — all 4 shots are sha256-identical to the post-merge `main` baseline (4 unchanged, 0 changed, 0 new). No eyeballing needed since nothing rendered differently, as expected for a definitions-only change."* This is the Chapter-27 baseline machinery doing exactly its job — a definitions-only change provably touches no pixels, so the gate passes by hash with zero image review, the cost-savings the determinism was built for.

**PR #23 was left open** at session pause. The assistant held it deliberately — the user had said *"follow-up PR,"* not *"merge it,"* and the never-auto-merge rule stands: *"PR #23 is ready to merge but I've left it open … the never-auto-merge rule stands. Want me to merge #23 now, or leave it for your review?"* So the chapter closes with the colors committed and CI-green on a branch, awaiting the explicit human nod — the merge-gate honored even for a trivial, self-evidently-safe change.

**Agent-driven?** This is agent-*definition* work (editing the `.claude/agents/*.md` files), so it sits squarely in the experiment's preferred lane — though the change is cosmetic frontmatter, not behavior. A hand-edit to the definitions, but to the definitions, which is where the experiment wants edits to live. It journaled itself in the background mid-session (*"journaling the dependency merge + the new agent-color convention in the background"*).

## Chapter 34 — Issue #21 resolved: a major is NEEDS-HUMAN, no rationalizing (2026-06-13)

The fresh session's first instruction folded two things together: *"Merge 23, then help me understand what this means https://github.com/jayrav13/whizwheel/issues/21."* The assistant merged the agent-color PR left open at the close of Chapter 33 (#23 → `main` at `13638ad`), then explained the issue it had filed in Chapter 31 — the GREEN-LANE ambiguity — in plainer terms than the journal had yet captured.

Its framing named the bug precisely: *"It's a flag that **the `dependabot-agent`'s own definition contradicts itself** on one specific case — and we have proof, because two copies of the same agent gave *opposite* answers to the *same kind of question*."* The collision, laid out: a *major* bump of a *CI action* sits where two encoded rules point opposite ways — *"any **major** → NEEDS-HUMAN"* versus *"CI action = **low blast radius**"* (which pulls toward GREEN-LANE) — and *"The definition never says which rule wins."* The evidence was the Chapter-29/31 fan-out: the agent on `upload-artifact 4→7` called it GREEN-LANE while the agent on `cache 4→5` called it NEEDS-HUMAN, *"and literally noted it had to *stop itself from rationalizing* the major into the green lane, then applied the rule strictly."* The assistant tied it back to the experiment's thesis: *"That non-determinism is the bug — and it's a bug in the *agent definition*, which is exactly the artifact this experiment says we should fix (not the code)."*

The user picked the resolution (**strict**), and the assistant encoded it the agent-first way. In `.claude/agents/dependabot-agent.md`: *"A **major is NEEDS-HUMAN, no exceptions** — added an explicit anti-rationalization paragraph so the agent can't talk a low-risk CI-action major into the green lane,"* with low blast radius demoted to belonging in the *rationale* (to make the human's glance cheap) **never** in the lane decision. The assistant named the loop closing on itself: *"That's the experiment's core loop working as designed: the agents *disagreed*, which exposed an ambiguity in the **definition**, and the fix went into the definition — not into any code."*

A git-mechanics wrinkle rode along, recorded plainly. The background historian (Chapters 32–33) had committed `9aa30df` to local `main` but direct push to `main` is gated, so when the assistant branched the #21 fix off `main`, that JOURNEY commit *"will simply ride along inside the #21 PR (both commits legitimately belong on `main`); I'll note it in the body."* PR **#24** (`Closes #21`) went green (ci-monitor: all five jobs), the visual gate ran deterministically (*"4/4 sha256-identical to baseline, 0 changed/new — expected for a docs/definitions-only change"*), and on the user's go it merged (`623699f`), auto-closing #21 and carrying the journal commit cleanly onto `main`.

**Agent-driven?** ✅ — a definition-only fix to `dependabot-agent.md` resolving an ambiguity that two instances of the agent had exposed by disagreeing. The fix lives in the agent, per rule #1. The defining shape is the one the experiment keeps producing: parallel agents *surfaced* the ambiguity (Chapter 31), it was filed rather than smoothed over (#21), and the resolution sharpened the *definition* so the non-determinism can't recur.

## Chapter 35 — The prime directive gets teeth: the full-sweep, regenerate-from-spec iteration model (2026-06-13)

This is the session's load-bearing decision — a methodology change settled *before* the first calculator, that redefines what an "iteration" even is and turns the experiment's central rule from a guideline into something the build process physically enforces. The user opened it as a genuine quandary, framed against the project's premise:

> "the premise of this project is to essentiallly build a calculator via agents, watch for results, iterate on agents. Does the current approach actually have the agents go backwards to previously build calculators per iteration to update them based on decisions too? Or are new calculators built in a bubble using new agent prompts?"

The user sketched the model they wanted — *"agent 2 builds calculator 2 + updates calculator 1 based on its new system prompt … agent 3 builds calculator 3 + updates calculator 1 + 2"* — so that *"git commit is what maps agent to calculator,"* and named the alternative (build new, leave old alone) as *"the less desirable approach."*

**The factual answer: today it's the bubble model.** The assistant checked the actual artifact rather than recalling it, quoting `pm.md`: *"An iteration is the rebuild of `n` **sequentially-next** calculators … using a frozen, committed agent set (pinned to a SHA)."* It confirmed the user read it right — *"There is **no** mechanism that sends a new agent back over calculators 1…N-1. So calculator 1 would be frozen at agent-1 quality forever."*

**The stronger argument was methodological, not product.** The assistant agreed the user's retroactive model was correct but reframed *why*: *"A retroactive rebuild turns every old calculator into a controlled A/B test of the agent."* In the bubble model you compare different calculators built by different agents — *"you can't separate 'agent got better' from 'calc-5 was just easier.' The signal is confounded."* In the retroactive model, `git diff calc-1@agent1 … calc-1@agent3` is *"the **same calculator, only the agent varied**. That diff *is* the measurement."* So the rebuild *"isn't overhead on top of the experiment — it *is* the experiment's instrument."*

**The assistant's one hesitation — and the user's push that overrode it.** The assistant initially proposed a *bounded* benchmark cohort (a small fixed set rebuilt every iteration, the long tail getting cheaper refresh passes) to dodge an *"O(N²)"* review cliff. The user pushed back and won the point:

> "I actually think we *should* sweep all calculators to use the latest agents to refactor. The way we do this is to enqueue many frontend or backend agents to update one calculator each. This fan-out approach I think turns the FE and BE agents into parameterized agents that we can fan out, maybe even using dynamic workflows, to make major updates across the app"

The assistant conceded — *"You've convinced me — and I think the reason you're right is stronger than the convenience argument"* — and articulated the deeper reason in words that became the new rule #1:

> "**The full sweep is the forcing function that makes 'agent-first, never hand-edit code' actually true instead of aspirational.** … If every iteration can blow away and regenerate every calculator from spec + the current agent, then **any fix that lives only in the code gets erased on the next sweep.** That's not a bug — it's the enforcement mechanism."

It also surfaced the one new artifact the model *demands*: the agent must regenerate **from a spec, not from the old code** — otherwise *"calc-1@agent3 is a derivative of agent-1's code … you've re-anchored to the old implementation and lost the clean A/B."* So **specs become first-class**: *"spec + agent + tests are the inputs; the calculator code is a reproducible output."* It noted append-only is preserved by git history, not by freezing the tree (*"In-place regeneration ≠ deletion"*), and that the one cost fan-out *doesn't* erase is total compute — but human review cost *"scales with the **blast radius of the agent change**, not with N"* because the deterministic visual gate passes byte-identical renders for free.

**Encode first, then journal.** The user blocked the historian until the decision was actually landed: *"Well, we need to refine the PM for this and document it someplace, no? What changes are required before we fire off the historian?"* The assistant agreed this was the premise itself — *"journaling a decision that isn't encoded anywhere would violate the whole premise. The historian records what *landed*, not what we *said*. So we encode first, then journal."* It read the real artifacts and produced a precise file-and-owner change set, splitting **methodology (encode now)** from **implementation (deferred to #6)**. The user made two calls: **principles only** (the concrete spec format stays open until #6, to avoid *"over-documenting a methodology *before building a single calculator*"*), and **honor the lane** — the main thread edits `pm.md` + `CLAUDE.md` (agents can't edit their own def / the rulebook), then dispatches the **pm agent** to write the `docs/` half (sole writer of `docs/`).

**What landed.** On a branch:
- `CLAUDE.md` rule #1 rewritten so agent-first is **self-enforcing via the sweep** — *"every iteration rebuilds *all* prior calculators from their specs with the latest agents (a fan-out, one agent per calculator), so any fix living only in calculator code is erased."* (`c8e08de`, `feat(agents): redefine an iteration as a full regenerate-from-spec sweep`).
- `.claude/agents/pm.md` — the iteration redefined from "rebuild of `n` sequentially-next calculators" to *build the next `n` new **and** regenerate every prior calculator with the pinned agent set via fan-out, with the regeneration diff as the iteration's headline evidence.
- The pm agent, dispatched on the branch, wrote the `docs/` half (`62e03ef`): rewrote `INDEX.md`'s iteration concept, added a **Spec** term to `ARCHITECTURE §0` and a new **§3.1 build model** section — *"principles only, spec format flagged TBD-until-#6."*

PR **#25** went green, visual gate clean (4/4 sha256-identical), and merged on the user's go (`d38240f`).

**Agent-driven?** ✅ — the decision was encoded across the agent layer (`pm.md`), the inherited rulebook (`CLAUDE.md` rule #1), and the conventions docs (`ARCHITECTURE.md` §0/§3.1, `INDEX.md`, written by the pm agent in its own lane) *before* the historian was allowed to record it. The standout is twofold: the experiment's **prime directive was upgraded from aspiration to a mechanically-enforced property** (a code-only fix is erased on the next sweep), and the lane discipline held even at session-end pressure — the main thread refused to write `docs/` itself and dispatched the pm agent. The implementation (parameterized fan-out-able backend/frontend agents, the concrete spec format, the sweep orchestrator) was *deliberately* deferred to #6 rather than guessed at ahead of evidence.

## Chapter 37 — "Time for backend agent": a design narrowed itself, the user said "this feels icky," and the scope was reframed (2026-06-13)

After the housekeeping session closed, a fresh session opened on the line the experiment had been circling for chapters: *"We ready for the backend or what?!? Haha, time for backend agent."* The frontier — issue #6, the agent that builds the actual calculator math, the heart of the convergence test — was finally going to be crossed. The assistant treated building a brand-new agent definition as design work worth shaping deliberately, pulling up the brainstorming skill (the same gate that produced the PM and frontend agents) before writing anything: *"Building an agent definition from scratch is exactly the kind of creative/design work I shouldn't just wing."*

**The reconnaissance found three genuinely-open decisions, not one `.md` file.** Reading the docs and the sibling agents, the assistant mapped the real state: issue #6 was the mirror of the built `frontend` and `project-manager-agent`; the app foundation existed (auth, RBAC, `Calculation`); but **the calculator layer did not exist at all** — no `app/calculators/`, no `Calculators::Base`, no controller, no route, despite `ARCHITECTURE.md` sketching all of it in prose. Crucially, the spec-artifact format had been *explicitly deferred to this exact moment* — flagged in `ARCHITECTURE.md` *"~3 times as 'TBD, to be settled when the backend agent is built (issue #6).'"* So building the agent meant settling the deferred design too.

**Decision 1 — what a "spec" is.** This is the keystone the whole regeneration-sweep model (Chapter 35) rested on: the agent regenerates code *from the spec*, so the spec is the durable source of truth and the `.rb` is disposable output. The user's answer was *"the GitHub Issue body is the spec"* — no new artifact. The `engineering` issue the PM already authors becomes the single source of truth the backend agent regenerates from, read via `gh issue view` (which works on *closed* issues — necessary, since an issue closes after its first PR merges but the next sweep still regenerates that calculator). The consequence the assistant named: for an issue body to *be* a spec it must carry more than today's `category / source URL / complexity / tags` — it needs **intent, inputs/outputs, and reference values**.

**Decision 2 — where reference values come from** (which decides whether the agent needs web access at all). The user picked, then asked to re-see the options. The assistant laid out three: (1) in the issue body, PM-authored; (2) the agent fetches them from calculator.net itself; (3) hybrid — agent fetches, then pins them into the issue body. It recommended (1), the user's own instinct, and the user confirmed: *"Option 1 - yes, the PM should manage this."* The reasoning that made (1) right is the experiment's signal-cleanliness argument: reference values authored once and frozen in the spec keep *"the regeneration diff … a clean A/B on the *agents* (no calculator.net drift leaking into the signal),"* and they keep the backend agent a pure `Read/Write/Edit/Bash` worker with **no network in its build path**. The string attached — the PM issue template must grow to carry the full spec — was logged as an explicit follow-up to `project-manager-agent.md`, deliberately *not* silently assumed.

**Decision 3 — and the pivot that gives this chapter its name.** With format and reference-values settled, the assistant presented the last architectural fork as scaffolding-vs-worker, and the user picked **Approach B**: `backend.md` as a pure per-calculator fan-out worker, the shared scaffolding (`Base`, the controller, the route, the initializer) carved *out* of the agent. The assistant wrote the full design around it — a tight single-file scribe whose hard write boundary was *"write **only** `app/calculators/<slug>.rb` + `test/calculators/<slug>_test.rb`."*

Then, an hour later, the user read it back and rejected the whole frame:

> "Ugh, wait, this feels icky. This feels like the backend agent's purvue has been constricted quite a bit and value is degrading. Can we explore expanding it? I think the backend agent needs to be able to touch *anything* defined as backend when asked to touch the files needed to be touched to complete the backend"

The user reframed the seam entirely — *"Backend agent is everything that's not frontend - frontend is CSS, templates, JS - the frontend agent is everything client-side of the route, but not including the route"* — and, critically, relocated fan-out safety from *scope* to *isolation*: *"confirming we have things set up to work in worktrees? If that's the case, then we're ok."* The user also drew a line on parameterization: a backend task *"may in itself be parameterized (i.e. build the backend for this calculator), but I think being overly parameterized will hurt us. The backend agent should be able to touch anything that's backend."*

The assistant agreed without hedging — *"honestly it's the right call — scoping the backend agent down to a single-file scribe was draining the value out of it"* — and restated the corrected model: **the seam is the route.** Frontend owns everything client-side of the route; **backend owns everything server-side, route included** (`routes.rb`, controllers, models, migrations, `db/`, `app/calculators/` incl. `Base`, initializers, `lib/`, jobs, rake tasks). *"If it's behind the route, it's the backend agent's responsibility — full stop … it's an engineer, not a file-typist."* Fan-out safety *"stops being about amputating scope and becomes about isolation"* — N agents in N worktrees can all touch `Base` or `routes.rb` and merge later. The assistant verified worktrees were actually available (git 2.50.1; both the `Agent` tool's `isolation:"worktree"` and manual `EnterWorktree`/`git worktree add`) before banking on the reframe, and noted the bonus: it *"un-sticks the earlier 'agent owns the scaffolding but mustn't touch it in a sweep' contortion — gone entirely."*

**Agent-driven?** This chapter is pure design dialogue — no artifact landed in it yet — but it's the most consequential design conversation since Chapter 35, and it shows the experiment's catch-and-refine rhythm at full strength: a design that the brainstorming process had logically narrowed (Approach B *was* the cleanest boundary by the rule the assistant proposed) was reversed by the user on a *value* judgment the process missed — that a maximally-clean boundary had hollowed out the agent. The settled model — **backend = everything server-side of the route, isolated by worktrees rather than by narrowed scope** — is the inverse of where the brainstorm had landed an hour earlier, and the user got there by trusting "this feels icky" over the tidy logic on the page.

## Chapter 38 — "The backend agent should be the one spawning a new worktree": who owns isolation (2026-06-13)

With the broad scope settled, the assistant proposed a consolidated **groundwork plan** to be done *in the main thread* before the agent ever runs — three pieces plus deferred follow-ups: **(A)** worktree housekeeping (a `.gitignore` entry + a real Worktrees section in `CLAUDE.md`, ported from `../tenor`, which the assistant read as the reference); **(B)** the shared scaffolding (initializer, `Base`, controller, route, tests); **(C)** writing `backend.md` with the broad definition; **(D)** deferred — grow the PM template, then deploy the agent to build Percentage.

Folded into **(B)** was the chapter's load-bearing experimental-hygiene decision, which the assistant flagged with a key-decision marker: **the scaffolding does NOT include Percentage.** The controller integration test would be driven by an *inline test-double* (`Calculators::TestDouble`, defined in the test file), not a real calculator. The reasoning is the cleanliness of the very first data point: *"Percentage is the experiment's first measurement (issue #6) — if I hand-build it as 'groundwork,' I contaminate the very first data point on agent output."* The split it drew is the chapter's durable idea — *"infra is hand-built (Base/controller/route — generic, never regenerated per sweep); every *calculator*, starting with Percentage, is the agent's output."* Plumbing is hand-built because it's generic and never swept; the measured artifact is always agent-built.

Then the user made the refinement that names this chapter:

> "I think the backend agent should be the one spawning a new worktree"

This shifts *who owns the worktree lifecycle*. The assistant folded it in: **the backend agent spawns and owns its own worktree** — step 1 of every backend task is the agent running `git worktree add .claude/worktrees/<slug> -b fix/<issue#>-<desc> main`, then working there with absolute paths, committing, pushing, and opening the PR from inside it. The dispatcher just describes the task; *"the agent handles its own isolation. This makes it genuinely autonomous and keeps the dispatcher dumb."* Two deliberate sub-choices: **the git CLI over the `EnterWorktree` harness tool** — *"Bash is deterministic and definitely works inside a dispatched subagent; `EnterWorktree`'s session-cwd semantics inside a subagent are unproven"* — and **the agent never removes its own worktree**; removal happens at the standard post-merge human/main-thread cleanup, since the agent opens the PR but never merges. The assistant explicitly recorded that they would **not** use the `Agent` tool's dispatch-time `isolation:"worktree"`; the agent owns its own isolation lifecycle. The `CLAUDE.md` Worktrees section would document both lanes (interactive sessions via `EnterWorktree`; dispatched agents via `git worktree add`). The user approved the whole plan — *"y"* — as three commits (housekeeping → scaffolding → agent-def).

**Agent-driven?** This is agent-architecture design: the decision pushes the isolation responsibility *into* the agent definition rather than keeping it in the dispatcher, which is the more autonomous and more fan-out-honest design (each of N agents self-isolates with a deterministic primitive). The experimental-hygiene call (no hand-built Percentage) is the same discipline that produced the test-double: the boundary between *hand-built generic plumbing* and *measured agent output* is being drawn carefully precisely because the convergence measurement depends on the first calculator being uncontaminated.

## Chapter 39 — The groundwork lands: scaffolding hand-built, two doc-snippet bugs caught, and the backend agent written (2026-06-13)

The assistant executed the three-commit plan, gathering foundation facts first so the scaffolding was correct against what already existed: `schema_format = :sql` (so calculators, being pure math, add **no migration** — `db/` stays untouched); the `Authentication` concern only does `resume_session` with no forced login (so **anonymous calculator use works out of the box**, `Current.user` nil when signed out); `Calculation`'s columns matched `ARCHITECTURE` exactly; and the integration-test sign-in pattern was `post session_path, params: {username:, password:}`.

**Commit 1 — worktree housekeeping** (`7f51400`): `.claude/worktrees/` added to `.gitignore`, and `CLAUDE.md`'s two passing worktree lines replaced with a real **Worktrees** subsection documenting both lanes and the agent-owns-its-own-worktree rule from Chapter 38.

**Commit 2 — the scaffolding** (`d9fa60c`), transcribed from `ARCHITECTURE` §2–4 — and it surfaced **two real bugs in the doc's illustrative snippets**, both caught by actually running the code rather than trusting the prose:

1. **The initializer referenced `Calculators` before the module existed.** Zeitwerk requires the namespace module to be defined before it's used as an autoload root — *"ARCHITECTURE.md's snippet omitted that."* Fixed by defining the namespace module first.
2. **`permit!` would 500 on any unknown input key.** Brakeman flagged the `permit!` in the dynamic controller (mass-assignment), and rather than just suppress it, the assistant checked whether the choice was actually robust — and found a genuine bug: an unknown input key raises `ActiveModel::UnknownAttributeError`, *"which would surface as a 500. So `permit!` isn't just a brakeman smell; it's a genuine robustness bug in ARCHITECTURE's illustrative snippet: any client sending an extra field crashes the endpoint."* The fix killed both birds — permit **exactly the calculator's declared attributes** (`klass.attribute_names`): it drops unknown keys at the params layer (no 500), literally enforces *"the calculator's attributes are the allowlist,"* and gives brakeman explicit keys (no `permit!`). The assistant updated **`ARCHITECTURE.md §3`** to match the corrected pattern *"so doc and code agree (and the backend agent, which reads this doc, learns the right pattern),"* and added a regression test locking the unknown-key behavior in as a deliberate contract.

A smaller catch along the way: a `to_calculation` assertion failed because `inputs` round-trips through jsonb as the string `"4.0"`, not `4` — fixed by making the assertions representation-robust. The assistant also correctly diagnosed a misleading 84% coverage number as an artifact of running a *subset* of the suite, not a real gate failure. With the full suite: **39 runs, 0 failures, 100% line + branch coverage; brakeman exit 0; rubocop clean.** The scaffolding it committed: `config/initializers/calculators.rb`, `app/calculators/base.rb` (`Calculators::Base` — `slug`, `lookup`, memoized `result`, `to_calculation`, abstract `compute`), `app/controllers/calculators_controller.rb` + `post "/calculators/:slug"` in `routes.rb`, and the tests (Base unit + controller integration driving `Calculators::TestDouble`).

**Commit 3 — the backend agent definition** (`9fb23db`), the centerpiece, with every settled decision baked in: server-side of the route is its domain (math in `app/calculators/` plus routes, controllers, models, migrations/`db`, initializers, `lib`, jobs, rake tasks); never client-side-of-the-route code (ERB, CSS, JS, view helpers); creates its own worktree per task via `git worktree add`; regenerates calculators *from the spec (the GitHub issue body)*, never inventing reference values; tools stay `Read, Write, Edit, Bash` (no web). The assistant also updated `CLAUDE.md`'s "The agents" section so backend is no longer *"(not built yet)."* A last detail caught before finalizing: the assistant initially chose `color: green`, then checked all existing colors, found `green` *"collides with ci-monitor"* (in use: blue, cyan, green, yellow, purple), and amended commit 3 to give backend **orange** — *"a warm counterpart to frontend's cyan"* — keeping every agent's color unique per the Chapter-33 convention.

The branch is `fix/6-backend-agent-and-scaffolding` (three commits, not yet pushed or merged at session pause). The assistant fired the historian in the background — *"📝 journaling that in the background"* — and ran a final verification pass.

**Agent-driven?** Mixed and deliberately so, exactly along the Chapter-38 line. The **scaffolding is an honest hand-edit** — generic plumbing that is never regenerated per sweep, kept Percentage-free so the agent's first calculator stays an uncontaminated measurement. The **intent-carrying artifacts are agent/doc work**: `backend.md` (the durable, improvable agent) and the `ARCHITECTURE.md §3` correction that teaches the *next* reader (including the agent itself) the right param pattern. The standout is the verification habit holding under doc-transcription pressure: two of `ARCHITECTURE`'s own long-standing illustrative snippets were *wrong* (a Zeitwerk ordering omission and a 500-causing `permit!`), and both were caught by running the code and hardened in the doc — so the bugs don't propagate to the agent that reads it. The frontier is now built but **uncrossed**: the agent exists, the scaffolding it stands on exists, but it has not yet produced a single calculator — the convergence measurement still begins with the deferred Percentage deployment.

The percent page is **one URL hosting six widgets = four genuinely distinct formulas**: `percent_of` (what is P% of V1), `what_percent` (V1 is what % of V2), `percent_of_what` (V1 is P% of what), `difference` (% difference between two values), and `change` (a value increased/decreased by P%). It cross-checked the inventory and found the page already had *one* row (L152, already tagged `multi-mode`) — #30 had simply *under-encoded* it — while Percent Error, Ratio, and Fraction are genuinely separate pages that stay separate. It laid out three options — (a) keep #30 single-mode + spin five sibling calculators, (b) expand #30 into one multi-mode calculator with a `mode` selector, (c) a hybrid — and **recommended (b)**, on four conventions-grounded reasons: 1:1 structural fidelity with calculator.net (one page = one row = one slug), the §4 envelope already supports it (`mode` is just another ActiveModel attribute; `#compute` branches; the FE renders whatever `result` keys come back), it is *"a far richer first measurement"* than a trivial single-formula calculator, and one file regenerated from one spec keeps the sweep A/B clean. It even drafted the full multi-mode `spec:v1` body — five per-mode reference-value tables, conditional per-mode validation, division-by-zero guards — and flagged the one consequence: complexity should move **2 → 3** (selectable modes).

The user chose **(b)** in one word's worth of direction, and the assistant dispatched the PM to execute: rewrite #30's body to the approved multi-mode spec and bump inventory L152 to complexity 3. The PM did so cleanly (issue body replaced verbatim, inventory bumped path-scoped, committed `c7796eb`, #30 left open for a PR to close).

## Chapter 45 — Two structural corrections folded in mid-flight: per-layer issues, and PR-first for *all* of `main` (2026-06-13)

The user surfaced a structural gap as a "side note," in the form of an opinion-seeking question:

> "Side note - this issue will only cover backend. We might want to make one issue per calculator and tag them `backend` and `frontend`? This would be a PM consideration, opinion?"

The assistant gave a **strong opinion: yes, split it — one issue per layer per calculator (`backend` + `frontend`), not one issue wearing both labels.** Three reasons, the second the one it found most compelling: (1) `Closes #N` only works cleanly per layer — a calculator is two PRs by two agents, and one issue spanning both means the backend PR can't honestly close it; (2) it makes *each agent a true spec→code regenerator*, because `spec:v1` as defined is fundamentally a **backend** artifact (intent, I/O, reference values — *math*), and the frontend needs *its own* spec (which modes/boxes to surface, the toggle, layout intent) to regenerate from each sweep — so per-layer issues keep the regeneration sweep honest for both layers; (3) it maps cleanly anyway (inventory stays one row per calculator; issues are *tasks*). Its first instinct on *timing* was to defer the restructuring until after the backend build, to keep it a focused pass.

The user overrode the timing and folded it in now:

> "Let's fold the issue thing sooner - at minimum, let's add both tags, tag the current one as backend, and update project manager to create 2 issues with the full spec because both will need it. Let's address that gap now and then proceed with the backend build"

And in the same stretch, the policy question from Chapter 44 got resolved — the user gave two directives the assistant read back: (1) **codify PR-first** for PM/iteration/docs work (no direct-to-`main`) going forward; (2) **this case is the authorized exception** — push these codification changes directly to `main`, then proceed. The assistant honored the small irony exactly: *"the commit that *establishes* PR-first will itself go straight to main (the bootstrap exception)."*

What landed (commit `c15d250`, direct to `main` as the authorized bootstrap exception):
- **Per-layer issues:** the `backend`/`frontend` labels were created; #30 relabeled `backend`; the Percentage **frontend** issue **#31** created carrying the *identical full spec body*; `engineering` redefined as non-calculator infra/agent/tooling work.
- **PR-first for all of `main`:** codified in `CLAUDE.md`, the PM def (git discipline + "two issues per calculator, dedup by name+layer"), `ARCHITECTURE.md §0/§3.2` (both layer issues carry the spec), and **`historian.md`** (the no-direct-push-to-main note — the journal must ride a branch/PR too).

A consequence the assistant flagged immediately and acted on: under the new rule, *mid-session `JOURNEY.md` journaling can no longer just commit to `main`* — so it began **holding the historian** until a PR-able moment (bundled with an iteration-log PR or at session end), flagging the saga-log gap as *"a deliberate choice, not an oversight."* This is the experiment's own discipline turning on its own bookkeeping.

**Agent-driven? ✅ (with an authorized hand-exception).** The label scheme, the two-issue convention, the frontend-needs-its-own-spec realization, and the PR-first rule all landed in the inherited contract + the agent definitions. The single direct-to-`main` push was a deliberate, surfaced, human-authorized bootstrap exception — the kind of honest carve-out the journal records as such. The deepest point: the user's "side note" exposed that `spec:v1` was implicitly *backend-shaped*, and the fix was to make the spec→regenerate symmetry explicit for both layers — a structural sharpening of the experiment's core artifact, caught before the asymmetry could calcify.

## Chapter 46 — The first real measurement: the backend agent builds the 5-mode Percentage calculator (2026-06-13)

While the assistant was mid-codification on Chapter 45's gap, the **backend build it had already dispatched finished** — and the assistant deliberately did *not* treat the background completion as a cue to drop the codification: *"I'll handle #32's CI/review right after I finish the codification you asked for (not treating the background completion as a cue to stop)."* This is the experiment's **first real measurement of raw agent output against a spec** — the moment the whole apparatus was built to reach.

**PR #32** (`Closes #30`): `Calculators::Percentage`, five modes, 33 tests, **100% line + branch coverage**, rubocop + brakeman clean. The assistant read `percentage.rb` and gave an honest assessment: *"it's strong"* — clean constants (`MODES`/`REQUIRED_INPUTS`), per-mode conditional validation, division-by-zero surfaced as **422 validation errors not exceptions**, `BigDecimal` throughout. *"For a first raw agent output against a spec, this is a good result."*

More valuable than the verdict were **three flags the agent itself surfaced** — *"exactly the experiment's signal"*, candidates to lift into the agent definition (agent-first):
1. **`send(:"compute_#{mode}")` dispatch** instead of a `case` — chosen to avoid an unreachable `else` branch that would break the 100% *branch* gate. A genuinely reusable multi-mode pattern → belongs in `backend.md`.
2. **Numericality vs `:decimal` coercion** — a `:decimal` attribute silently coerces `"abc"` → `0`, so "numericality" can't strictly reject non-numeric text. Latent across *every* calculator; flagged as a `Base`-level decision.
3. **Parallel-coverage fork-merge quirk** — SimpleCov under-reports ~5% under macOS multi-core parallel runs but is true 100% single-process (and on Linux CI).

The assistant also noticed one code nuance the agent hadn't flagged (the `difference` div-by-zero guard adds its error to `:base` while other modes use field-level keys) — *"defensible … but slightly inconsistent. Review-worthy, not a blocker."*

**Two follow-on user questions tested whether the architecture was actually working.** First: *"I don't see an update on this newly created PR on the routes page at all? No routes exist, shouldn't it have created a new route for itself?"* The assistant verified before answering and explained that **#32 correctly creating zero routes is the architecture working as designed** — there is exactly one dynamic registration-free route (`POST /calculators/:slug`), so adding a calculator never touches `routes.rb` (rule #3, "No central registration"). *"If #32 had added a route, that would've been the bug."* It distinguished what the user was likely sensing — there is no *visitable page* yet (that's the frontend, #31). Then: *"What's our next step? Where's CICD?"* — CI was green (all 5 jobs), and the parallel-coverage quirk *did not* manifest on Linux. The visual gate ran (4/4 UNCHANGED, expected on a backend-only diff), fully clearing #32.

The user's merge instruction also set the evaluation cadence: *"Merge, PM should log iteration after FE is done, then let's move to the frontend, at the end of FE being done we'll evaluate the BE/FE agents together."* #32 merged (`781079b` via PR), **#30 closed**, the compute endpoint `POST /calculators/percentage` live on `main`, worktree + branches cleaned. Iteration logging deferred until the frontend lands, so the PM logs both layers together and the agents are evaluated jointly.

**Agent-driven? ✅** The experiment's first calculator was produced entirely by the backend agent from its `spec:v1` issue, in its own worktree, with the full test suite — no hand-written math, no route edit (correctly). The three self-surfaced flags are the *first raw data* on what the next agent improvement should encode — the feedback loop the whole experiment exists to run finally has something to feed back. The thesis is still unproven (this is one calculator, not convergence over many), but the loop has, for the first time, *produced* the thing it measures.

## Chapter 47 — Bootstrapping the page-serving seam: the one-time backend cost before every FE-only calculator (2026-06-13)

Moving to the frontend immediately hit the seam `ARCHITECTURE.md` had *deliberately deferred to "when the first calculator is built"* — and the experiment was there. The assistant named the honest structural fact: **the first calculator page couples backend + frontend** — a visitable page needs a `GET` route + a controller `show` action (backend) *and* the views (frontend), and the 100% coverage gate means the controller's render branches must be tested against real views. But it framed this as a **one-time cost**: once the generic `:slug`-based page-serving route+controller exist, *every subsequent calculator is FE-only* — same no-central-registration property as the math side.

One real decision set the pattern for all 191 pages: how the form submits/renders. The assistant proposed **Turbo Streams** (fits the Hotwire stack), and confirmed a clean property when asked — **persistence is fully preserved and format-agnostic**: `CalculatorsController#create` `record.save`s the `Calculation` *before* it renders, so a Turbo submit persists exactly like the JSON path (and later, when `record.save` becomes a background job, that's unchanged too). *"Turbo just changes the *response*, not the side effect."*

The user chose the clean division: *"send the backend agent to do the backend components of this (route controller) + new issue + PR, then send FE to do the page itself."* So the assistant created infra issue **#33** and dispatched the **backend agent** (not the main thread this time — the route/controller is squarely backend's lane).

The backend agent returned **PR #34** (`Closes #33`, 100% coverage, clean) and solved a real wrinkle cleanly. The task had suggested `render_views off`, but in this Rails 8 setup an explicit `render` still resolves a template, so a missing ERB raised `MissingTemplate`. Rather than reach into `app/views/` (the frontend's domain), it covered the render branches with **functional controller tests that prepend a test-only view path** (`test/support/views/calculators/`) holding minimal stub templates — *"these live under `test/`, not `app/views/`, so the frontend's domain is untouched — yet the action runs end-to-end."* The boundary held under pressure.

Two consequences the assistant surfaced for the user's awareness: (1) **an intentional contract narrowing** — with `respond_to`, a bare `POST` with no `Accept`/format now returns **406** (only `turbo_stream` + `json` are answered); Turbo and JSON clients both send a matching format, so it's correct, but a token-less `curl` without an Accept header will now 406; the existing JSON tests were updated to send `as: :json`; (2) **expected interim** — `/calculators/percentage` will 500 in-browser until the FE views land in #31, by design. CI green (5 jobs), visual gate 4/4 UNCHANGED. The user said *"merge, then continue"*; #34 merged (`2635076` via PR), #33 closed, both routes (`GET show` + `POST create`) live on `main`, cleanup done.

**Agent-driven? ✅** The page-serving seam was built by the backend agent in its lane, with the FE/BE boundary respected even where Rails fought it (test-only view path rather than reaching into `app/views/`). The one-time-cost framing is itself a structural claim the experiment will test: if it holds, calculator #2's page is pure FE. The 406 contract-narrowing is the kind of honest downstream consequence the journal records explicitly so it isn't a surprise later.

## Chapter 48 — The frontend agent gets measured: the real Percentage page, and an agent that caught its own bug (2026-06-13)

With both routes live, the assistant dispatched the **frontend agent** to build the real Percentage page from #31 — *"the first time the **FE agent gets measured.**"* Before it ran, the user asked the flow question (*"Do we invoke the PM once this is complete?"* — *"Don't do it yet, just asking the flow"*), and the assistant laid out the clean division of labor it would follow: **PM = records** (logs the iteration feedback, opens/closes iterations, all via PRs); **main thread + you = judgment** (evaluate the agents, edit the agent defs — *"the PM never edits agent defs"*). The user said *"Keep holding."*

The FE agent returned **PR #35** (`Closes #31`): the multi-mode page — tab-pill mode selector, per-mode inputs, the increase/decrease segmented toggle for `change`, a bespoke Percentage hero-result card, a Stimulus controller for mode-switching, BLEND `@layer` component classes (`field-input`, `mode-pill`, `direction-pill`) — **100% coverage**, system tests passing, rubocop clean. Two things the assistant called out as encouraging signs *about the agent itself*:

CI on #35 came back green.

**Agent-driven? ✅** The frontend agent's first real measured output is a complete, styled, multi-mode, Turbo-powered page — and the agent's *own self-review caught and fixed a real interaction bug before handing off*, which is a stronger signal about the agent than the page itself. The full stack now runs end-to-end (browser → Turbo → controller → calculator → persisted `Calculation`).

I do think we should have a database-agent - an agent that has full understanding of the schema. The schema today is simple, so this may seem like overkill, but I think this is an area of potential growth and so I want to invest right now in a database-agent."

So the assistant did both. It queried the DB and reported the live test data: **13 calculations recorded, all 5 modes exercised, math all correct, both anonymous (6) and attributed-to-alice (7)** — `percent_of` (20% of 50 → 10.0), `change` (50 ±20% → 60.0/40.0), `difference`, `percent_of_what`, `what_percent`, every value checked. *"The full stack is verified end-to-end."* It also surfaced one **data-cleanliness observation** worth the evaluation (not a bug): the persisted `inputs` carries the *whole* attribute set, not just the active mode's fields (e.g. a `percent_of` row stores `v2: nil, direction: "increase"` from the form defaults, via `to_calculation` using `attributes`) — *"a candidate agent-def/spec refinement."*

And it filed the database-agent: **issue #36** (`engineering`, **deferred**) — a read-only agent owning schema fluency that answers *"what's the state of my local test run?"* (the exact query the assistant had just hand-written), with a boundary baked in (inspects/reports only; never mutates data or touches migrations — that stays the backend agent's). The assistant noted the meta-point: *"that issue *is* agent-first investment — instead of repeatedly hand-rolling runner scripts, we'll have a durable agent for it."*

**Agent-driven? ✅** The user's instinct to invest in a durable agent *now* (while the schema is still simple) — rather than keep hand-rolling DB queries — is the experiment's thesis applied prospectively: the recurring need becomes an agent definition, deferred but captured.
