## Chapter 50 — The centerpiece: "the Mode pills are bunched too tightly," and learning to fix the agent, not the page (2026-06-13)

This is the chapter the assistant flagged in-session as *"a centerpiece moment for the saga"* and the user called *"a phenomenal example of the direction we're going in."* It is the experiment's first full, honest run of its own core loop — and the first time that loop *visibly failed and had to be re-aimed*.

**The flag.** Clearing #35 through the visual gate, the assistant viewed all 6 changed/new shots and gave a genuine PASS-with-notes verdict (BLEND nailed: cream bg, green-as-accent, coral-as-action, tabular hero numbers, every state correct). The user added one note: *"my only note is that the Mode pills are bnched too tightly togheter."* The assistant confirmed it by eye (*"Both the horizontal *and* row gaps are cramped"*) and named the agent-first call: this is *"the first real FE feedback,"* and the fix *"shouldn't be a one-off CSS tweak (the regeneration sweep would erase it)"* — the pill gap is really a **`DESIGN.md` gap** (no pill/tab-group spacing guidance existed), so *"the durable fix is: add that spacing guidance to `DESIGN.md`, then have the FE agent re-apply it."* The user chose **"fix then merge."**

**The principle, named by the user.** While the FE agent worked, the user articulated the thesis cleanly:

> "I think this is a phenomenal example of the direction we're going in with this journey. We're updating the agent's reference docs to give it direction, not just jumping into code"

The assistant sharpened *where the diagnosis landed*: *"We didn't say 'the FE agent rendered cramped pills' (a bug in the output); we said '**DESIGN.md was silent on pill-group spacing**' (a gap in the agent's reference material). That reframe is the whole game."* It named three things that make it more than an anecdote: the regeneration sweep makes the doc-update *mandatory* (a hand-tweak would be erased), it *compounds* (one `gap` line applies to all 191 future pages), and it's *falsifiable* — *"the testable claim is: with the guidance in DESIGN.md, the next regeneration sweep should produce roomier pills with no re-prompting."* And it named the real skill honestly: not "update a doc" but *"routing the fix to the right layer — DESIGN.md (look) vs. `frontend.md` (how the agent works) vs. the spec (what to build) — and resisting the pull to over-specify."*

**The first attempt was too timid.** The assistant's `DESIGN.md` edit set a `gap-2.5` *minimum*; the FE agent dutifully applied exactly that (`gap-2` → `gap-2.5`, i.e. 8px → 10px) and its self-review reported the pills now *"breathe."* The error-label fix (raw `v1` → "Value (V1) can't be blank", via a new `calculator_error_messages` helper) came out clean. But on re-review the assistant was honest: the pill change was *"only +2px … I wouldn't call them *roomy*."*

**The user pushed, and exposed the deeper failure.** Asked to A/B the before/after CI screenshots, the user was blunt: *"I don't see a meaningful difference … I don't think these two runs actually changed much after editing the fe agent?"* The assistant gave a straight answer: the change *did* take effect, but *"`gap-2` → `gap-2.5` is +2px, which is essentially imperceptible. My DESIGN.md guidance set the *minimum* at basically the original value, the agent applied that minimum … That's on the guidance being too timid, not a broken pipeline."* It then started to **dial the gap in live** to find the right number — and the user stopped it cold:

> "No! Don't dial it live, revert. Let's really hone in on the agent"

The assistant reverted (*"dialing it live is me doing the agent's job"*) and produced the chapter's sharpest diagnosis — the number was a *symptom*; the real failure was that **the FE agent's screenshot self-review looked at cramped pills and reported "pills breathe"** — its visual *bar* for spacing was miscalibrated. *"If we keep handing it pixel values, we're permanently doing its job — the agent will never develop the eye."* It identified **two durable agent-level levers**: (1) `DESIGN.md` has no real spacing *system* (just the word "generous" + the timid floor it had just added — *"my recent 'gap-2.5 min' made it worse by handing the agent a *floor*, which it dutifully sat on"*); (2) `frontend.md`'s self-review *"has no teeth"* (it let the agent rubber-stamp; it needs an anti-pattern checklist + a bias to err toward more space).

**The methodology pivot — use the iteration loop, not a hotfix.** The user named the larger move:

> "let's actually do this - just the spacing system. Let's do this more systematically. Let's work with the agent to solidify what we have now and then actually use iterations to iterate towards a good idea"

The assistant reflected the shift back precisely — it had been *"about to reactively patch DESIGN.md and re-dispatch the FE agent"*; the disciplined version is to **solidify the current build as the baseline, make the agent-level change, then regenerate Percentage from spec under iteration 0002 and measure whether the improved guidance *alone* produces better spacing — no hand-holding.** This **reverses the earlier "fix-then-merge"** into **"merge the imperfect baseline now, improve via the loop"** — the assistant surfaced the reversal explicitly and confirmed it twice with the user before merging an intentionally-imperfect page (*"is that the intent, or did you still want the spacing sorted before #35 lands?"*). The user confirmed: *"merge the PR as is and close out the iteration … and then start working on the design.md changes to carry forward for pills for iteration 2."*

**What landed.** **#35 merged as-is** (`1408d0a` via PR) — Percentage page on `main` as the **iteration-0001 baseline**, #31 closed, cleanup done. The PM was dispatched to **log + close iteration 0001** in its own docs PR (**#37**), capturing the build outcome *and* the key data — the cramped pills **and the self-review declaring them "roomy."** Then the assistant drafted the **`DESIGN.md` spacing scale** for the user's review: a **relationship-based rhythm** (closer = more related), six named tiers (Affix `gap-2` / Grouped `gap-3` / **Grouped-wrapping `gap-x-3 gap-y-4`** / Stacked `space-y-5` / Section `mt-7–8` / Layout `gap-8`), framed as **targets, not minimums** (*"when in doubt, go one step more open"* — directly killing the failure where the agent sat on a floor), with two rules: wrapping groups get a larger row-gap than column-gap, and tappable chips/pills *"never keypad-dense."* The user approved (*"This all looks good"*); it landed as **PR #38** (`08b83ef`), the iteration-0002 agent change.

**Agent-driven? ✅ (and the cleanest example yet).** Every correction was routed *up* into the durable layer: the spacing fix into `DESIGN.md` (not the page), the self-review weakness named as a future `frontend.md` change. The user twice stopped the assistant from doing the agent's job — once forbidding live pixel-dialing, once redirecting a hotfix into the iteration loop. The deepest data point: the experiment's *own loop failed visibly here* (a too-timid doc edit + an agent self-review that rubber-stamped cramped output), and the response was not to abandon the loop but to **re-aim it** — make the doc a *system* not a floor, and set up a clean measurement of whether that alone is enough.

## Chapter 51 — Iteration 0002 opens as a single-variable bisect: the first regeneration sweep is dispatched (2026-06-13)

With both docs PRs cleared (#37 closed iteration 0001; #38 landed the spacing scale; both CI-green and 8/8 visually unchanged — the Percentage shots now correctly part of `main`'s baseline), the user said *"go for all."* Both merged, `main` at **1e18ce2** (spacing scale live), iteration 0001 closed.

Then the experiment ran its **first real regeneration sweep**. The assistant kicked **two agents in parallel** (PM touches `docs/logs`, FE touches `app/views` — no collision): the **PM** opening **iteration 0002** (pinned `1e18ce2`, tag `iteration-0002`, INDEX row, README noting the agent delta is the DESIGN.md spacing scale and that this is a *frontend-only* regen — the backend math is unchanged so it carries no signal; **PR #39**, docs-only), and the **FE agent regenerating the Percentage page from spec** — *explicitly not reading the old page*, rebuilding fresh from issue #31's spec + the new `DESIGN.md`.

The assistant framed the measurement precisely, and the user gave it the word that named it:

> "This is an awesome case study and your framing here is perfect, we'll bisect which part of the agent to work on next"

The assistant agreed *"bisect"* was exactly right *"because we changed precisely one variable: the DESIGN.md scale, holding `frontend.md`'s self-review constant."* So the regen outcome isolates cause: **pills come out roomy → DESIGN.md was the missing piece (the self-review bar was adequate, it just had nothing concrete to measure against — lever found); pills still cramped → the binding constraint is the agent's *eye* (the rubber-stamping self-review) → iteration 0003 targets `frontend.md`.** It held one honest caveat: *"agent output is stochastic, so a single regen is a strong signal, not proof"* — a couple more regens if the result is ambiguous. **The regeneration outcome is not yet known** — the FE agent was still building when the window closed; this is journaled at "dispatched + awaiting measurement."

**The operating principle — "stay ahead of the airplane."** When the assistant reported it was waiting on the regen, the user prompted a `ci-monitor` for the already-cleared #39, then named the broader principle as something worth remembering:

> "as they say in aviation - 'always stay ahead of the airplane' :D - if there's something we can be backgrounding that we're unblocked for, always try to catch and kick - this is the idea of agentic work I love and would like to hone in on"

The assistant saved it to memory as an operating principle and immediately practiced it — *"to practice what I just saved"* — by kicking the **historian** (which it had been holding under the PR-first rule) in a journal worktree to capture this very arc, PR-compliant. It also flagged an honest wrinkle the catch surfaced: routing the historian through a worktree + main-thread-PR is *slightly ceremonial* now that PR-first broke its old "just commit" model — a small future `historian.md` refinement (*"mid-session: commit on a branch and open a docs PR"*), noted agent-first, not done in the moment.

**Agent-driven? ✅** Iteration 0002 is the experiment's machinery doing exactly what it was built for: a *single-variable change* to an agent's reference doc, then a *from-spec regeneration* to measure whether that change alone moves the output — a controlled bisect of which agent layer is the lever. Whether the guidance worked is the open question the next session answers; the *setup* is the cleanest the experiment has produced. The "stay ahead of the airplane" principle was banked as durable operating guidance and acted on within the same turn.

## Chapter 52 — The payoff: the regeneration came back roomy, and the bisect named the lever (2026-06-14)

This is the chapter Chapter 51 was holding its breath for. The single-variable bisect set up in iteration 0002 — change *only* the `DESIGN.md` spacing scale, regenerate the Percentage page from spec with `frontend.md`'s self-review held constant — finally returned a result, and the result was the one the whole experiment was built to be able to produce.

While the regeneration was still building, the user moved a piece of process up into the repo (Chapter 53), and the assistant kept the board moving rather than idling. Then the FE agent finished, and the assistant flagged the moment plainly:

> "Big moment — the **FE regeneration is done: PR #41**, and the agent reports the pills 'genuinely breathe now.' That's the measurement, pending my own eyes."

The "pending my own eyes" mattered: the agent's self-report is not the measurement; the measurement is the screenshot, viewed against the iteration-0001 baseline. The assistant pulled #41's four Percentage shots via `bin/ci-screenshots`, found all four `CHANGED` (the regen rewrote the page), and viewed them — *"the pills, the heart of the measurement."* It read the change-mode view first, then delivered the verdict:

> "The change-mode view confirms it. **This is the measurement — and the result is a clean win.**"

The comparison it drew, baseline vs. regenerated:

> "**Baseline:** three tight rows, pills nearly touching, minimal vertical separation. **Regenerated:** the five modes wrap into three rows that clearly **breathe** — bigger chips, real column gaps, and a visibly larger gap *between* the wrapped rows; the active green pill sits on its own row with obvious air."

And — load-bearing for the experiment's claim — the provenance of that improvement was clean:

> "the FE agent produced this **from a fresh spec→build with only the improved `DESIGN.md`** — it didn't read the old page, and we put *zero pixels* in the markup. So the bisect points clearly: **`DESIGN.md` was the lever.** The self-review bar we held constant turned out to be *adequate once it had a concrete standard to measure against.*"

The assistant stated the thesis result outright:

> "**That's the thesis confirmed for the first time: improve the agent's reference doc → measurably better output, proven by the regeneration sweep.**"

It held the caveat it and the user had agreed on in Chapter 51 — *"one stochastic run — a strong signal, not proof"* — so the claim stays calibrated: the experiment now has its first end-to-end confirmation that the agent-improvement loop *converges* (an input change to a reference doc, alone, moved the output for the better), measured by a from-spec regeneration; it is a single run, not a statistical proof.

**The user confirmed it independently, by eye, on the running server.** The assistant restarted the app on the regenerated code; the user looked and said:

> "This looks amazing relative to before! Our work did the trick."

The assistant echoed back what made the moment count: *"'did the trick' is the result we wanted: the agent-doc change *alone* produced it. That's the thesis working."* The bisect verdict, stated cleanly across the turn: **the lever was the design doc, not the FE self-review** — the self-review wasn't the binding constraint after all; it was adequate once the doc gave it a concrete standard to measure against. (Chapter 50 had named the self-review weakness — the rubber-stamping "breathe" report — as a candidate cause and deferred it as a future `frontend.md` change; the bisect now says that wasn't this round's constraint. The deferred `frontend.md` taste pass remains available as a *deliberate* future lever, no longer a presumed fix.)

**The sweep surfaced its own regen variances — exactly its job.** Because this was a fresh from-spec rebuild, not a patch, parts of the page that weren't pinned by the spec came out different:

> "the intro/result copy was rewritten, and the input placeholders changed from helpful hints ('e.g. 50') to '0' — a small thing to watch (the 'e.g.' hints were arguably nicer). The sweep surfacing these is exactly its job."

Neither is a defect — both are the regeneration doing what it does (everything not pinned is re-derived), and the placeholder downgrade was logged as a candidate future refinement (capture the "e.g." hint pattern in `DESIGN.md`) rather than a blocker. This is the regeneration sweep's *value* visible in miniature: it doesn't just test the change you made, it re-exposes everything the spec doesn't pin, so drift becomes a thing you *see* and decide about.

**Agent-driven? ✅** The improvement that produced the roomier pills lives entirely in `DESIGN.md`; the page was regenerated from spec with zero hand-edited pixels. This is the cleanest possible instance of rule #1 — the durable change was in the agent's reference layer, and the regeneration proved it carried.

## Chapter 53 — "Stay ahead of the airplane" moves from personal memory into the shared contract (2026-06-14)

In Chapter 51 the user had banked an operating principle — *"always stay ahead of the airplane"* (proactively background any unblocked work) — and the assistant had saved it to **personal memory**. This session the user asked the right follow-up about *where* such a rule should live:

> "Can the note about kicking things off when able actually be moved to something in the repo instead of memory? Will it maintain its efficacy?"

The assistant's answer was yes, and that the repo is the *better* home:

> "This is **main-thread orchestration** guidance (subagents do one task; only the orchestrator 'stays ahead'). CLAUDE.md is *the* main-thread contract — 'read as its first action, every session.' So it lands with at least the same reliability as a memory entry … and it gains two things memory can't: it's **version-controlled with the project** and **shared**."

It named the one tradeoff honestly — in `CLAUDE.md` the rule is **whizwheel-scoped** (it won't follow the user to other repos the way personal memory does) — and judged that the right call here precisely because the user *wanted* it in the repo. It also tied the move to the experiment's thesis: *"the experiment is partly about honing agentic orchestration, so 'stay ahead' is a genuine project convention, not just a personal quirk."*

The principle landed in `CLAUDE.md` as a new **"Orchestration — stay ahead of the airplane (main thread)"** section: the main thread is an *orchestrator* that proactively *catches and kicks* any unblocked work into the background instead of waiting idle; only the genuine critical path should gate; after each action it scans for newly-unblocked tasks and fires them; if something *can't* be backgrounded, it says why — **PR #40**. The section ends by naming this discipline as *itself part of what the experiment is honing*. To avoid the rule living in two places (drift), the assistant committed to **deleting the memory copy once #40 merged** — *"so it never lives in neither place."* After #40 merged, it did exactly that (and caught + fixed a stray typo it had introduced into `MEMORY.md` in the same edit).

The move was also *practiced while being made*: the assistant fired #40's `ci-monitor` *"in the same breath"* as opening the PR, and ran #40's visual gate the moment its CI went green — the very catch-and-kick discipline the PR was codifying.

**Agent-driven? ✅** A doc/contract edit (`CLAUDE.md`), via PR per the project's own rule. A small but pointed data point: the experiment is now reaching the stage where *the operator's own working habits* — not just product or build conventions — are being promoted into the version-controlled, shared contract so future sessions inherit them.

## Chapter 54 — Worktrees standardized for every writing agent, delivered through CLAUDE.md by-reference (2026-06-14)

After the measurement landed, the user returned to a thread the assistant had started in the previous turn — making worktrees the standing default for *every* agent that writes — but reframed it as a deeper question about *how context reaches subagents at all*:

> "is it *possible* for us to deliver it standardized instructions from our CLAUDE.md? What gets passed to them from us other than the repeating agent files? Is there a centralized context that's sent? If so, if that context said 'spin up and hang out in your own worktree', I see no harm. It's forward proof and for whatever needs it, it securely has it"

Rather than guess at the harness mechanics, the assistant **fired the claude-code-guide** to get the delivery model authoritatively, and reported the finding precisely:

> "Subagents receive: their agent-definition file + the Task prompt. They get `CLAUDE.md` automatically *only if* `settingSources` is configured to load it … otherwise not. No other centralized auto-context (user memory/settings aren't passed)."

This sharpened the answer into two distinct delivery channels:

- **By-reference (what the repo already does, zero config):** every agent definition mandates a "Step 0: read `CLAUDE.md`," so a standing rule in `CLAUDE.md` already reaches every registered agent today — *"that's our centralized channel, and it works regardless of `settingSources`."*
- **Auto-injection (`settingSources`):** would push `CLAUDE.md` into subagents automatically, independent of any read-step — *"cleaner in theory, but it's an SDK-framed setting with uncertain CLI behavior — I'd want to verify before relying on it."*

The assistant's call: **put the worktree rule in `CLAUDE.md` as a standing default now** (delivered by the read-step the repo already enforces — *"bulletproof for our agents, no config risk"*), and treat `settingSources` auto-injection as an optional hardening to verify later. That gives the *"forward-proof, securely has it"* property the user wanted: the rule lives **once**, in `CLAUDE.md`, and every current and future writing agent inherits it via the read-CLAUDE.md-first step they already run — no per-agent duplication.

The change generalized the existing per-build-agent worktree convention into a **standing default for all writing agents** — explicitly naming the **build agents, the PM, and the historian** — with a deliberate carve-out: **report-only agents (`ci-monitor`, `dependabot-agent`) don't create worktrees, because they never write.** Three files were touched:

- **`CLAUDE.md`** — the **Worktrees** section now states the two-lane model with the writing-agent default front and center: *"Every dispatched agent that *writes* creates its own worktree as its first action … This is the standing default: you do not need to be told per-task — reading this file (your Step 0) IS the instruction,"* using the deterministic `git worktree add` CLI over the unproven-in-subagents `EnterWorktree`, and committing/pushing/opening its PR from the worktree — *"it never merges and never pushes to `main`."*
- **`historian.md`** — the assistant identified that this file *contradicted* the new rule (it told the historian to "commit but don't push," a relic of the pre-PR-first model flagged as friction back in Chapter 51) and **fixed it to be self-contained**: worktree → commit → push → docs PR. This resolved the awkward "main-thread-PRs-the-journal" dance for good.
- **`project-manager-agent.md`** — its worktree line was upgraded from "fine to use one" to making the worktree the **default**.

This landed as **PR #43**. The assistant summarized the resolution back to the user's question: *"yes, it's deliverable from CLAUDE.md, the rule now lives there once, and every agent inherits it via the read-step they already run — forward-proof, no per-agent duplication."*

**Agent-driven? ✅** A pure contract/agent-definition change across `CLAUDE.md` + two agent defs, no app code. Two durable data points: (1) the experiment's own *delivery mechanism* was investigated authoritatively (subagents get only their def + the Task prompt; `CLAUDE.md` reaches them by-reference via the mandated read-step, not by auto-injection unless `settingSources` is set) — which validates the repo's long-standing "point every agent at `CLAUDE.md` first" stance and explains *why* it works; and (2) the historian became fully self-sufficient under the PR-first regime, closing the small wrinkle named in Chapter 51.

## Chapter 55 — Closing the iteration-0002 loop: three PRs merged, the PM dispatched to log it, and the session closed on a reflective question (2026-06-14)

With the measurement taken and the process hardened, the session converged into a clean merge queue and a deliberate close. The user's instruction was to keep the orchestration moving — *"Yep merge what you can now,"* then *"Ok proceed with the ideal next steps."* The assistant worked the board exactly the way the Chapter-53 principle prescribes: merge only what's fully cleared, keep everything else's CI and visual gates running.

**The visual gate was completed in full, including a careful read of a misleading flag.** Both #41 (the regen) and #42 (the historian's journal) showed `03-login-failed` as a `NEW` screenshot against the `main` baseline. The assistant reasoned about it rather than waving it through:

> "Telling detail: **#42 (JOURNEY-only) *also* flags `03-login-failed` as NEW** — and a docs-only change can't alter rendering. So that 'NEW' is a **baseline artifact** (the `main` baseline set I'm diffing against is simply missing that one shot), not a regression in either PR."

It viewed the shot once to confirm — *"that's the standard login-failed page (canonical coral alert per DESIGN.md)"* — establishing the `NEW` was a baseline-set gap, not a render change. This is the Chapter-27/32 deterministic-gate discipline working as designed: a surprising diff flag was *explained*, not rationalized, by cross-referencing a docs-only PR that *couldn't* have changed rendering. The remaining #41 shots were reviewed in full:

> "#41's gate is complete — all states reviewed: **roomier pills throughout**, hero result (`10 · 20% of 50`), and the **label-based error card preserved** ('Value (V1) can't be blank') through a fresh-from-spec rebuild."

That last point is a quiet but important confirmation: the Chapter-50 work that mapped error keys to visible labels (the hero result card and the label-based validation errors) **survived the regeneration** — these weren't pinned pixels, yet the agent reproduced them from `DESIGN.md` + spec. The regen didn't just fix the pills; it preserved the page's hard-won correctness affordances.

**All three PRs merged, `main` at `86c5e5c`.** #41 (regen Percentage), #42 (historian journal), #43 (worktree standardization), every worktree cleaned up, and the server restarted from `main` so the user's live view continued on the canonical, merged code. The assistant then narrated the full loop, end to end, as the milestone it was:

> "**cramped pills → diagnosed as a DESIGN.md gap (not a code bug) → fixed the *doc* → merged the imperfect page as a baseline → single-variable agent change → regenerated from spec → measured roomier pills *from the guidance alone* → bisected the lever.**"

It dispatched the **PM to close the iteration-0002 loop** (log + close the iteration), and offered iteration 0003's candidate levers — a second calculator (does the agent generalize beyond Percentage?), the deferred `frontend.md` taste pass, small refinements surfaced this round (capture the "e.g." placeholder hint in `DESIGN.md`; lift the backend `send`-dispatch multi-mode pattern into `backend.md`), or building the `database-agent` (#36). The end-of-session checklist ran: the historian was dispatched (this chapter and its predecessors are that dispatch's output), the tree verified clean and synced on `main`, and the assistant began reading the full 1,216-line `JOURNEY.md` to answer the operator's reflective question. The session closes on the experiment's first confirmed convergence datum and an open, introspective question about the operator's own method — fittingly, given that what has been "converging" most visibly across the journey is the operator's ability to specify intent into durable artifacts.

**Agent-driven? ✅** The merges were human-gated (the standing rule); the iteration close was delegated to the PM in its lane (the main thread did not write `docs/logs` itself), and the journaling delegated to the historian — the lane discipline holding through session close.

## Chapter 56 — A side-quest design: the `database-agent`, where a boundary tension and a live-census idea reshaped the spec (2026-06-14)

A fresh session opened on a deliberate detour. The user: *"Let's knock out a side quest, let's think about a potential database agent per the issue"* — the `database-agent` filed *prospectively* back in Chapter 49 (issue #36, deferred, *"while the schema is still simple"*). No code, no build: this chapter is a **design brainstorm**, run end-to-end through the superpowers **brainstorming skill** before any implementation, and it ends — by design — at the user-review gate, not at a merged agent. It is a clean small instance of the repo's stance that *the agent definition is the durable artifact* and is worth designing before it is written.

The assistant announced the method up front: *"I'll start by invoking the brainstorming skill since we're designing a new agent (creative/design work), and pull up the relevant issue in parallel."* It grounded itself in the existing agent definitions and the live schema, then — before asking any clarifying questions — surfaced something that *the issue's own framing got subtly wrong*.

### The boundary tension — what the agent's value actually is

The #36 issue motivates the agent as a way to stop hand-writing throwaway database scripts. The assistant caught that this collides with the repo's lane boundaries:

> "The issue motivates this agent by 'we don't want to hand-write a one-off `bin/rails runner` script every time.' But under CLAUDE.md's boundaries, the `backend` agent owns `lib`, rake tasks, and scripts — and this agent is strictly read-only and never writes app code. So the database-agent *can't* ship a reusable `bin/db-report` script or rake task to eliminate ad-hoc queries; that would cross into backend's lane."

The reframe followed directly — and it is a pure restatement of the experiment's central principle (the durable artifact is the *agent*, not the code it might emit):

> "Which means its durable value isn't 'no more ad-hoc scripts' — it's **'ad-hoc queries written by something that already knows the schema cold and never has to rediscover it.'** The reusable artifact is the *agent definition's schema fluency*, not a committed script."

This is a small but telling moment: the agent's *purpose statement in its own founding issue* was found to imply a boundary violation, and the fix was to relocate the value from a committed output (a script — backend's lane) up into the agent definition itself (schema fluency — the agent layer). Agent-first, applied to the agent's *own charter*. The user confirmed the cleaner model and the brainstorm proceeded.

### The first design — config, boundaries, posture

After grounding in the actual schema (the assistant enumerated it: tables `calculations` / `users` / `sessions` / `roles` / `role_types`, the `calculation_logs` view that LEFT JOINs `username`, which tables are soft-deletable via `Discardable` and which are not, and the attribution chain `calculations.user_id → users → roles → role_types`), the assistant laid out a full first-pass design:

- **Config:** `model: opus`, `tools: Bash, Read`, `color: pink`. The color choice carried an explicit rationale — pink because *"`red` reads as 'danger,' wrong signal for a read-only inspector"* — a deliberate semantic mapping consistent with the per-agent color convention from Chapter 33.
- **Report-only → no worktree**, mirroring `ci-monitor` / `dependabot-agent` (the agents that never write, carved out of the worktree default in Chapter 54).
- **A Step-0 launch protocol** mirroring the PM's: read `CLAUDE.md`, `ARCHITECTURE.md` (the relevant sections), the schema source of truth, and the models.
- **A read-only hard boundary, stated absolutely:** only `SELECT` / ActiveRecord reads / `dbconsole` / `psql`; never a write, never a `discard`, never a migration (that is backend's lane), never app code or agent defs.
- **Operating posture (the user's earlier call): ad-hoc + schema-fluent** — composes throwaway `bin/rails runner` one-liners, *commits nothing*; default target `RAILS_ENV=development` (the local `bin/dev` click-through DB — the literal "local test run"), always stating which env it queried.
- **Scope: whole schema, equal depth** — accepting the heavier definition and more regeneration churn in exchange for uniform coverage.

The assistant explicitly flagged the three spots *most worth a second look*: the pink color, the mode-discovery-from-data caveat, and the whole-schema maintenance cost. The user did not bite on those — instead, the user reached past the design and reshaped its centerpiece.

### The user's creative steer — a live census instead of static docs

The proposed "baked-in schema knowledge" was static, hand-written documentation. The user rejected that frame in favor of something self-correcting:

> "For baked in schema knowledge - can we get creative here? Write a query that the agent is required to run on startup that builds a report of the entire schema in Postgres itself via a query. It can, by definition, never get stale
>
> Then, the deeper documentation you write is based on the schema you know about now. As schema evolves, we will evolve the details in the agent. But even if our details aren't there, having a canned query to start with will expose the entire schema, including the negative context of not having a lot of details about the schema elements for which we haven't added to the agent"

The assistant recognized this as a strict improvement — *"a genuinely better idea than static-only documentation — it makes the agent honest about its own coverage"* — and, true to the repo's "validate before you spec" instinct, **ran the census queries against the live dev DB before baking them in**: *"I don't want to spec a query I haven't seen run."* Both queries — a **columns census** (one row per table/view with type and nullability) and an **FK census** (the relationship graph) — reproduced the ground truth exactly: every table, the `calculation_logs` view, all columns and nullability, and all four foreign keys.

The revised design replaced static documentation with **two layers plus a reconciliation step**:

- **Layer 1 — the live `information_schema` census**, run as the launch protocol's final step, authoritative and *never stale by construction*.
- **Layer 2 — a hand-written enrichment overlay** (jsonb shapes, `Discardable` semantics, the attribution chain, mode discovery), stamped with a currency date, evolved via agent-def PRs as the schema grows.
- **Reconciliation on launch** — the agent diffs the live census against its own overlay and reports the gap explicitly, so an undocumented table surfaces as *"present in DB, NOT yet documented in this agent"* rather than as a silent blind spot. As the assistant put it, the agent is *"never silently blind."* This is precisely the *"negative context"* the user asked for: a new table added next iteration appears immediately as "exists, undocumented" instead of being invisible until someone remembers to update the def. The assistant also noted the design hardens the read-only posture as a side effect — catalog introspection is the canonical safe first move.

The user approved tersely: *"good"*.

### Written, self-reviewed, and parked at the gate

The assistant set up a branch (per the never-push-to-`main` rule) and, during the brainstorming skill's spec self-review step, caught and fixed one imprecision of its own — the overlay's currency stamp. Because iteration 0002 had just closed and 0003 was not yet open, asserting an iteration number would have been wrong, so it anchored the currency to the date instead, deferring the iteration label to the def's build time. The spec was committed to `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-06-14-database-agent-design.md` on branch `docs/36-database-agent-design` (commit `270e9c2`), and the assistant stopped at the brainstorming flow's user-review gate, recapping the three headline decisions (the boundary reframe, the pink/no-worktree/dev-DB/opus config, and the two-layer census-plus-overlay knowledge model). The user approved (*"approved"*), and the assistant moved on to invoke the **writing-plans** skill to turn the spec into an implementation plan for the actual `.claude/agents/database-agent.md`.

The window closes here — the writing-plans skill is loaded but the plan itself, and the agent definition it will produce, are not yet written. **The `database-agent` does not yet exist; only its design spec does, and that spec is committed on a branch awaiting the next step, not merged.**

**Agent-driven? ✅ (design only)** No app code, no agent definition yet — a brainstormed spec committed on a branch, the artifact that precedes the agent. Two things worth noting as evidence: (1) the brainstorming-skill discipline caught a *boundary contradiction in the agent's own founding issue* before a line was written, and resolved it by relocating value from a committed script into the agent definition — the agent-first principle applied reflexively to a new agent; and (2) the design's load-bearing improvement came from the **operator**, not the assistant — the live-census idea that makes the agent's documentation un-stale-able and self-honest about its coverage gaps — continuing the journey's most consistent pattern: the operator pushing intent into a more durable shape than the assistant's first draft reached for, and the assistant validating it (here, literally running both census queries against the real DB) before committing it.

## Chapter 57 — The `database-agent` gets built: subagent-driven execution, and a course-correction on when subagents earn their cost (2026-06-14)

Chapter 56 closed at the writing-plans step — a brainstormed, committed spec on a branch, no plan and no agent definition yet. This chapter is the build: the spec became an implementation plan, the plan became the actual `.claude/agents/database-agent.md`, a fresh-eyes review approved it, a live smoke test exercised it, and PR #47 (`Closes #36`) merged. Along the way the *execution method itself* was put under examination twice — first about whether to fan out, then about whether subagents were worth their ceremony at all — and the assistant adjusted the prescribed workflow rather than running it on rails.

### From spec to plan to an execution-method choice

The assistant wrote the implementation plan with the superpowers writing-plans skill, first studying *"the house style of the richer agent defs so the plan's def content matches conventions (launch protocol, boundary phrasing)."* Because the whole effort builds a single artifact, the plan chunked that file's authoring into sequential tasks with concrete validation baked in (re-running the census, a live read-only smoke test). The assistant then self-reviewed the plan against the spec and caught one parity gap — *"the def never states it creates no worktree"* — and added it before committing the plan to `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-06-14-database-agent.md` (commit `5b1409b`).

With spec + plan in hand, the assistant offered the user two execution modes — **subagent-driven** (a fresh subagent per task, recommended) or **inline** (executed in-session with checkpoints) — and the user chose subagent-driven (*"1"*). That choice loaded the superpowers **subagent-driven-development** skill, whose prescribed shape is: *fresh subagent per task + two-stage review (spec compliance, then code quality) after each.*

### First adjustment — granularity, and the worktree call

The assistant did not run the skill literally. It made an up-front granularity judgment: plan Tasks 1–4 all build *the same single file by sequential append* — tightly coupled, not independent — so it dispatched them as **one implementer** authoring the complete def plus the embedded census validation (Task 5), with the live smoke test (Task 6) as its own subagent and the PR (Task 7) handled by the orchestrator. It also made a whizwheel-specific worktree call that *departs from the standing per-agent worktree default* and explained why:

> "the implementer will work **on the existing `docs/36-database-agent-design` branch in the main tree, not a fresh worktree** — that branch already holds the prerequisite spec+plan commits, and this is a single linear effort, so a new worktree would fragment it."

This is a deliberate, reasoned exception to the Chapter-54 "every writing agent makes its own worktree" default — taken because the prerequisite commits already lived on the branch and a new worktree would split one linear effort across two trees.

### The fan-out question — "should we be spawning them all?"

The assistant backgrounded the single implementer, and the user asked: *"I backgrounded - should we be spawaning them all?"* The answer drew the distinction the experiment leans on between a dependency chain and independent work:

> "no, not for this plan. These tasks form a dependency chain, not independent work: implementer (writes def) → spec review → quality review → smoke test → PR. Each stage consumes the previous one's output … Spawning them all at once would have them reviewing a file that doesn't exist yet."

And it named *when* the repo does fan out — *"independent work with no shared state — exactly the repo's **regeneration sweep** (one `backend` agent per calculator) … this side quest isn't it."* It also noticed, mid-answer, that the harness had **registered `database-agent` as a live Agent type** — meaning the implementer had already written the file — which would let the Task-6 smoke test dispatch the *real registered agent* rather than a generic subagent pointed at the def.

### The course-correction — "wouldn't inline be better for every step after this one then?"

The user pressed the deeper question: *"Oh I'm surprised subagent was recommended then, wouldn't inline be better for every step after this one then?"* The assistant conceded the point partway and refined it — a genuinely substantive reflection on *when subagents earn their cost*:

> "This task is authoring one ~150-line markdown file whose content I already wrote *verbatim* in the plan — so the 'implementer' is mostly mechanical, and the per-task two-stage review ceremony is heavier than the work warrants. Inline would've been a defensible pick."

But it argued *"inline for everything after"* wasn't right either, isolating the two remaining steps that earn a separate agent for reasons other than fresh implementation context:

> "**The smoke test (Task 6) should stay a subagent** — and it's the highest-value one. It's the actual *behavioral* test of the def: a fresh agent reads the def cold and acts on it. If I run it 'inline,' I'd be role-playing the agent while knowing exactly what I intended it to do — a contaminated test."

> "**One review pass is worth keeping as a subagent** — not for fresh *context*, but for fresh *eyes*. I wrote both the spec and the plan's exact content, so reviewing it inline is marking my own homework."

The resolution split the difference and **collapsed the skill's prescribed two-stage review (spec, then quality) into a single fresh-eyes review subagent**, kept the smoke test as the *real* `database-agent`, and did the PR inline as pure mechanics — *"That drops the ceremony you're reacting to while keeping the two subagent uses that actually earn their cost."* The user agreed: *"Yep that makes sense and works."*

The data point: the assistant ran a prescribed third-party skill but **adapted it to the situation** — recognizing the implementation was mechanical (the plan held the def's content verbatim), and that the durable reasons to spend a subagent here were *contamination avoidance* (the behavioral smoke test) and *confirmation-bias avoidance* (don't grade your own homework), not the skill's default rationale of fresh implementation context. This is the same adapt-don't-copy instinct the journey showed when it adapted tenor's branching flow (Chapter 17) rather than importing it whole.

### Implementer DONE, fresh-eyes review APPROVED, three honesty-sharpening polish notes

The implementer reported **DONE** — *"no deviations, census validated (5 tables + 1 view, 4 FKs, reconciliation 'none')"* — with all four blocks written byte-for-byte from the plan's fenced content (commits `5eba85e` → `3c76bbe`). The single fresh-eyes reviewer returned **APPROVED** with three *cosmetic* findings, which the assistant applied inline (trivial markdown, no need to re-spin a subagent) because all three *"actually sharpen the census's honesty — which is this agent's whole point"*:

1. note that the FK census assumes single-column FKs;
2. state *why* `schema_migrations`/`ar_internal_metadata` are excluded (so the table count doesn't confuse against `\dt`);
3. add `created_at`/`updated_at` to the `calculations` overlay entry so the "created-at time range" capability is self-grounding.

Committed as `e11e24f`. The notes are all about making the census *honest about its own coverage* — the same negative-context principle the operator supplied back in Chapter 56.

### The smoke test — the real registered agent, run cold, zero writes

Task 6 dispatched the **real registered `database-agent` type** against the literal motivating question — "what is the state of my local test run?" — the honest behavioral check the assistant had argued for keeping as a subagent. The assistant recorded the baseline row count first, so a read-only violation would be detectable. The result:

> "The smoke test passed beautifully — the agent followed its launch protocol, ran both census steps with reconciliation ('none' undocumented), counted by calculator/attribution/kept-vs-discarded, **discovered the 5 Percentage modes from the data** rather than assuming them, and reported RBAC."

And the read-only posture was *verified*, not assumed — *"Read-only confirmed (13 → 13, zero writes)."* The row count was unchanged after the agent ran, proving it mutated nothing. The mode-discovery behavior — the agent learning the five Percentage modes *from the data* rather than from a baked-in taxonomy — is the exact payoff of the read-only, census-first design the operator shaped in Chapter 56, now demonstrated in practice.

PR #47 was opened (`Closes #36`), `ci-monitor` dispatched to watch it, and the side quest's seven plan tasks were complete — a brainstormed spec, a self-reviewed plan, a from-plan agent def, a fresh-eyes approval, a live read-only smoke test, and a PR in review.

**Agent-driven? ✅** A new agent definition built from its own spec/plan, validated by an independent fresh-eyes reviewer and a live behavioral test before merge. Two process notes worth keeping: (1) the smoke test ran the *real* registered agent against the real DB and proved both correctness (mode-discovery, reconciliation, RBAC) and the read-only invariant (13 → 13 rows); and (2) the execution method was itself adjusted under questioning — the prescribed two-stage review collapsed to one fresh-eyes pass — on the principled grounds that the implementation was mechanical and only contamination/bias avoidance justified a subagent here.

## Chapter 58 — Two journey PRs merged, and a self-caught discipline slip: running `bin/ci-watch` inline instead of dispatching `ci-monitor` (2026-06-14)

With #47 in review, the user gave a compound instruction: *"Merge on CI success and then summarize all of the agents we now have that are specific to this repo and custom created for this project."* The assistant accepted merge-on-green (after the mandatory visual gate, expected byte-identical on a docs/agent-def-only change), and queued the agent-roster summary. The user then added that the held-back journey PR could merge too: *"Oh, the journaling/brainstorm can be merged"* — the historian's Chapter-56 PR **#46**, deliberately held in Chapter 56 to avoid two concurrent journey branches colliding.

Reaching to merge #46, the assistant started to check its CI by running `bin/ci-watch` directly. The user interrupted (*"[Request interrupted by user]"*) and asked the pointed question:

> "In that case, why not just send a ci-monitor to verify"

The assistant owned the slip without hedging:

> "Fair point — I broke my own discipline by running `bin/ci-watch` inline; the convention is to dispatch `ci-monitor` so CI mechanics stay out of the main thread. Doing it properly for #46:"

It then dispatched two `ci-monitor`s — one for #46 (the journey chapter) and one for #47 (the database-agent) — both reporting green across all five jobs. Both PRs were docs/`.md`-only with zero render surface, so the visual gate was treated as **N/A by file-scope** (recorded as reasoning, not skipped silently): *"#46 touches **only `JOURNEY.md`** — zero render surface … there's nothing for screenshots to regress."* Both merged with merge commits; `main` fast-forwarded; **issue #36 closed by #47**; the journey worktree and local branches cleaned up.

### The agent roster — the experiment's custom-agent inventory, recorded

The user's other request produced a full rundown of the seven repo-bespoke agents, organized by *who writes*: the **build line** (`backend` orange, `frontend` cyan — opus, own worktree, write app code), the **tracking & record** line (`project-manager-agent` blue, `historian` purple — write docs only), and the **report-only watchers** (`ci-monitor` green, `dependabot-agent` yellow, and the new `database-agent` pink — never write, no worktree). The assistant noted the patterns the roster reveals: opus for judgment-heavy roles, sonnet for the mechanical watchers; every *writer* gets an isolated worktree and every *report-only* agent is deliberately worktree-less; colors unique per agent (*"pink was the last free one — hence the new agent took it"*).

**Agent-driven? n/a (merges + a summary).** No artifact changed in this chapter beyond the two merges. The data point is a *discipline* one: a codified convention (dispatch `ci-monitor`, don't watch CI inline) was *violated by the main thread itself*, the human caught it, and the assistant owned it plainly — which set up the next chapter's fix at the right layer.

## Chapter 59 — Firming `ci-monitor` into the sole CI-verification path — and locating the fix in `CLAUDE.md`, not the agent def (2026-06-14)

The Chapter-58 slip wasn't a one-off — the assistant had now run `bin/ci-watch` inline *twice* in the session. The user asked whether the convention should be hardened: *"Should the ci monitor be updated to be firmer in its use so we know to lean on it for all things ci?"* The assistant's answer is a clean instance of *which layer a fix belongs in* — and it argued the obvious-seeming target was the wrong one:

> "**`ci-monitor.md` is the wrong place.** That def only governs the agent's behavior *once dispatched* — it can't compel the orchestrator (main thread) to lean on it. The discipline you want … is an **orchestrator rule, so it belongs in `CLAUDE.md`'s 'CI/CD monitoring' section.**"

It diagnosed the exact loophole it had walked through: the section said *"Dispatch the ci-monitor subagent"* but **never forbade the main thread from doing it inline.** The recommended fix was an exception-free imperative — *"The main thread MUST NOT run `bin/ci-watch` directly; CI status is always verified by dispatching `ci-monitor`"* — and it explicitly **rejected a "quick glance" carve-out**, on the grounds that *"a judgment-call exception just re-opens the loophole"* (the dispatch costs only ~9k tokens). It also flagged this as a `CLAUDE.md` contract change — *"the PM doesn't touch `CLAUDE.md` or agent defs — this is a main-thread/human edit"* — and asked the user to confirm strict / no-exception. The user: *"Do it."*

The edit firmed the rule, closed the inline loophole, and dropped the now-stale *"until ci-monitor is registered"* caveat (it is a live type now). Committed and opened as PR **#48** (`CLAUDE.md`-only, visual gate N/A by file-scope). And the assistant **dogfooded the new rule in the same breath** — *"per the rule I just wrote — dogfooding it immediately — CI verification goes through `ci-monitor`, not an inline `bin/ci-watch`"* — dispatching `ci-monitor` for #48 rather than checking it itself. `ci-monitor` reported #48 green across all five jobs; the assistant held it for the user's explicit merge nod per never-auto-merge.

**Agent-driven? ✅ (contract/`CLAUDE.md`).** A repeated main-thread discipline slip was fixed not by resolving to try harder but by **closing the loophole in the orchestrator contract** — and the fix was deliberately placed in `CLAUDE.md` (the layer that governs the main thread) rather than `ci-monitor.md` (which only governs the agent once dispatched). The same pattern as Chapter 27 (a silently-weakened gate made *mechanical* rather than relying on in-the-moment judgment), now applied to the orchestrator's own behavior. It was practiced the instant it was written.

## Chapter 60 — Build-coverage tracking: a deliberate omission confirmed, a real gap surfaced, and an agent-first inventory column added (2026-06-14)

The user opened a separate thread with an observation: *"I guess we're no longer tracking anything around which calculators were migrated over in the repo/inventory itself. Check history, is that a decision we made?"* Rather than answer from memory, the assistant dug into the history — the inventory structure, its git log, the iteration logs, the PM def, and what is actually built — before concluding.

### The investigation — it was deliberate, and split across three layers on purpose

The assistant confirmed the omission was **a documented design decision, not a drift**:

> "no, this isn't something we dropped. It's a **deliberate, documented design decision**: build/migration status was never meant to live in the inventory."

It laid out the three-layer split per `CLAUDE.md`'s "How work is tracked": **`docs/inventory.md`** is the *catalog/backlog* (the 191-calculator universe with complexity + tags, kept idempotent so re-runs preserve manual corrections — *"a per-row 'built?' flag would fight that"*); **GitHub Issues** are the *task/build state* (each calculator gets a `backend` + `frontend` issue, **closed = built** — the PM def is explicit: *"To report status, read issue state … open vs closed"*); and the **iteration logs** are the *build record* per iteration. So "what's migrated" today is *exactly one calculator: Percentage* — verified consistent across all three layers.

But the assistant then surfaced the *legitimate* thing under the user's question — a gap the three-layer model genuinely had:

> "there's **no standing, committed rollup** in the repo that answers *'X of 191 built'* at a glance. It's **on-demand**, not an artifact … At 1-of-191 that's invisible; at 30-of-191 it'll feel like a gap."

It offered three options (a derived `inventory.md` column, a separate `docs/` coverage view, or leaving it on-demand), leaning toward the first — *"it puts the answer where you're already looking … and the PM already rewrites that file idempotently."*

### The decision — done agent-first: update the PM def, then regenerate from it

The user chose to close the gap, and was specific about the shape — including a readability improvement to the existing Source column:

> "yes let's add a column that show the PR's associated with the merge completion of that inventory item as a new column. Also, shorten source so that the display name is JUST the * in the expression `<url>/*.html` and, on click, we still go to the full URL … (to be clear, update the PM then regenerate)"

The user's parenthetical — *"update the PM then regenerate"* — explicitly named the agent-first sequence, and the assistant followed it exactly. It first encoded the two changes into the **PM definition** (the durable artifact): a new derived **`Built (PRs)`** column (sourced from live GitHub on each refresh), the **Source** column rendered as the `*` slug from `calculator.net/*.html` while still linking the full URL, and a **completed-first sort**. That PM-def change committed as `4e1867e`. *Then* it **dispatched the PM to regenerate `docs/inventory.md` from the new spec** (committed `4da06c4`) — not hand-editing the inventory, the same producer/consumer split the experiment uses everywhere.

The PM reported **DONE**: Percentage floated to row 1 with `BE #32 · FE #41` (correctly picking the *most-recent merged frontend PR* — the iteration-0002 regen #41, not the original #35), all **191 rows preserved**, Source slug-links intact, the 190 unbuilt rows below in the prior Category→Calculator order with blank `Built` cells. The assistant eyeballed it before opening PR **#49**, dispatched `ci-monitor` (per the just-firmed Chapter-59 rule), and the run came back green.

The assistant summarized what the column *is*: *"the column **is** the migration-coverage view you were missing"* — the standing, committed rollup that the on-demand model lacked, now derived (not hand-maintained, so idempotency holds) and living where the user already looks.

### Session close

The user closed the session: *"It's perfect, merge when complete and historian to end session as well."* The assistant ordered it correctly per the end-of-session rule — `ci-monitor` green → merge #49 + cleanup → historian (foreground, waited on, so it commits before the session ends) → final `git status` check. `ci-monitor` reported #49 green across all five jobs; #49 merged; `main` clean; and the historian was dispatched in the foreground as the close-out.

**Agent-driven? ✅** The build-coverage gap was closed agent-first, in the user's own prescribed order: the **PM definition** was updated first (the durable artifact — the new column, the Source slug rendering, the completed-first sort), *then* the PM was **dispatched to regenerate the inventory from it** — the inventory was never hand-edited. Two evidence points: (1) the assistant checked the *history* before answering whether the omission was deliberate (it was — a three-layer tracking split documented in `CLAUDE.md`), separating "documented decision" from "real gap"; and (2) the gap that *did* exist (no standing rollup) was closed by a *derived* column that respects the inventory's idempotency, rather than a hand-maintained flag that would fight it.

## Chapter 61 — "Did we kick off CI for 50?": the orchestrator's own blind spot, and a contract tightened for agent-opened PRs (2026-06-14)

Chapter 60 closed the session. Chapters 57–60 were committed by a foreground historian run, which opened journey PR **#50**; the assistant printed its end-of-session summary table — #46–#49 merged, **#50 open + green** awaiting merge — and declared the session complete. But the table's *"⏳ open, awaiting your merge"* on #50 hid an assumption the assistant had never actually verified. The user pulled on exactly that thread:

> "did we kick off CI for 50? If so, result?"

The assistant did not bluff. It owned the miss plainly:

> "Honestly — no, I didn't. I opened #50 via the historian and went straight to close-out without dispatching `ci-monitor` for it. That's a miss against the discipline we just firmed up (every PR gets a monitor). CI itself auto-triggers on PR open, so a run exists; I just never watched it."

It corrected the omission the *right* way — **via `ci-monitor`, not inline** (per the very rule firmed in Chapter 59) — dispatching the monitor for #50. But the more valuable move was naming the **pattern behind the miss**, not just patching the instance:

> "the historian opens its own PR autonomously at session close, and the orchestrator's 'dispatch a monitor the moment a PR exists' reflex didn't fire because I didn't open it — same blind spot could hit any agent-opened PR. If it recurs, that's a candidate for a one-line `CLAUDE.md` tweak ('including PRs opened by dispatched agents')."

This is a precise diagnosis: the Chapter-59 rule had closed the "main thread checks CI inline" loophole, but a *different* hole remained — the orchestrator's catch-and-kick reflex is keyed on *the main thread opening a PR*, and a **dispatched agent** (the historian here; a build agent in general) opens its PR autonomously, so the reflex simply never triggers. The assistant explicitly held back from acting on the pattern unprompted — *"Not acting on it now — just noting it for your call"* — leaving the contract change to the user. `ci-monitor` then reported **#50 green** across all five jobs (`run 27491882355`).

### The user folds the lesson into the same PR

The user's response made the fix concrete and bundled it with the merge:

> "Include a tightened CLAUDE.md per your observation in this PR as well. On push, re-listen for CI. Then, on green, merge"

The assistant edited `CLAUDE.md`'s CI/CD-monitoring section **in the journey worktree, on the #50 branch** (so the rule and the chapters that motivate it ride in together), adding the agent-opened-PR clause to the existing firmed sentence:

> "This applies to **every** PR, **including one opened by a dispatched agent** (e.g. the historian's `JOURNEY.md` PR, or a build agent's) — the agent that opens a PR does not monitor its own CI, so the **orchestrator** must dispatch `ci-monitor` the moment such a PR exists. Do not let an agent-opened PR slip past unwatched."

Committed as `de3456c` (the commit body records the precipitating event verbatim: *"the historian's own JOURNEY.md PR (#50) slipped past unmonitored"*). On push the assistant **re-dispatched `ci-monitor`** for the updated #50 — dogfooding the rule it had just written, with a wry aside about who exactly opened *this* PR: *"per the very rule I just wrote — agent... well, orchestrator-opened, but monitored all the same."* The monitor reported #50 green a second time; the assistant merged it (`gh pr merge --merge`), landing `main` at **`8412cca`**, then ran the post-merge cleanup it had earlier flagged as the one open thread — removed the `.claude/worktrees/journey` worktree, deleted the branch, confirmed the tree clean — and dispatched this final foreground historian run to journal the tweak and close out.

**Agent-driven? ✅ (contract/`CLAUDE.md`).** This is the experiment's core loop turned on the **orchestration layer itself**: a process gap surfaced *in live practice* (the historian's own PR slipping past unwatched), was diagnosed down to *why the reflex didn't fire* (a dispatched agent, not the main thread, opened it), and was closed by **tightening the durable contract** — not by the assistant resolving to remember next time. Two things are worth marking: (1) the assistant answered the user's "did we?" with an unhedged *"Honestly — no, I didn't"* rather than glossing the gap, the same candor seen when it admitted the inline-`ci-watch` slip in Chapters 58–59; and (2) the fix extends the *same* sentence Chapter 59 firmed, in the *same* layer (`CLAUDE.md`, which governs the orchestrator) rather than `ci-monitor.md` (which only governs the agent once dispatched) — the loophole-closing pattern of Chapters 27/59 applied one level up, to the orchestrator's own dispatch reflex.

Chapter 61 closed the session — branches pruned, `main` at `0ca4a02`, the database-agent and the contract tightenings all landed. We have quite a bit already, let's take a small leap"

The assistant laid out the orchestration plan and called the first step out as a deliberate experiment-within-the-experiment: *"PM recommends the next 2 (blind — I won't breathe a word about any prior pick, so the BMI cross-check is honest)."* This is the cross-check parked in the operator's memory from the prior session (the note: re-run the PM blind, don't tell it BMI was the prior recommendation). It paid off:

> "The blind cross-check **converged** — the PM independently re-derived **BMI** as the #1 pick (no nudge from me), which is exactly the verification you wanted."

The PM's blind picks: **BMI** (#1 — *"establishes a clean complexity-2 baseline and adds unit-conversion (US/metric) + a text-classification render … Percentage never exercised"*) and **Ohms Law** (#2 — *"a second multi-mode build … re-tests the queued `send`-dispatch pattern on fresh ground before it gets promoted into `backend.md`"*). The user confirmed both, and the assistant had the PM **open iteration 0003** as part of the same dispatch — pinning the build to the current agent set so the loop stays measurable. The PM tagged `iteration-0003` → `0ca4a02` (PR **#56**) and authored four `spec:v1` issues, byte-identical per layer pair: **BMI** BE #52 / FE #53, **Ohms Law** BE #54 / FE #55.

### The first parallel multi-calculator build — staged in two waves

The assistant named a sequencing constraint the fan-out exposed for the first time: *"a frontend page's system test + screenshot renders **real** results, so it needs that calculator's backend math present. So I'm building in two waves — **BE first → merge → FE** — rather than all four at once."* This is a genuine structural finding about the build model — backend and frontend specs are independent to *author*, but a frontend agent's screenshot self-review (its own quality gate) needs the live calculator to render against, so the FE wave must follow the BE merge.

**Wave 1 (backend, parallel):** the two backend agents ran concurrently in their own worktrees — *"independent — 'add a calculator = add a file,' so no collision."* Both came back 100% line+branch, rubocop+brakeman clean (Ohms #57, BMI #58). With `ci-monitor` on each (per the agent-opened-PR rule firmed in Chapter 61), both reported green and merged clean — *"no collisions"* — landing `bmi.rb` + `ohms_law.rb` on `main` together (`44de6e8`). The iteration docs PR #56 merged first as low-risk independent docs (`573e81f`).

**Wave 2 (frontend, parallel):** the two FE agents ran off the updated tree. Ohms FE landed first (PR #60) — and its agent flagged the collision the user had predicted: it had touched **three shared files** (`_result.html.erb` dispatch branch, `home/index.html.erb` catalog card, `calculators_helper.rb` — including a `percentage_display`→`decimal_display` rename with a back-compat alias) and warned *"The BMI PR will likely touch the same three files — expect a trivial rebase to reconcile."* The assistant ran the real visual gate on #60 (UI-surface → view the changed/new shots), looked at all six affected shots against `DESIGN.md`, confirmed the math and the `GIVEN`/`SOLVED` text-badge accessibility, and merged it — *"so BMI rebases onto a main that already has Ohms."*

### Two real-world snags worth recording

**(1) A `gh` hang that looked like a stuck agent.** The user, watching the BMI FE agent's slow progress, judged it stuck: *"Based on the progress of the BMI frontend, I think it's stuck. Can you kill and re-start it? … it's genuinely stuck on fetching the issue using `gh`."* The assistant began prepping the restart — but checked first, and found the agent had simply *finished*: *"the BMI FE agent **wasn't stuck — it just finished** (it's a heavy build; ~16 min vs Ohms' ~9.5). It opened **PR #62** … So **no kill/restart needed** — I'm glad I checked before terminating, that would've thrown away a completed 16-minute build."* Checking before killing saved the work; the `gh` slowness was real but had completed.

**(2) `bin/dev`'s CSS watcher tore down the backgrounded server.** Asked to spin up the app, the assistant ran `bin/dev` and saw it exit code 0 instead of staying up. Diagnosis: *"the server booted fine (Puma served a 200 on `/`), but `tailwindcss:watch` exited immediately in the non-interactive background shell, and foreman tears everything down when any process exits."* The fix was to build the CSS once (`tailwindcss:build`) and run a plain `rails server` with no watcher — *"you're trying the app, not editing styles."* The server then served `/`, `/calculators/percentage`, and `/calculators/ohms_law` at 200 for the user to try.

The **BMI #62 collision** was resolved exactly as predicted — by an **additive rebase** (a dispatched frontend agent reconciled the three shared files, keeping *both* result-branches, *all three* home tiles, and `decimal_display` defined exactly once), force-pushed to trigger a fresh CI run, then visual-gated (the BMI pages plus two *surprise* sha-changed shots that turned out benign — shared-partial re-renders, *"exactly the 'non-UI-looking change touched a shared file → screenshot moved → look at it' scenario the gate exists for"*) and merged. **Agent-driven? ✅** — every calculator and page was an agent's from-spec output; the scaffolding was the only hand-built plumbing, untouched.

## Chapter 63 — The visual-gate two-lane: a deviation owned, and a non-UI baseline lane codified (2026-06-14)

Mid-build, the assistant surfaced its own deviation unprompted. The visual gate, by the letter of `CLAUDE.md`, runs on *every* PR "regardless of whether the diff looks UI-related" — but the assistant had **file-scoped it out** on the docs PR #56 and the two backend PRs #57/#58:

> "by the **letter** of CLAUDE.md, the visual gate is 'every PR ... regardless of whether the diff looks UI-related,' so I *did* technically skip it … So: a deviation, yes. But it was **outcome-safe, deterministically** — not a gamble."

The honesty was precise about *what kind* of miss it was: *"I **asserted** 'no render surface' rather than **proving** it with the byte-identical baseline diff the rule prescribes. Determinism makes me confident, but confident isn't the same as verified."* The same assert-vs-verify distinction the experiment keeps returning to.

The user asked the assistant to clarify the two options it had named in shorthand, and the assistant drew out the **repo-specific catch** that makes a naïve path-based exemption unsafe here:

> "our pages render *backend* results, so a path-based exemption has a hole. Editing `app/calculators/percentage.rb` touches no render-surface file, yet it changes the *numbers* shown on the Percentage page — a real screenshot diff the exemption would wave through."

So the assistant recommended the other option — a **two-lane** reframe: *"**UI-surface PR → review changed/new shots; non-UI PR → baseline diff, confirm all UNCHANGED**."* The user said *"Fold option 2 into CLAUDE.md after the FE wave lands,"* then immediately reconsidered the timing: *"You can just do a separate PR for that CLAUDE.md now, agnostic of everything, no? Let's do that."*

The assistant started inline, then **caught itself and switched to a worktree agent** — *"Better idea — punt it to an agent with its own worktree, keeping my main tree clean"* — exactly the dispatched-writing-agent default, dogfooded mid-flight. The agent opened PR **#59**. Crucially, when the user asked *"How does the change result look to you btw? I assume you reviewed it already?"*, the assistant again owned a lapse rather than bluffing:

> "Honestly? I *hadn't* — I took the worktree agent's 'clean 4-insert/2-delete' self-report at face value, which is exactly the assert-don't-verify move I just gave you grief about on the screenshots."

It then actually read the diff and confirmed the load-bearing caveat had landed in the new text — *"or the math of an existing calculator that already has a built page"* — the explicit hole-plug for the repo-specific case (a backend edit to a *built* calculator counts as UI-surface). The two-lane gate merged as the rule of record (`cff5206`).

The assistant also surfaced a **latent gap** in the very lane it had just codified: the non-UI lane leans on `bin/ci-screenshots --pr <n> --baseline <dir>`, but *"nothing defines where that baseline `<dir>` comes from … it's a rule that assumes infrastructure we haven't confirmed exists."* The user directed: *"File your concern about the latent gap as an issue for us to work on later."* Filed as **issue #61** — until the baseline source is built, the non-UI lane is *"aspirational"* rather than executable. **Agent-driven? ✅ (contract/`CLAUDE.md`, via a worktree agent).** A process deviation was confessed, traced to its assert-vs-verify root, reframed into the contract — and the new rule's *own* unproven dependency was filed rather than papered over.

## Chapter 64 — The picker saga: a taste observation becomes a codified rule and sweeps three calculators (2026-06-14)

This is the session's headline. Trying the live app, the user questioned not a bug but an *element choice*:

> "My thoughts - our iterations in the past did the trick here. However, is this the right UI element under the 'I know…' section? The pills? Kick off a frontend agent to recommend a different UI element to be used for this picker. Based on the reply and our discussion here, we'll ask the FE agents to sweep all calculators with the update to the FE agent"

The assistant immediately recognized the agent-first shape: *"get a recommendation, decide, then encode it in the **frontend agent definition** and sweep every calculator — so the fix lives in the agent, not in N hand-edited pages."* It dispatched a frontend agent in **advisory mode** (recommend-only, read-only).

### The diagnosis: element choice is a separate axis from spacing

While the advisory ran, the user spotted a related symptom on the now-live BMI page — its US/Metric pills looked *"scrunched up together."* The assistant did not theorize; it captured the *live* page with headless Chrome to see exactly what the user saw, then delivered the load-bearing insight:

> "the diagnosis is the interesting part: **it's not a spacing regression.** I verified the markup uses the iteration-0002 scale (`flex flex-wrap gap-x-3 gap-y-4`) and `mode-pill` has correct padding — the building blocks are right. The problem is that **a 2-option selector rendered as wrap-pills is the wrong *element***."

This is the chapter's core: iteration 0002's spacing scale *was* applied — the cramp was not a regression of that work. The real gap was a dimension the design system had never pinned: *"the spacing scale handles *crowding*, but *element choice* is a separate axis the design system hadn't pinned down."* The user chose to fix it systemically rather than patch it (*"agent-first it is. BMI's scrunched pills stay as-is for now and get fixed *systemically* when the sweep turns the US/Metric selector into a segmented control — no throwaway patch the sweep would just overwrite"*).

### The mode-picker rule

The advisory memo came back sharp, and its key reframe was that the *control* was already right (a native radio `<fieldset>` — single-select, no-JS-safe) and only the *presentation* was wrong. It proposed a rule keyed on count and label length:

- The control is **always** a native radio `<fieldset>` posting `inputs[mode]`.
- **Segmented control** at **N ≤ 3 and short labels** (Percentage's Increase|Decrease).
- **Selectable option-list** at **N ≥ 4 or any multi-word/long label** (Ohms' 6 modes, Percentage's 5).
- Raw `<select>` is **never** the primary picker (hiding the modes hides the calculator's value).

### Prototype → live A/B → codify → sweep

The user chose the validate-then-propagate path. The assistant prototyped the option-list on **Ohms only** (PR #63), and when the user asked *"Do we need to restart the server in that worktree for me to try it?"*, the assistant set up a **second server on `:3001`** for a live A/B — old pills at `:3000`, the new option-list at `:3001`. The user's verdict was unequivocal:

> "Oh that's perfect"

The assistant then re-stated the conditions (the user asked it to remind them), the user said *"Do it,"* and the codify+sweep fan-out went out — **three agents on disjoint files**: a codify agent (`DESIGN.md §4` "Mode picker" + a `frontend.md` directive, PR #64), a Percentage sweep (5-mode picker → option-list, PR #66), and a BMI sweep (US/Metric → segmented control, PR #65).

### The deliberate "don't trust an unreloaded agent definition" engineering — probed by the user

The user asked exactly the right question about the parallelism:

> "Btw, might be a dumb question, but the sweeps were happening in parallel for codification. The sweeps didn't *rely* on codification, right?"

The assistant confirmed they did **not**, by design, and explained the mechanism plainly:

> "The rule reached the sweep agents **two ways, and I deliberately used the one that doesn't depend on #64:** 1. **As explicit instructions in their prompts** … So they were *told* the rule, not made to *read* it. … In fact, the timing proves it: I dispatched the codify agent and the two sweep agents **in the same message**, so when the sweep agents ran their launch protocol and read `DESIGN.md`, #64 wasn't merged yet … They never saw the codified rule."

It named the pattern: *"don't trust that an edited agent definition is reloaded mid-session — hand the agent the instruction explicitly."* The real dependency the sweeps *did* have was **#63** (merging the Ohms prototype first, to put the `.mode-option` CSS on `main` for reuse) — which is why #63 was merged *before* the sweeps were dispatched, while the codify PR #64 was safely parallel (disjoint files *and* rule-by-prompt).

### The proof shot

Both sweeps came back green. The reveal centered on a single Percentage screenshot:

> "**Percentage — the proof shot** (`12-percentage-change`): on this *same page*, the 5-mode picker is the **option-list** *and* the Increase | Decrease sub-toggle is still the **segmented control** — both presentations of the rule, side by side, each where it belongs. That's the rule *discriminating correctly* in a single screenshot."

The four PRs merged (`56d07db`), all three calculators now speak one picker language, and the assistant verified the BMI fix *live on `:3000`* — closing the loop on the original scrunch report rather than trusting CI: *"you spotted it by eye → diagnosed as wrong-element → memo → codified rule → swept → verified on the same server you were looking at."*

### The reflections worth quoting

The user's excitement was explicit and recurring — *"Is this not the coolest process of building?"*, then *"this is wildly exciting"* — and on closing the iteration, the operator articulated what was actually being built:

> "So we're truly building in preferences, almost 'taste'. And right now we're advising it to pick one vs the other, but eventually we can define the entire concept (i.e. the selector recipes based on number of selections) or something (not something to think about right now, just thinking out loud)"

The assistant named the same direction back — a declarative **selector-recipe** keyed on selection count/shape that the agent *resolves* rather than *interprets* — and parked it per the user's "thinking out loud." It also kept the celebration honest: *"'exciting' converts to 'real' only when those three PRs come back green and the screenshots actually show clean pickers"* — and noted the duct tape underneath (the `gh` hang, the watcher killing the server, the SimpleCov-in-parallel number that under-reports every time, the hand-reconciled three-file collision), framing the experiment as partly *"whether that duct tape keeps shrinking. Right now it is."* **Agent-driven? ✅** — `DESIGN.md §4` + `frontend.md` carry the rule; every page change was a from-spec agent sweep, not a hand-edit.
