## Chapter 65 — "Forward progress?" — the honest framing: reach, not autonomy, and not a clean A/B (2026-06-14)

Before concluding, the user asked the assistant for its own read: *"So do you view this iteration (opinion please) as forward progress? 2-4 sentences."* The answer is worth banking verbatim as the chapter's load-bearing assessment, because it draws a distinction the running assessment below leans on:

> "Yes — and specifically the *most valuable* kind. … the load-bearing result is that the convergence loop closed on **taste**: a vague 'those pills look off' became a codified rule that propagated to every calculator and will constrain ones that don't exist yet. That's the system's *reach* expanding from math-correctness and spacing into design judgment — genuinely new ground."

And the reservation, stated without hedging:

> "But I'd hold one honest reservation: it advanced the system's *reach* more than its *autonomy*. You caught the scrunch, you made every merge call, you chose the rollout — the agents executed beautifully but didn't notice the problem themselves, and this iteration wasn't the clean controlled A/B that 0002 was (the agent set shifted mid-flight)."

This framing is the honest counterweight to the celebration of Chapter 64: iteration 0003 is forward progress on what the loop can *encode* (it now encodes design taste), but it is explicitly **not** a clean single-variable bisect like iteration 0002 — the agent set was changed *mid-iteration* (the picker rule was encoded reactively, in response to a problem found during the build), so the controlled measurement is deferred. The iteration-0003 close-out PR recorded this in the same terms: *"Logged **honestly as not a clean A/B**: the agent set evolved mid-iteration … so the controlled measurement is deferred to a future regeneration sweep."* **Documented, not graded** — the journal records the operator caught the problem and drove the rollout; the agents did not self-notice.

The conclusion turned into a fourth process-gap-to-contract conversion. The user, mid-close, asked an almost offhand question:

Then the user filed one more refinement — issue **#70**: *"inventory completed calculators should be ordered by BE PR # ASC, and then the rest of the incomplete calculators should be alpha."* Rather than a separate PR, the user chose to **fold #70 into the inventory PR** (*"dispatch agent to add a commit to this branch specifically"*); the fold agent found the work already present on the branch from a prior run (commit `2834d79`) and **verified rather than duplicated** it, yielding the completed-block order **Percentage #32 → Ohms Law #57 → BMI #58** (BE-PR ascending), with `Closes #70`.

Two post-conclusion beats opened while the historian ran (off the record, parallel and read-only): the user asked the PM to pick the **next five** calculators and to *"Replace the previous top 1 BMI memory with this for next time"* (BMI having now shipped — the parked memory is spent), and floated a possible `CLAUDE.md` update — *"we should prefer backgrounding agents unless otherwise specified (such as end of session historian)"* — a candidate orchestration-default tightening left for the user's call. **Agent-driven? ✅ (PM definition + PM-regenerated `docs/inventory.md`).** The session's fourth instance of the same loop: a gap spotted in practice, fixed both immediately *and* as a durable contract change at the layer that governs it.

**The orchestration side-thread became its own standalone PR.** When the user said *"Fold it in with a separate PR,"* the assistant shipped the foreground-carve-out sharpening (the "idling is not a reason to block" nuance) as a standalone `CLAUDE.md` edit — kept separate from the iteration work. **The fan-out was dispatched, all backgrounded.** With the five confirmed and 0004 decided, the assistant kicked off three parallel workstreams and stayed idle to catch the next unblock: the **PM** (backgrounded) to open iteration-0004 and author **10 spec:v1 issues** (5 calculators × backend+frontend, plus planning the regen-sweep-as-separate-PR), the general-purpose agent for the CLAUDE.md PR (#74, since landed), and the historian to journal the kickoff (this chapter). ## Chapter 69 — The carve-out PR lands, and the non-UI visual gate throws its first false positives (2026-06-14)

The window opens with the user clearing the lone main-thread change from Chapter 68. *"Merge 74 once CICD is done,"* the user said — and then, a beat later, added the check the assistant had skated past: *"Btw, that assumes you're happy with the edit."* The assistant owned the gap rather than hiding behind the spec it had handed the worktree agent — *"Fair — I should actually look at it, not just trust the spec I handed over"* — read the diff, and endorsed it on its merits: *"I endorse the edit on its merits, not just because I speced it."* A small but recurring pattern this session: the operator catching a step the orchestrator was about to assert through, and the orchestrator reversing to *prove* rather than assume.

Then `ci-monitor` reported PR #74 green (all five jobs), and the **non-UI baseline visual gate** — the very lane Chapter 63 codified — threw a surprise. PR #74 changed *only* `CLAUDE.md`, yet the baseline diff reported **6 CHANGED screenshots**. The assistant did not merge through it; it named the fork exactly: *"A docs-only change cannot move a rendered pixel — so either the screenshots aren't actually deterministic (which would undermine the whole non-UI baseline gate, issue #61's exact concern) or something else is drifting. Per CLAUDE.md I must **stop and view** a surprise CHANGED set before merge, not merge through it."* It dispatched a vision subagent to classify the 6 changed pairs, holding the merge: *"Merge of #74 is **held** pending that investigation — exactly the case CLAUDE.md says not to merge through."*

The vision subagent returned a clean verdict: **all 6 are false positives** — *"vertical scroll-position jitter at capture time, transient native number-input spinner arrows (focus/hover state), and sub-pixel anti-aliasing. No layout moved, no content changed, no style differs."* With the gate satisfied and the user's merge instruction in hand, the assistant merged #74 (`c1b1db6`) and cleaned up — and immediately surfaced the underlying finding rather than let it dissolve: *"the non-UI baseline gate threw **6 false positives** because the screenshot harness doesn't capture deterministically."* When it tried to file that as an issue, the **permission classifier denied it** ("filing a new issue wasn't part of what you asked, and it's an external write"); the assistant accepted the boundary and offered the finding for the user's go instead. The user then asked it to also file the recurring `--delete-branch`-vs-worktree ordering bug it had hit during #74 cleanup: *"Can you also file the whole --delete-branch thing that we've run into a number of times?"* Both landed — **#86** (`--delete-branch` vs worktree ordering) and **#87** (non-deterministic screenshot capture / false-positive CHANGED). **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — the carve-out shipped as a worktree-agent PR, the visual gate ran exactly as codified (held the merge, dispatched vision, viewed before merging), and the two findings became tracked issues rather than mental notes.

## Chapter 71 — The backend wave merges green, and the regen A/B's first half lands: rendered-equivalent output from spec (2026-06-14)

The backend PRs poured in over roughly ten minutes — Simple Interest (#89), Mean/Median/Mode/Range (#90), Tip (#91), Age (#95), Amortization (#97), and the three regens BMI (#92), Percentage (#94), Ohms (#96). The assistant dispatched a `ci-monitor` per PR and ran each visual gate proactively at green, and in doing so confirmed a structural fact the gate had only ever asserted before: **a backend-only calculator file does not surface on the home page.** On #89 the manifest showed *"still exactly 16 shots, no new Simple Interest page, and `01-home-anonymous` is byte-identical to baseline"* — proving the new-build backend PRs are genuinely non-UI (the calculator stays invisible until its frontend ships), so the §3 "add a calculator by adding a file" rule holds at the backend layer with zero shared-file churn (every backend agent reported *"no shared files touched"*).

The session's most elegant gate-handling moment came when the assistant stopped eyeballing the #87 noise shot-by-shot and reasoned about it instead. With multiple PRs in flight it noticed a **cross-PR correlation**: *"every CHANGED shot has also flagged in a PR that doesn't touch that calculator (e.g. `22-bmi-metric` changed in the Age PR, which has nothing to do with BMI …). A shot that 'changes' in an unrelated PR *cannot* be a real change — so the entire CHANGED set is provably #87 noise, no eyeballing needed."* That is a clean logical disproof of false positives — a sharper instrument than the per-shot vision check, and a concrete validation that #87 is non-determinism rather than regression.

The same lens delivered the **regen sweep's backend half**: *"every regen's own result shots are stable (BMI: default/us/error unchanged; Percentage: default/result/change unchanged) → the regenerated backends produce rendered-equivalent output. Clean positive A/B signal."* The regenerated math, rebuilt from spec under the pinned agent set, produces the *same* rendered results as the prior code — the regeneration sweep working as designed (Chapter 35's "git diff as a clean A/B on the agent"). The backend agents also surfaced two findings for the experiment: the **Tip `people` integer-coercion gap** (the `:integer` cast truncates `"2.5"`→`2` before validation, so a hard `only_integer` rejection branch is unreachable under 100% coverage) and the **Percentage non-numeric `:decimal` coercion gap** (`"abc"`→`0.0`, so the nil-means-missing pattern doesn't reject arbitrary non-numeric strings) — both flagged by the agents as belonging in the **backend agent definition / `Base` contract**, *not* patched in calculator code (which the next sweep would erase). The assistant merged the seven ready PRs (`8d0a39d`), then #97 once green (`ee28299`) — **four new calculators + three regenerations** on `main` in one wave, all independent files, no conflicts. **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — every calculator is agent-built from spec, every finding routed to the agent layer.

Two reusable patterns also got named as missing agents. The assistant noted it had *"now hand-rolled that same vision-confirm subagent **three times** this session"* — a strong signal it should be first-class — and filed **#93** for a `visual-qa-agent` (cross-linked to the #72 meta-agent and #87). The hand-rolled vision review would recur through the rest of the build, each time an ad-hoc `general-purpose` dispatch standing in for the not-yet-built agent.

## Chapter 72 — A worktree-isolation leak, caught live: `fe-tip` writes into the main checkout (2026-06-14)

With the backends merged, the assistant fanned out the **frontend wave**. Mid-dispatch, a `git checkout main` surfaced something it should not have: *"the merge succeeded … **but `git checkout main` surfaced two uncommitted modifications in the main working tree**: `app/helpers/calculators_helper.rb` and `app/views/calculators/_result.html.erb`. Those are shared frontend files — a regen frontend agent may be writing into the main checkout instead of its worktree (the same failure mode the historian hit)."* This is the worktree-isolation hazard the contract had warned about, now caught *in the act* rather than after damage.

The assistant did not flail. It traced the leak to a single culprit before touching anything: *"the `fe-tip` worktree is **empty** and *all* Tip work (the helper map, a new `money_display` helper, the views) landed in **main's working tree**. The work looks sound — it's just in the wrong place. The agent created its worktree but operated with cwd at the repo root."* It confirmed the agent's own self-diagnosis from its dying output — *"The view/helper/CSS edits I made were on the main checkout path, not the worktree"* — stopped it, and then addressed the *systemic* risk before the individual one: **six more frontend agents were running with the same potential flaw, all editing the same shared files.** It checked every frontend worktree and cleared the bigger fear: *"only `fe-tip` leaked. All 6 other frontend agents are correctly isolated … So it was a one-off slip by that one agent, not a systemic agent-def flaw — the other 6 keep running untouched."*

The recovery was textbook agent-first: file the lesson, discard the misplaced work, re-dispatch hardened. Issue **#98** *"codif[ied] the worktree-isolation leak with root-cause + agent-def fix direction, cross-linked to the historian's sibling slip and #72"* — naming that two distinct writing agents (the historian in Chapter 68's note, now `fe-tip`) have hit the same cwd-vs-worktree trap, so the fix belongs in the agent definition, not in a one-off scolding. The assistant discarded the leaked Tip files, removed the worktree, and re-dispatched `fe-tip` *"with hardened isolation — `cd` into the worktree, verify `pwd`, all paths worktree-absolute, and a pre-commit self-check that main stays clean."* The hardened dispatch later reported back **isolation verified clean** — *"the hardened prompt worked — pre-commit self-check confirmed main stayed empty."* **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — the failure was contained, the misplaced (correct) work discarded rather than salvaged in place, and the durable fix is a filed issue plus a hardened re-dispatch prompt; nothing was hand-patched into `main`.

## Chapter 73 — The mode-picker A/B pays off: 3 of 3 regen pickers chosen correctly, from spec alone (2026-06-14)

This is the chapter iteration 0004 was built to produce — the **controlled measurement Chapter 65 deferred and Chapter 68 scheduled.** The three regeneration frontends were dispatched with one deliberate omission, which the assistant named explicitly: *"The 3 regen frontends I'm deliberately **not** telling which picker to use — that's the whole A/B: they get only the spec + DESIGN and must reach for the right control unprompted."* Each agent, regenerating from its closed `spec:v1` issue + `DESIGN.md`, chose its picker independently — and all three chose correctly, with the *discrimination* that proves the rule isn't a blind default.

**Percentage (#99)** reached for the **option list** for its 5-mode main picker and the **segmented control** for the 2-way Increase|Decrease sub-toggle. The agent's own report shows it derived this from the design rule, not memory: *"The five modes are all multi-word (N ≥ 4 / multi-word labels), so the design system dictates the bordered, divided full-width option list … I reached this independently; it's exactly what DESIGN.md §4 prescribes for this shape, so the A/B confirms the prior choice rather than diverging from it."* **Ohms Law (#100)** independently picked the **option list** for its 6 multi-word modes (*"N=6 (≥4) and labels are multi-word … the spec mandates the `.mode-option` list, not pills"*). And **BMI (#101)** — the discriminating case — chose the **segmented control** for US|Metric: *"N=2 with short labels … DESIGN.md §4 'Mode picker' prescribes a segmented control for N ≤ 3 with short labels and explicitly names 'BMI's US|Metric' as the canonical segmented-control example."*

The assistant read the result precisely as the experiment intends it: *"this is the **strongest A/B signal yet**: the agent chose the **segmented control** for US|Metric (N=2, short labels), correctly *discriminating* from the option list it used for Percentage/Ohms. So the rule isn't being blindly defaulted — the agent applies the right control per shape … **3/3 regen pickers correct.**"* An independent vision-review agent later confirmed all three against `DESIGN.md` (APPROVE on #99/#100/#101), checking the actual rendered controls — Ohms's bordered `.mode-option` divided list, BMI's connected segmented track — against §4 by eye. And the new builds reinforced it: **Simple Interest (#103)** independently picked the segmented control for its Years|Months selector, which the assistant counted as a fourth confirmation. The regen frontends' result shots changed (as expected — a real, intended A/B difference), while the backend-half result shots had stayed stable (Chapter 71) — together giving both halves of the sweep: equivalent *math*, correctly-chosen *controls*. **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — this is the cleanest agent-layer measurement to date: the picker rule, pinned in `DESIGN.md` from the iteration's open, reproduced by three independent from-spec regenerations with zero prompting and correct discrimination between control types. It directly answers Chapter 65's standing debt that iteration 0003 *"was explicitly not a clean A/B."*

## Chapter 74 — The central-registration friction: a serial merge pipeline, and the frontend's de-facto registry (2026-06-14)

Where the backend wave merged seven-at-once with no conflicts, the **frontend wave did the opposite** — and exposed a structural finding the assistant tracked carefully. Every new-build frontend PR touches the *same* shared files: the result-dispatch in `_result.html.erb`, the label maps and `field_labels_for` dispatch in `calculators_helper.rb`, and the catalog card in `home/index.html.erb` (and the regens collide on `application.css`). The assistant named the consequence plainly: *"every new-build FE PR edits the same 3 central-registration files … and the regens collide on `application.css`. So each merge invalidates the next branch's mergeability — I'll merge one, rebase/resolve the next against the new `main`, repeat. Reliable but one-at-a-time."* A **serial merge pipeline** — the precise opposite of the conflict-free parallel fan-out the no-central-registration rule (#3) guarantees the backend.

The assistant connected it directly to the contract: *"the frontend has **three de-facto central-registration points** … Every new-calc FE PR edits all three, so parallel FE builds *will* conflict on merge … This cuts against the 'no central registration — add a calculator by adding a file' principle that keeps the *backend* conflict-free."* It filed the finding as **#107** (proposing dynamic dispatch / per-calculator registration for the frontend). The pipeline ran exactly as predicted: #99 and #103 merged clean, then each subsequent PR had to merge `main` into its branch and **union-resolve additive conflicts** — handled in-domain by dispatched `frontend` resolver agents rather than risked as hand-merges. The fiddliest reconciliation was the **dual `money_display` helper**: Amortization defined one taking an already-rounded 2dp String from the envelope, Simple Interest defined one taking a BigDecimal; the resolver merged them into *"a single method that handles both inputs … '2dp, half-up, thousands-delimited.'"* The `field_labels_for` case statements likewise had to be *merged into one* with a `when` branch per calculator, not duplicated. Each resolution re-ran the suite to 100% single-process coverage and verified the main checkout stayed clean before pushing.

A second frontend-agent gap surfaced in the same stretch: **MMR (#102) shipped without a home-page card.** The vision review returned CHANGES-NEEDED — *"the page itself is fully BLEND-conformant, but the agent didn't add the MMR card to `home/index.html.erb`, so MMR has no discoverable link from the home grid."* The assistant filed it as **#108** (the frontend agent def needs an explicit "register the home card" step) and dispatched a frontend agent to both resolve MMR's conflicts and add the missing card. A `ci-monitor` diagnosis also explained why **Tip's #106 never fired CI**: its branch was `dirty` (conflicting with `main`), and *"GitHub Actions sometimes skips firing when a PR has merge conflicts with the base"* — so the same conflict-resolution step that the central-registration friction forced *also* re-armed its CI via the `synchronize` push, no separate trigger needed. **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — every conflict resolution went through a dispatched frontend agent in its own worktree, and both structural gaps (central registration #107, home-card registration #108) were filed as agent-def-targeting issues rather than absorbed as orchestration toil.

The session filed a dense batch of findings here — **#107–#112** in one pass — covering frontend central-registration, the frontend-agent registration checklist (the MMR miss), integer-coercion vs `only_integer` (Tip/Amortization), non-numeric `:decimal` coercion (Percentage regen), the SimpleCov-in-parallel undercount, and two DESIGN conventions (financial color: interest shown green in the Amortization donut but coral in tables; and card-eyebrow `faint` vs green §2). The assistant framed this finding-pile as *"the experiment's real output"* — the build's durable value being the agent-layer lessons it surfaces, not the calculators alone.

## Chapter 75 — The app stands up headless, and the last three merge independent (2026-06-14)

Mid-pipeline the user asked a product question: *"Web app on?"* Starting it re-exposed the Chapter 62 failure mode — *"`bin/dev` exited (code 0) instead of staying up."* The assistant diagnosed it precisely rather than retrying blind: *"the Tailwind **watch** process exited immediately (code 0 — it doesn't stay alive in this non-TTY background shell), and foreman then SIGTERM'd everything, taking Puma (which *was* listening fine) down with it."* The workaround it had improvised in Chapter 62 now became a documented standup: *"build the CSS once, then run just the Rails server."* The app came up at `http://localhost:3000` (HTTP 200) serving the seven then-live calculators, and the assistant filed **#113** to codify the headless standup (`db:prepare` → `tailwindcss:build` once → `bin/rails server`, with a suggested `bin/serve-headless` helper) — turning a twice-hit foreman-teardown surprise into a tracked convention.

The user, viewing the live home grid, caught a count discrepancy: *"I only see 4 new ones, shouldn't there be 5?"* — correctly, because Tip (#106) had not yet merged at that point. After interrupting and resuming (*"Sorry, continue filing"*), the user pushed the pipeline to completion: *"I don't see anything running for 106, can you clean these up and merge."* Re-assessing the actual PR state, the assistant found a pleasant surprise that broke the serial constraint for the finish: **the last three PRs were mutually independent.** #106's resolver had in fact finished (merge commit pushed, PR MERGEABLE) — *"I just never got a clean completion ping"* — and crucially *"#100 and #101 are both MERGEABLE/CLEAN with zero conflicts — the regens only touched their own calculator's files … not `application.css` or any central-registration file. So the last three (#106, #100, #101) are **independent** — no serial constraint among them."* This is itself a refinement of the #107 finding: *regens* of existing calculators don't touch the central-registration files (the cards/dispatch already exist), so only *new-build* frontends serialize — the regen frontends could merge in parallel.

The three final gates ran concurrently: #106 Tip (CI green, vision APPROVE — the reviewer counted *"8 cards in the 3-up grid … the new Tip card sits in the bottom row"*), and #100 Ohms + #101 BMI (CI green, vision APPROVE — the reviewer confirmed Ohms's 6-mode option list and BMI's segmented US|Metric control against §4 one final time). The assistant merged all three. **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — the standup failure became a filed convention, and the finish honored every gate (CI via `ci-monitor`, vision review against BLEND) on each independent PR before merging.

The assistant attached one self-aware caveat to #98: because no agent *owns* agent definitions yet (that is precisely the deferred meta-agent #72), the worktree-isolation hardening would go to a general-purpose agent, and — the load-bearing timing point — *"since agent-def edits change the pinned set, this is best landed **now, between iterations**, so the next regen sweep inherits the fix."* The recommendation was explicit: fire the five quick wins, *"each in its own worktree → PR → you merge."* The operator confirmed the fan-out and set the stopping rule: *"Yeah, fan all 5 out, stop short of merging - advise when all 5 are complete and have passing CI."* **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — the very output of the build (its findings) became the input to a new agent-dispatched work phase, triaged by the same agent-vs-human-decision criterion the experiment uses everywhere.

## Chapter 81 — The operator reviews all 8 calculators with his own eyes, and every note routes to the agent (2026-06-14)

With iteration-0004 fully closed and the app running on `:3000`, the operator did the *evaluate* phase by hand — clicking through every calculator and annotating screenshots one by one. The notes were a mix of approvals and three pointed design questions, and the framing was unambiguous: *"let's step through these notes - all changes go to the agent."* That single clause is the experiment's thesis in the operator's own words, applied to a review: nothing he found would be patched in a calculator's code; every finding would become an agent/doc change that the next regeneration sweep propagates.

The approvals were specific and grounded in real use — *"Amortization looks good, I used numbers from my actual mortgage and like what I see"*; *"Simple interest looks good"*; *"tip calculator is perfect"*; *"table at the bottom of Amortization looks good."* He also spot-checked correctness himself, asking *"is that median confirmed accurate?"* — and the assistant verified it by hand against the data: for `1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 5`, the median is the average of the 3rd and 4th sorted values, `(3 + 4) / 2 = 3.5`, with mean, mode, and range all confirmed too. The math layer held under direct inspection.

The three open notes each became an agent-bound decision, and the assistant gave its opinion before the operator confirmed (per the operator's *"Give me your opinion here, once I confirm, let's codify it in the FE agent for the next round"*):

- **Age date default (#3).** The operator asked whether date fields should default to today. The assistant argued *against* silent auto-prefill and *for* an explicit **"Today" quick-fill button** — *"Auto-filling a field silently submits a value the user never typed … A visible 'Today' chip is explicit and discoverable."* Rule for the agent: optional as-of/end dates get a Today button; primary inputs (birth date) never auto-default.
- **"Total Hours" cutoff (#4).** The operator flagged a clipped value and said it was *"unclear how to advise FE agent."* The assistant root-caused it: a rigid 4-up stat grid that couldn't fit a 6-digit value, and prescribed **auto-fit/wrap stat grids** plus *"a test with a worst-case large number so values can't clip — more durable than just shrinking the font."*
- **Amortization charting (#6).** The operator proposed a *"Major change in approach"* — swapping the CSS charts for an interactive library: *"let's ask the FE agent to use another charting library - maybe tradingview to leverage all of its hover features?"* The assistant endorsed **TradingView lightweight-charts** (MIT, ~45KB, importmap-loadable, no build step) for the time-series, then immediately flagged an honest limit: *"TradingView's **lightweight-charts has no pie/donut type**,"* so the donut needs a complementary hover-capable library. It codified the *intent* — JS hover-capable charts for all chart types, the **agent installs what's needed via importmap itself** — and left the final library choice to the agent, *"as you said, it should figure out install + fit."*

The operator also asked to file the home-grid crowding observation (*"this'll start to get crowded, file issue"*), which became **#122**. **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — a full hands-on product review whose entire output was routed into agent definitions and issues, never into code.

The operator agreed twice — *"Perfect, let's correctly slim down frontend agent and make sure DESIGN.md has everything it needs"* — and the assistant did the verification-before-deletion that the slimming demanded: it read *both* PRs' diffs first, confirming **#125 (DESIGN.md §4) was complete** (it fully carried the date field, the responsive stat grid, and the interactive-charts spec) *before* stripping frontend.md down, *"I don't want to delete a convention detail from frontend.md that DESIGN.md doesn't yet capture."* The slim landed as an 18-line net change: a pointer to DESIGN.md §4 plus the two genuine agent-process bits (install the chart-lib importmap pin yourself; ship the worst-case-value stat-grid clipping test). The operator green-lit the pair — *"123 merge when ready"*, *"125 ready to merge when ready"* — and both merged together (main `f8e42c7`). The two artifacts now agree by construction: DESIGN.md leads, frontend.md follows. **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — and a clean instance of the assistant catching its *own* drift risk and re-aiming the work to keep one source of truth.

## Chapter 83 — The parity side-quest: 75% of calculator.net by feature, 100% by correctness — and the gaps cluster into two shapes (2026-06-14)

The operator opened a side-quest: *"ask the PM agent to cross check between all completed calculator against the source on calculator.net to determine to what quantitative extent we are matching what's there to what we have."* The assistant routed it to the PM (it has WebFetch and owns docs/status synthesis) and dogfooded the freshly-merged `bin/serve-headless` to stand the app up for the audit — *"#116's script is validated in practice too,"* working first try.

The PM's audit produced a quantitative answer with two findings the assistant surfaced as the ones that matter. First, the headline: **~75% feature-weighted parity (71/95 source features), 80% simple mean — and 100% correctness.** Per-calculator parity ranged from 100% (Percentage, Tip, MMR — the last *exceeding* its source with four extra stats) down to 53% (BMI, missing child/teen mode, Age/Gender, healthy-weight/BMI-Prime/Ponderal). Second, and more strategically useful: *"**Every gap is *scope*, never *accuracy*** — 100% correctness parity: every calculator reproduces its source to the figure across its reference table,"* and *"**The 24 gaps collapse into two reusable *shapes*** — **inverse / multi-mode solving** and **input-side unit conversion** — not 24 one-offs."* The assistant drew the leverage point: encoding those two patterns into the agents once would lift parity across the *whole* 191-calculator backlog — a stronger candidate iteration theme than chasing per-calculator long-tail outputs.

The report landed as PR #126. The operator then made a placement call — *"docs/parity-calculator-net.md seems like too prominent of a file path. Maybe docs/experiments/* because this is an experimental concept that is not a foundational part of our workflow"* — and the file was relocated to `docs/experiments/parity-calculator-net.md` via a history-preserving `git mv`, merged at `e84b05a`. In the same exchange the operator filed two more pieces of forward direction: a database-agent step to verify newly-created calculators' DB records persist (*"db agent should have some canned sweeps of calculators, and the main thread tells the db agent which calculators to scan for"* → **#127**) and a rename of `inventory.md` → `INVENTORY.md` (**#128**); the per-calculator informational-modal direction filed earlier became **#124**. **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — the parity audit is the first quantitative external-baseline measurement of *what we've built*, and it reframed the long-tail-vs-pattern tradeoff into a concrete future-iteration theme.

## Chapter 84 — The iteration delivery lifecycle is codified: open → build → evaluate → harvest → journal → close (2026-06-14)

The operator named the gap directly. Reflecting on how iteration-0004 had actually unfolded, he observed that the last iteration *"should actually expand until here, because I don't think it covers updates to agents based on the results itself, which happened rather late in the game"* — the harvest had happened *after* the iteration was nominally closed, ad hoc. He sketched the corrected order — *"THEN we start a new iteration, and we *only* do regen for this one … then, we fan out agents to go regen the UI's of all of these"* — and explicitly framed it as a process-design act: *"what I'm doing here is potentially codifying an ambiguous part of our process for delivering each iteration."*

The assistant treated it as exactly that, declining to rush into agent dispatch and instead invoking the brainstorming skill: *"This is a process-design moment — you're defining the iteration *delivery lifecycle* itself, not just the next batch of work."* It diagnosed precisely what CLAUDE.md *did* and *didn't* already say — it defined iterations, the regeneration sweep, and an end-of-session checklist, but *"What it does **not** define is the per-iteration *delivery lifecycle* — specifically the **evaluate → harvest agent/process changes → propagate** phase, which is exactly what happened ad-hoc and late in 0004."*

Through the dialogue the core structural choice settled: *"the regen sweep is the propagation mechanism, and it runs every iteration"* — the next iteration regenerates the priors (carrying the prior harvest) and builds whatever new calculators are asked for, **with the new-build set allowed to be empty (a regen-only iteration).** The assistant proposed a six-phase lifecycle for section-by-section reaction — **Open → Build (fan out) → Evaluate → Harvest → Journal → Close** — with the key fix stated plainly: *"phases 3–4, *evaluate → harvest*, are named, owned, and placed **before close** — that's the ambiguity that bit us in 0004."* Two specifics were folded in and confirmed: **layer-scoped regen** (a sweep rebuilds only the layer(s) whose agents/conventions changed — frontend-only, backend-only, or both), and an amendment to the *closed* iteration-0004 log to add the harvest section that had happened after its close. The operator approved both — *"yes both correct, approved"* — and again later in full — *"yep, let's update 140 ... go all the way to PR."*

A deliberate divergence from the brainstorming skill was named: rather than write a separate spec doc, the lifecycle would be codified *directly into CLAUDE.md* — *"codifying a process *as* a doc-about-a-doc would just create drift."* It landed as PR #130 (`124cde7`, +16/−1: a new "Iteration delivery lifecycle" section plus a light touch on Rule 1 to say a sweep *"may be layer-scoped or even regen-only"*), merged at `e878c66` — and the assistant noted the recursion of practicing it as it codified it: it held the iteration-0005 fan-out until 0005 was formally *opened*, *"practicing the lifecycle we're codifying — open before build."* **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — the operator caught an ambiguous, repeatedly-tripped-over part of the process and turned it into a named, ordered, six-phase contract in the file every agent inherits.

## Chapter 85 — The agents Pages site ships and deploys live, lockstep-by-construction (2026-06-14)

Filed mid-conversation as **#129**, a GitHub Pages site *generated from* the agent definitions — so it cannot drift from them — was built in parallel by a subagent (the operator's *"The other two are sufficiently modular"* confirming it could run independent of the lifecycle work). The agent built `bin/build-agents-site` (standalone, pure-stdlib Ruby — no Rails/DB/Node, so it runs in the Pages workflow without `bundle install`): it parses each `.claude/agents/*.md` front-matter and body, derives a per-agent surface (purpose, model, tools, and a **writes-via-worktree-PR vs report-only** role inferred from whether the toolset has `Write`/`Edit` — `database-agent` correctly resolving to report-only), and emits a static roster index plus one page per agent. It hardened its own markdown renderer through testing — notably handling *"fenced code blocks **nested inside list items**"* (the historian's transcript step had a bash fence inside a numbered list that an early version mangled).

The lockstep property is structural, not a rule: the `.github/workflows/agents-pages.yml` workflow regenerates the site from `.claude/agents/*.md` on every push to `main` (path-filtered), with `_site/` gitignored — *"no checked-in artifact, so the site can't drift; no CI guard needed."* PR #132 merged at `dd31674`, the deploy workflow fired automatically, and the assistant confirmed it went live: **`http://jayravaliya.com/whizwheel/`** returning HTTP 200, *"deploy succeeded on the first try."*

The operator then asked the contract question: *"Have we codified that when agents update, the GH site needs to update too?"* The assistant gave the honest answer — not in CLAUDE.md text, but *"enforced more strongly than a written rule would be"* by the auto-regeneration mechanism — and recommended a small documentation note. The operator declined it: *"Ok, no need to codify, that's more perfect."* The assistant agreed and closed #129 with no follow-up: *"auto-generation *is* the enforcement, cleaner than a rule that could be forgotten."* **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — and a notable case where the *right* answer was to **not** add a CLAUDE.md rule, because the mechanism already made the rule unforgettable; a structural enforcement chosen over a written one.

## Chapter 86 — Iteration-0005 opens: the first regen-only, frontend-only iteration (2026-06-14)

Iteration-0005 was opened against the post-harvest agent set, pinned at `e84b05a` — the first iteration to run the newly-codified lifecycle, and the first **regen-only** one: no new calculators, only a frontend regeneration of all 8 to propagate iteration-0004's harvest. The PM expanded the (closed) iteration-0004 log with its harvest section *and* opened iteration-0005 (PR #131, `6fa212b`, tag pushed), and the operator merged it — *"Merge 131"* — which formally moved 0005 from Open to Build.

The Build phase fanned out **all 8 frontend-regen agents in parallel**, each rebuilding a calculator's UI from spec plus the harvested DESIGN.md §4 conventions. The assistant named the marquee tests up front: **Age** (the Today button + the responsive grid fixing the Total-Hours cutoff), **Amortization** (interactive charts + the importmap self-install bet), and responsive stat grids everywhere. Backend was deliberately untouched — the operator's lifecycle sketch had said *"backend not required because we made no changes relevant to backend,"* and the layer-scoped-regen rule made that a first-class iteration shape rather than an exception. This is the validation of the layer-scoped model codified one chapter earlier: a harvest that only touched the frontend agent and DESIGN.md needs only a frontend regen, and 0005 is exactly that.

The fan-out also re-exercised the known frontend central-registration friction (#107): the regens touched the shared `application.css` (and, for the chart work, `config/importmap.rb`), so the merges would need serial union-resolves — though a refinement from the iteration-0004 analysis held, that *regens* of existing calculators avoid the worst of the new-build shared-file collisions. **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — iteration-0005 is the first iteration delivered entirely under the codified lifecycle, and the first to prove the regen-only / layer-scoped iteration shape in practice.

## Chapter 87 — The importmap self-install bet pays off, and the agents diverge on *how* to implement the same convention (2026-06-14)

The headline experiment of iteration-0005 was whether a frontend agent, handed only the *intent* ("hover-capable JS charts, install what you need via importmap"), would figure out the library install itself. The Amortization regen agent did exactly that: it detected the chart libraries weren't pinned and **installed them itself**, vendoring them under `vendor/javascript` (no runtime CDN) — **lightweight-charts** @5.2.0 (+ its `fancy-canvas` dependency) for the balance-over-time area chart with crosshair/tooltip, and **Chart.js** doughnut for principal-vs-interest with hover tooltips. It even navigated a real packaging snag on its own: the jspm/jsDelivr dist builds of chart.js *"split into sub-chunks … that `bin/importmap` does **not** download, so they 404 at runtime,"* and it resolved it by switching to the self-contained `chart.js/auto` esm.sh bundle. The assistant recorded the result plainly: *"the importmap self-install bet **paid off**: the agent detected the chart libs weren't pinned and installed them itself … It even hit and solved a real snag on its own."*

But the evaluate phase began surfacing signal *during* the build. The assistant noticed the agents had **diverged on how to implement the same convention**: BMI introduced a shared `.stat-grid`/`.stat-card` component in `application.css`, while Ohms/Tip/others inlined the `auto-fit` classes at varying min-widths (8/9/9.5/11/13rem). It flagged this in real time as harvest material: *"That's exactly the kind of inconsistency the evaluate→harvest loop should catch (likely → codify 'stat grid = a shared BLEND component' in DESIGN.md next harvest)."* This is a subtle but real datum about the from-spec sweep: handed the same convention, eight independent agents produced eight *correct* but *inconsistent* implementations — the convergence loop reached the *what* (every grid is responsive and never clips) but not the *form* (one shared component vs. inlined utilities). The agents also self-caught some of their own clipping: the Simple Interest agent's *"first attempt at 9rem min clipped the total at scale; I caught it in the pixel review and fixed it to 11rem before committing."* **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — the self-install bet is a strong positive autonomy datum, and the implementation divergence is a precise instance of the evaluate→harvest loop producing its own next-round agenda mid-iteration.

## Chapter 88 — The visual gate catches two real chart bugs nothing else did — its strongest validation yet (2026-06-14)

When the operator asked whether to merge regens on CI-green alone — *"Should we start merging all of the ones that have CI green btw?"* — the assistant held a qualifier: these are **UI-surface** PRs, so they merge only on CI-green *and* a visual approval. That qualifier immediately earned its keep. The Amortization visual review **caught two genuine chart defects that every markup test and the agent's own local Mac screenshot had passed**: the principal-vs-interest **donut rendered blank** (Chart.js had been handed the wrapper `div`, not the `<canvas>`), and the balance curve's **x-axis showed "1970"** (a month index read as a UNIX timestamp). The assistant named it as the gate's strongest validation: *"the **Amortization review caught two *real* chart bugs** that every markup test + the agent's local Mac screenshot missed … Strong validation that the visual gate earns its keep — and exactly why I won't merge these UI regens on CI-green alone."*

The fix went back to the agent, which repaired both and — critically — added a **pixel-level "chart actually painted" test**: the donut must have more than 500 painted pixels, *"so a blank chart now fails CI."* This closes the structural hole the build exposed: a JS-painted `<canvas>` cannot be asserted by the markup-only integration suite, so a chart that silently fails to draw passes every test; the pixel-test makes "did it paint?" a mechanical CI gate rather than a human-eyeball check. The re-review confirmed both charts now paint correctly — donut with the largest slice green per §1, balance curve declining from ~$100k to $0 with a proper "Start … Yr 25" axis — and called it *"a clear upgrade over the prior CSS charts."* The pixel-test rule + the importmap chart-lib packaging gotcha were filed as deferred harvest issue **#144**. **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — the visual gate caught a class of bug (a chart that renders blank while passing every assertion) that no other gate in the pipeline could, and the fix was encoded as a mechanical pixel-test plus a filed convention, not a one-off patch.

## Chapter 89 — The ci-monitor / agent-death investigation, and the pivot to retries-only (2026-06-14)

The recurring "API Error: socket connection closed unexpectedly" deaths that had dogged the bookkeeping agents finally got a dedicated investigation, at the operator's request: *"I'd like to explore the actual ci-monitor failure, any idea what might be causing it? Quick debugging subagent to give us some ideas?"* The assistant offered a hypothesis (long-lived blocking connections) and dispatched a scoped read-only investigator — which **corrected the hypothesis with evidence.** The two genuine deaths in the session's transcripts were **not** ci-monitors at all but the **historian and PM** (both Opus writing agents), and they died *"within 0.3s of each other, mid model-inference, `error: 'unknown'`, `isApiErrorMessage: true`"* — two unrelated agents dying at the same wall-clock second being *"the fingerprint of an **upstream Anthropic-API/harness connection drop**, not anything `bin/ci-watch` does."* The investigator also found three of the "socket" sightings were **false positives** — the string appeared because the historian was reading transcripts that *quoted* earlier errors. The assistant owned the correction: *"My long-poll theory was wrong as a *cause* but right as *exposure*."* The structural lever the repo *does* control: `bin/ci-watch`'s `gh run watch` has no max timeout, so a `--poll` call blocks for the entire CI run, maximizing the window for a platform drop to land on the monitor silently. The analysis was filed as **#140**.

The fix's design then evolved through dialogue. The operator pushed on whether a script change was even needed: *"can't we just ask the ci-monitor to retry for inconclusive results? Does this have to be baked into the script itself, or can we ask the ci-monitor to do more due diligence? Or was this failure causing the failure to bubble up and abruptly end the agent run?"* The assistant separated the two failure modes precisely: the abrupt API death is *the agent's own brain connection dropping* — *"there's nothing for the agent to 'catch': it's not running anymore"* — recoverable only by external orchestrator re-dispatch; while inconclusive/PENDING results *can* be handled at the agent layer with no script edit, *"which fits 'fix the agent, not the code.'"* It first built a bounded-poll + retry version (`7ac919b`, PR #142). But the operator then redirected to a simpler shape — retries only, and *always* use the monitor: the assistant restated the goal as *"ci-monitor.md = **retries, NO bounded-poll/give-up**, and the main thread **always uses ci-monitor**"* (dropping the direct `gh pr checks` glances it had used as a stopgap during the deaths). Because the bounded-poll and retries were in the same file, the assistant **edited #142 directly** rather than split it: stripped the bounded-poll/deadline, kept the retries, restored watch-to-terminal, retitled the PR (`22478ad`). It merged at `849e176`, and since it's on `main`, *"every future `ci-monitor` dispatch uses it — 'always use ci-monitor' restored."* **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — a misattributed recurring failure was investigated to its real (platform) root cause, the repo-controllable exposure was identified, and the chosen fix lived entirely in the agent definition, refined twice by operator direction.

## Chapter 90 — Iteration-0005 called "a huge success," and the layer-scoped regen-only model validated (2026-06-14)

The operator reviewed the live regenerated app and rendered his verdict: *"I've already reviewed the web app and have no notes. I'm ready to call this iteration complete as a huge success given what we were looking for."* All 8 regens had merged green (the assistant taking clean statuses via direct `gh pr checks` glances when monitors died, then merging green+visually-approved ones — Tip and Ohms first as independent files, then Amortization after its chart fixes, then the remaining five with the expected serial `application.css` union-resolve when BMI's `.stat-grid` and Age's `.today-button` collided, resolved by an additive-union merge that kept both and held 100% coverage). The final Build state: main `250e9a4`, 8 calculators live with interactive Amortization charts, the Age Today-button, and responsive grids throughout.

The assistant framed what the iteration proved: *"a layer-scoped regen iteration that propagated a whole harvest (interactive charts, responsive grids, Today-button) across all 8 calculators from spec alone, *and* the visual gate caught real chart bugs that every other test missed. The experiment's loop worked end to end."* Per the codified lifecycle, the two substantive evaluate-phase findings that weren't landed this round were filed as deferred harvest issues rather than lost — **#143** (consolidate the stat-grid into one BLEND component, the convergence-on-form gap from Chapter 87) and **#144** (the chart pixel-test + importmap chart-lib packaging rule from Chapter 88) — while the one process harvest that *did* land was #142 (ci-monitor retries). The operator then set the close-out order: *"Once the background agent is done, let's historian and then iteration close and merge those in and end,"* and parked the next session's plan — *"When we come back next time, we'll start by going through and knocking out issues"* — asking for an effort/now-vs-later triage of the open backlog as the session's final act. **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — iteration-0005 closed having validated the regen-only, layer-scoped iteration shape end-to-end: a frontend harvest propagated across the whole catalog from spec, with the controlled visual gate catching real defects, and the substantive remainder filed agent-first for the next round.

## Chapter 91 — "What's next" becomes the backlog sweep: two warm-up issues fanned out as general-purpose agents (2026-06-14)

With iterations 0004 and 0005 closed and the open-issue backlog triaged into a Now/Later × effort table the previous session (the `docs/experiments/` snapshot merged at `1482a2c`), this session resumed on the plan that triage had set up: stop building new calculators for a round and **start knocking out the backlog**. The operator opened with two words — *"what's next"* — and the assistant picked up exactly where the triage left off: *"Last session (iters 0004 + 0005 done) you said next time we'd **start knocking out issues**. The triage is still accurate — nothing on it has been closed."*

The assistant laid out a sequenced plan rather than diving in. It nominated the two ⭐ (now + low-effort) rows as warm-ups: **#128** (rename `docs/inventory.md` → `docs/INVENTORY.md` and update its references — *"Pure mechanical rename"*) and **#144** (codify into the frontend agent definition the two chart lessons harvested from iteration-0005's visual gate — a chart *"did-it-paint"* pixel test and an importmap chart-lib packaging rule — *"Codifying a proven lesson into the agent"*). Behind them it sketched the Now/Med cluster that pairs up (#109+#110 on `Base` input-coercion validation; #87+#61 on the visual gate's screenshot non-determinism and the non-UI baseline source; #111 on the SimpleCov parallel-coverage undercount every build agent works around; #147 on historian efficiency as `JOURNEY.md` grows) and named the keystone deliberately to be scheduled, not rushed: **#107**, the frontend de-facto central-registration refactor — *"High effort, but it's the **keystone**: unblocks #122, #124, #143, #15."* Its recommendation was to clear the two warm-ups first *"since they're fast and #144 banks an agent improvement before any regen"* — i.e. land the agent-def fix while between iterations, so the next regen sweep inherits it.

The operator's response shaped the orchestration: *"Can 128 and 144 be sent off as subagents."* The assistant confirmed they parallelize cleanly — *"both are independent (separate files, separate PRs, each in its own worktree), so they parallelize cleanly"* — and, before dispatching, deliberately pulled up the dispatching-parallel-agents playbook to check the work against its when-to-use test (independent problem domains, no shared state). The two tasks pass it: #128 touches docs/contract references, #144 touches an agent definition; even if both happen to graze a shared file like `CLAUDE.md`, *"any conflict only surfaces at the second merge — manageable."*

The chapter's load-bearing orchestration nuance is **which** agents got dispatched. Neither task maps to the scoped `backend`, `frontend`, or `pm` agents — #128 is a cross-cutting rename spanning docs, contracts, and an agent definition's references, and #144 *edits* an agent definition, which is precisely the work the not-yet-built meta agent-authoring agent (#72) would eventually own. So the assistant reached for **general-purpose agents**: *"Both land in the agent/docs/contract layer (not calculator code), so neither maps to the scoped `backend`/`frontend`/`pm` agents — I'll dispatch two general-purpose agents, each pointed at `CLAUDE.md` as step 0 and each working in its own worktree → PR."* It fired both in parallel, each instructed to read `CLAUDE.md` first and create its own worktree (the standing default for any writing agent), and emitted the `📝 journaling that in the background` marker before continuing — practicing the background-historian convention as it went.

The post-dispatch summary stated the gate plan up front, before either PR existed, so nothing would slip past unmonitored: the moment each opens a PR, dispatch `ci-monitor`; then run the visual gate before any merge — and because both are docs/agent-def changes that can't move a pixel, both resolve through the **non-UI baseline-diff lane** (*"#128 is a docs/non-UI PR → baseline diff, confirm every shot `UNCHANGED`"*; *"#144 is an agent-def/non-UI PR → same baseline-diff check"*), with nothing merging without the operator's explicit go-ahead. The window closes with both agents dispatched and running, the historian dispatched alongside them, and the operator's next request already in — *"Show me all of the issues and their classifications in a table"* — pivoting from dispatch to a backlog overview (whose answer falls after this anchor). **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — the first move of the backlog-sweep phase routed two independent fixes through parallel dispatched agents in their own worktrees, with the deliberate choice of *general-purpose* agents over the scoped roster precisely because both tasks live in the agent/docs/contract layer that no specialized agent yet owns (the gap the deferred meta-agent #72 names).

## Chapter 92 — The two warm-ups land, and the non-UI visual gate is proven non-functional (2026-06-14)

The three non-UI PRs from the backlog sweep's opening move — #150 (#128 rename), #149 (#144 chart rules), and #151 (the historian's Chapter 91) — all returned CI-green, and the assistant moved each into its visual gate. Per the contract, none touches a rendered surface, so each resolves through the **non-UI baseline-diff lane**: pull a main-branch CI run's screenshots as the baseline and confirm every shot is `UNCHANGED` (sha256-equal), *proving* no pixel moved rather than asserting it from the file diff. The first diff broke that promise. Diffing PR #149 against the baseline, **30 of 41 shots reported `CHANGED`** — on a PR that touched only `.claude/agents/frontend.md` and `docs/DESIGN.md`, *"neither file is rendered by the app, so no pixel can have moved."*

The contract is explicit that a surprise `CHANGED` on a non-UI PR must not be hand-waved — *"stop and view it before merge."* The assistant honored that instruction's *intent* while recognizing its likely cause, and instead of eyeballing 30 shots it ran a controlled experiment to determine whether the churn was real or capture noise: *"let me prove it's capture noise rather than assert it: diff two different main CI runs (identical code) against each other. If main-vs-main shows comparable churn, the #149 diff is noise, not the PR."* The result was decisive. Two *different* `main` CI runs — byte-for-byte identical application code — differed on **32 of 41 shots**, *more* churn than the PR's 30. The assistant's read: *"Confirmed: it's #87, not the PR. Two different main CI runs — identical app code — differ on 32 of 41 shots. That's more churn than PR #149's 30. The CHANGED reports are pure screenshot-capture non-determinism; the sha256 baseline-diff can't tell a real change from capture noise, so the non-UI 'prove every shot UNCHANGED' gate is currently non-functional (exactly what #87 describes, and why #61 wants a defined baseline source)."*

This is a notable evidence chapter: a gate that the contract *mandates on every non-UI PR* is shown, with a clean controlled measurement, to be incapable of distinguishing a real pixel change from capture noise — *"Eyeballing the 30 would be theater — main-vs-main already proves they're byte-different-but-visually-identical noise."* What actually cleared the three PRs was the file-level evidence the assistant fell back on: a table showing each PR's changed files against the question *"Rendered surface?"* — all three answered "none" (zero `app/views`, `app/helpers`, `app/assets`, `app/javascript`, layout, or calculator-math changes), so no pixel *could* have moved and the false positives were confirmed noise. The operator's instruction was terse and three-part — *"merge all 3 + document 87 evidence then move on to 107"* — and the assistant executed it: merged #150, #149, #151 sequentially (no file overlap between them) via `bin/merge-cleanup`, posted the `9 unchanged / 32 changed` main-vs-main numbers as an evidence comment on **#87**, and confirmed `main` fast-forwarded to `f405f63` with #128 and #144 auto-closed and all worktrees pruned. **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — the warm-ups landed exactly as filed (a clean rename with history preserved, two harvested chart lessons codified into the frontend agent), and the side-effect was a concrete, reproducible data point feeding the #87/#61 fix: the non-UI gate's "prove UNCHANGED" mechanism is, today, defeated by screenshot non-determinism.

## Chapter 93 — The keystone reframed: #107 is an architecture change, not a bug (2026-06-14)

With the warm-ups clearing, the operator turned to the keystone — *"Let's talk about the keystone - what's the lift on this bug."* Before estimating, the assistant read the three shared files #107 names (the `_result.html.erb` dispatch, `calculators_helper.rb`, and the home-page cards) plus the backend's discovery machinery, then delivered a reframing that reshaped the whole effort: **#107 is filed under engineering but it is not a bug.** *"There's no defect to fix; the FE just never got the convention-based discovery the backend has. That reframes the lift: the durable deliverable is a convention + agent-definition update, not a one-off refactor — otherwise the next regen erases it (rule #1)."* This is the experiment's first rule applied as a *design constraint on the fix itself*: because the regeneration sweep erases anything living only in calculator code, the keystone's deliverable has to be pushed up into the convention/agent layer or it won't survive a single round.

The assistant then mapped the three shared files into very different sizes. The **result dispatch** is *"trivial, FE-only, low risk"* — the `is_a?` chain already just renders a slug-named partial in every branch, so it collapses to one convention-based render with a generic fallback. The **helper split** is *"the real work, FE-only but touchy"* — `calculators_helper.rb` is a ~300-line monolith every frontend build appends to (not just the `field_labels_for` case but per-calc constants and methods), coverage-gated to 100%, so the honest fix is splitting it into per-calculator helper modules with a discovery convention. The **catalog** is *"medium-high, and it crosses the agent boundary"* — it needs an autoload-aware enumeration on `Base` (today nothing *lists* calculators; `lookup(slug)` only resolves a known one) plus per-calculator display metadata that lives nowhere yet. The assistant surfaced the one genuine fork early and refused to start coding before it was settled: *"The one genuine fork in the road is where catalog/calculator metadata lives — that decision shapes both the backend PR and whether #124 comes nearly for free."* It staged the lift as three PRs (backend enumeration+metadata, frontend dispatch+catalog+helper-split, agent-def harvest) plus a regen sweep, and earned the keystone label by naming what it directly unblocks: #122 (catalog UI), #124 (info modal), #143 (stat-grid), #15 (CSS collision). **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — the keystone was correctly re-classified from "bug" to "bring the no-central-registration convention to the client side," which made the deliverable an agent/convention change by construction rather than a refactor the next sweep would erase.

## Chapter 94 — The operator overrules the agent: a DB-backed registry, deliberately, against the assistant's recommendation and rule #3 (2026-06-14)

This is one of the experiment's pivotal moments — the operator overriding both the agent's architectural recommendation *and* one of the repo's own load-bearing rules, on purpose. The fork the assistant had flagged ("where does calculator metadata live?") drew the operator straight to the substrate question: *"Is this essentially requesting the need for a DB table that tracks every type of calculator? If that is one of the best foundational options, I think we should move forward in creating that registry."* He sketched the mechanism himself — a migration that migrates all calculators into the DB in one shot, plus *"a small playbook on how to idempotently re-insert (shape of the inserted data, queries executed)."*

The assistant pushed back, hard and on principle rather than just building it. Its recommendation was a **code registry, not a table**, and it grounded the objection in the repo's rules: *"A DB table that tracks every calculator type is a central registry — and rule #3 is 'no central registration — add a calculator by adding a file; never edit a shared registry/route/table.'"* It argued a seed/migration insert would become *"the new merge-conflict point — which is the precise problem #107 exists to eliminate. You'd move the collision from three ERB files to one seed file. Net zero, arguably worse,"* and that it would couple the pure math layer (rule #5: no DB) to data that is *"100% derivable from code."* It proposed instead an autoload-aware class registry — each calculator self-declaring `title`/`category`/`blurb` in its own file, with `Base.all` globbing `app/calculators/*.rb` — and showed how the operator's "migration + idempotent re-insert" instinct maps onto it *for free*: *"recomputed every boot from the source of truth, so it's idempotent by construction — zero drift, no seed to keep in sync."* It conceded a table would be justified later for analytics joins or an admin-editable catalog, but *"even then you'd sync it from Base.all, not hand-maintain it."*

The operator heard the argument and overruled it with a single load-bearing sentence: *"I get why you're pushing for a code registry, but that's just tons of hard coded data about the most important component of the entire app."* He chose the DB deliberately — *"Let's go with all of these living in a DB"* — and specified the operating model: a migration that creates the tables *and* executes a newly created rake task which idempotently ingests the data, refreshed via CLI on demand, with the **database-agent owning that capability** (*"when we ask for this, kick db agent which knows how to do the steps"*). His framing of the assistant's own pre-work: *"converting all inventory into a deterministic shape that gets ingested by the rake task, maybe the output here is a CSV."* He also bounded the blast radius — *"I don't think there's anything that we can or should update right now based on this … We still need UI's for each of these individually in code"* — and, notably, asked the assistant to *stress-test his own decision* rather than just execute it: *"do an investigation on what else should change given this data … And yes evaluate my flows in ingest. and confirm once more of course that this solution works."*

The assistant accepted the reversal cleanly and without litigating it further: *"Decision heard — DB-backed registry, database-agent owns the refresh capability. Your call, and it's a deliberate evolution of the architecture, not an accident."* **Agent/doc-driven? — a deliberate human override.** This chapter is the experiment recording its own boundary: the agent argued the architecture-purist position (and rule #3) faithfully, the human weighed it and chose the opposite for a reason the agent had not weighted as heavily — that the catalog is *the* central component and burying its data in hand-coded class methods is itself the wrong kind of "registration." The override is the point of the entry, captured verbatim and unjudged.

## Chapter 95 — The investigation that validated the operator's flow — and caught a CI-fatal bug in it (2026-06-14)

Asked to confirm his solution works, the assistant did the investigation grounded in the actual repo and returned a verdict that was neither rubber-stamp nor reversal: *"your flow works, with two corrections that are non-negotiable for CI."* The headline correction was a real bug in the operator's flow as drawn — and it turned on a detail of the repo's own configuration. The repo uses `schema_format = :sql` (`db/structure.sql`), and CI builds the test DB with `bin/rails db:test:prepare`, which *"loads structure.sql directly and runs neither migrations nor seeds."* So the operator's "migration executes the rake task" step would be **skipped in every test run**: *"A migration that runs the ingest → skipped in test/CI … The table would exist but be empty in every test run → any test reading the registry fails, and the 100% coverage gate can't pass."* The fix: the **migration creates the table only** (that's what lands in `structure.sql`); the **ingest is a standalone idempotent rake task** — *"exactly as you want for CLI refresh — just not invoked from the migration."* The assistant laid out the four-substrate resolution: fresh dev/prod DBs auto-populate via a `db/seeds.rb` hook (the existing `RoleType` pattern), CLI refresh via `bin/rails calculators:ingest`, and **tests get `test/fixtures/calculators.yml`** because `db:test:prepare` skips seeds too.

The investigation then answered the source-of-truth question the operator had left open as a CSV idea. The assistant flagged that *"a single committed CSV is a shared-edit file"* — the very conflict #107 exists to kill, relocated from three ERB files to one CSV — and offered the alternative it leaned toward: **(B) the ingest reads `docs/INVENTORY.md` directly**, because the PM already maintains that catalog idempotently and new calculator builds never touch it, so *"the registry source stays conflict-free, and there's no second place to keep in sync"* — at the cost of a missing blurb column and more brittle markdown parsing. It also named a correctness property unique to the DB substrate that the code registry never had: **the DB can drift from reality.** A row can claim a calculator the code doesn't have (a 404 link) or miss one that exists (invisible). So the ingest must **reconcile against the code** — *"The code (Base.lookup(slug)) is authoritative for 'is this actually built/routable'"* — validating each slug resolves to a class and, per rule #4's spirit, flagging vanished rows **deprecated, not deleted.**

Finally the boundary cost. The assistant surfaced that the operator's "db-agent owns this" collides with the agent's current definition: *"database-agent is hard read-only — its def says 'Read-only, full stop… never INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE… never a migration… never app code,' and it only has Bash+Read tools (no Write). It literally cannot author the CSV/migration/rake task, and running the ingest is a mutation it's currently forbidden from."* The clean split: **backend builds** the machinery (migration, `Calculator` model, the ingest **lib service** + thin rake task so the logic is unit-testable to 100%, fixtures — all its existing turf), and **database-agent operates** it (runs the ingest on request, reports before/after counts + drift), which requires *editing its definition to carve out one sanctioned mutation while keeping "no ad-hoc writes."* The assistant also enumerated the wider ripple — **CLAUDE.md rule #3 has to be amended** (*"We're deliberately creating one. The contract has to be amended to describe the new model honestly, or it contradicts itself"*), `calculations.calculator` becomes a future soft join (no hard FK — append-only calcs outlive deprecated calculators), and **routing needs no change** (already dynamic; a registry guard would make the DB authoritative over routability, bad given drift). The two calls it sent back: registry source A vs B, and whether to redefine db-agent. **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — the agent stress-tested the human's directive and found a genuine CI-fatal defect (migration-driven ingest skipped by `structure.sql` loading) plus the drift-reconciliation requirement, validating the decision while materially correcting *how* it gets built — the override and the audit together producing a design neither party held at the start.

## Chapter 96 — The contract made honest: rule #3 amended for the derived registry (2026-06-14)

The operator answered both open calls and pointed at the next move: *"B / Update CLAUDE.md / What's next."* The assistant took the registry source as `docs/INVENTORY.md` read directly (*"conflict-free, no new hand-maintained file"*) and amended the contract — on a branch, because main is protected — to make rule #3 stop contradicting the deliberate decision just made. The change reworked rule #3 from a flat *"no central registration"* to *"no hand-edited central registration"* (a *derived* registry is fine) and added a new **"The calculator registry"** section describing the derived model: a `calculators` table ingested idempotently from INVENTORY.md, reconciled against `Base.lookup`, refreshed by a CLI rake task, with deprecate-never-delete, the fixtures-for-tests / seeds-for-fresh substrate split (because `db:test:prepare` loads `structure.sql` and runs neither migrations nor seeds), and the backend-builds / db-agent-operates ownership split. It landed as **PR #152**, CI-green on the first poll, and the assistant cleared its visual gate on the now-established basis — *"It touches only CLAUDE.md (zero rendered surface) … the #87 baseline noise is already confirmed, no need to re-run it"* (Chapter 92's finding now operating as standing precedent rather than a per-PR re-derivation).

With the contract green, the assistant laid out the build sequence, naming the one genuine gate up front: *"Merge #152 — everything below builds to this contract, and the build agents read CLAUDE.md as step 0, so they need the merged version. This is the one genuine gate — I can't usefully background the build before it lands."* The sequence behind it: (1) a precursor — the **PM adds a `Description` column to INVENTORY.md** so the card blurb stays in the single source rather than reintroducing an FE-side metadata home; (2) **backend builds the machinery** (table-only migration, `Calculator` model, ingest lib service + `calculators:ingest` rake task, seeds hook, fixtures, 100% tests); (3) the **database-agent definition is extended** from pure-inspector to operate the one sanctioned ingest; (4) the **actual #107 FE work** (catalog iterates the registry, convention dispatch, helper split). It also proposed filing a **foundational registry issue** that #107/#122/#124 depend on, to keep the kanban honest about the dependency.

The operator approved the slate point by point — *"0 - yes / 1 - Yep, PM can add description, let's keep it all in DB"* (blurb in the DB, sourced from the new INVENTORY column) — and *"2, file foundatioal registry issue whenever."* The assistant merged #152 (`main` at `e764988`, contract live), then dispatched the **PM** (owns Issues + `docs/`) for the two unblocked items — file the foundational registry issue linking #107/#122/#124, and add the `Description` column for the 8 built calculators — and fired the **historian** in the background. It noted a small self-correction in passing — *"correcting an earlier marker where I flagged it but hadn't dispatched"* — having emitted the `📝 journaling` marker once before the dispatch actually went out. The window closes with the contract merged, the PM and historian backgrounded, and the build queued in strict dependency order: PM's `Description` PR → backend machinery → db-agent def update → #107 FE — *"backend waits on the Description PR so it builds against the real INVENTORY.md shape."* **Agent/doc-driven? ✅** — the architecture change began by amending the shared contract every agent inherits *before* any machinery was built, so the deliberate departure from rule #3 is recorded honestly in the contract itself rather than living as an undocumented exception, and the build was sequenced so each agent reads the corrected contract as its first action.

---

## Running assessment — "Does this work?"

*Evidence to date; updated as the experiment proceeds. Deliberately unopinionated — observations, not verdicts.*

- **Every product and process decision so far landed in an agent definition or a doc, never in hand-written app code.** The two hand-edits (CI config, a `.keep` file) were infra, not the product. The experiment's discipline has held.
- **The clearest single data point:** when the user changed their mind about issue-seeding, the fix was a six-line edit to `pm.md`, committed as `feat(pm):`. Changing behavior meant changing the agent. That is the loop working.
- **The method produced a layered structure quickly** — three agents (`pm`, `ci-monitor`, `historian`), two north-star docs, a conventions doc, a tracking spine, and green CI — *before a single calculator was built.* This is the "heavy upfront investment" the user anticipated; whether it pays off is the open question the later chapters will answer.
- **A new kind of data point appeared in Chapter 10:** the decision about whether the historian should exist was reversed *four times* before settling, and every reversal happened in dialogue and resolved into an agent definition + a `CLAUDE.md` convention — not into code. The "describe my work with great intent" challenge here took the form of the user repeatedly catching that *the premise had changed underneath the previous conclusion*, with the assistant re-deriving rather than flip-flopping silently. The historian then journaled its own contentious origin as its first task — the loop closing on itself.
- **One verification habit is visible and held:** before committing to the transcript-reading design, the assistant *checked that the transcript was actually readable and parseable* (size, structure, real text) rather than assuming. Design feasibility was confirmed empirically. The habit recurred in Chapter 12: asked about durability, the assistant pulled the transcript's actual byte/line count, timestamp span, and grepped for the user's literal first message before claiming the record was intact — *"evidence, not reassurance."*
- **The journaling discipline is showing self-restraint, not just activity (Chapter 11):** the assistant *deliberately declined* to fire the historian for a minor checklist addition, judging it below the "decision/pivot" bar and choosing to keep the journaling signal-to-noise high. The system is being used with judgment about *when not* to use it — and that minor change was correctly swept into the next checkpoint anyway.
- **A second durable artifact now backs the synthesis (Chapter 12):** the raw, verbatim transcripts (main + every subagent) are snapshotted to `conversations/` on every historian run. The experiment now keeps *both* the lossy synthesized narrative and the complete replayable record — and the historian's "does this work?" account can be audited against the raw source at any time.
- **The first real product code was built — and it was agent-built, per the docs (Chapters 13–17).** The app foundation (auth, RBAC, `Discardable`, `Calculation` + `calculation_logs` view, CLI tasks, the 100% coverage gate) was produced by **five subagents**, each instructed only to read `ARCHITECTURE.md` + `CLAUDE.md` first — none saw the conversation. The conventions docs were sufficient to carry the user's intent into code. This is the strongest evidence yet that the method *produces working software*, not just documents: the build is green, 100% line+branch covered, and **ran live end-to-end** (anonymous → "not signed in"; login as `demo` → "Signed in as demo"; bad password → flash). Notably, no app code had ever run before this point — the foundation is the first executable product.
- **The review loop caught a real bug, and it was fixed through the process (Chapter 14).** The code-quality reviewer found that failed logins set a flash that *nothing rendered* — a genuine silent-rejection bug. The fix went through a subagent, not a manual patch, and was then **verified live** (the "Invalid username or password" flash appearing on the running server is that exact fix). The two-stage review + judgment-over-agreement discipline (defer-with-reason on three minor findings; re-scope the cookie-`secure` finding while still adopting it) held across both the per-task review and the final holistic review.
- **The conventions doc paid off exactly where predicted.** Two earlier decisions proved load-bearing under the first build: the SQL schema format (Chapter 9) was *needed* for the `calculation_logs` **view** to round-trip through `structure.sql`; and `ARCHITECTURE.md` was complete enough that the foundation went *straight to a plan with no redesign* — the assistant explicitly noted *"we don't need a new brainstorm."* The heavy upfront investment began to discharge here.
- **The full intended workflow was dogfooded once, end-to-end (Chapters 13–17):** PM-minted issue (#4) → branch → subagent-driven build with two-stage review → PR (#5, `Closes #4`) → `ci-monitor` green verdict → human-gated merge (left unmerged, awaiting say-so). A cross-project convention (tenor's `Issue → Branch → PR → Merge → Cleanup` flow) was reviewed, *adapted* rather than copied, and codified into `CLAUDE.md` — keeping the project's intentional differences (label taxonomy, the `Claude Fable 5` trailer).
- **The cost is real and visible, but is beginning to convert:** the dialogue-and-docs investment now has its first tangible return — a running, tested foundation other agents can build *on*. The bet (reusable infrastructure making the next 191 calculators cheap) is still mostly unproven, but no longer at zero user-visible output.
- **A recurring pattern:** the user repeatedly caught structural improvements (Issues, ARCHITECTURE, PRODUCT.md, CLAUDE inheritance, the historian, the branching strategy, the app-startup convention) that the assistant then designed. The "describe my work with great intent" challenge is being met largely through this catch-and-refine rhythm — and in Chapter 17 the assistant *corrected* one of the user's instincts with evidence (no foreman; `bin/dev` + `db:prepare`).
