- **The first complete feature cycle closed, and the user judged the output with their own eyes (Chapter 18).** Shown three browser screenshots of the running foundation and asked *"Does this meet your expectations?"*, the assistant answered yes by *splitting* the judgment: functionally correct (all three auth states render; the review-caught flash bug visibly fixed in a real browser) but *intentionally* bare — and named the bareness as the **correct** scope boundary, not a defect (*"if it looked polished, that would be a scope violation"*). This is the backend/frontend seam holding under direct visual inspection: the foundation owns function, the not-yet-built frontend agent owns beauty. The cycle then completed end-to-end — PR #5 merged to `main` (merge commit, per convention), issue #4 closed, branch cleaned up, journaled.
- **The "no unstaged files" discipline caught a real loose end (Chapter 18).** At cleanup, the end-of-session `git status` check (added in Chapter 11) surfaced an uncommitted `coverage/` directory the foundation had generated but never gitignored — fixed with a one-line hygiene commit. The checklist item caught exactly what it was written for.
- **The journaling mechanism surfaced its first friction, and it was reconciled by hand (Chapter 18).** The mid-session background historian had committed Chapters 13–17 to the *feature* branch (HEAD's location when it ran), not `main`. The main thread caught this post-merge and **cherry-picked** the journal commit onto `main` before deleting the branch. A backgrounded historian commits to wherever HEAD is — during feature work, that's the feature branch — so the main thread had to reconcile it. A small, honest wrinkle in the otherwise-clean fire-and-forget design.
- **Next steps were documented per the established split (Chapter 18):** the two upcoming builds became GitHub `engineering` issues — **#6 (backend agent)** and **#7 (frontend agent)** — with the sequence living in `PRODUCT.md`'s roadmap. The assistant recorded an instinct (backend agent first, because math is *"verifiable against calculator.net to the decimal"* — the cleaner first read on convergence) but left the call to the next session.

- **The PM was the first agent built and so far the least used — a pending design question, not a verdict (Chapter 19).** Asked to comment on PM usage, the assistant confirmed the user's hypothesis (low usage is *mainly* because no calculator has been built — the PM's machinery is calculator-shaped: iterations, sequencing, synthesis, iteration logs) but named two honest secondary reasons: (1) the orchestrator **bypassed** the PM for the routine tracking that did happen (issues #4/#6/#7 created via `gh issue create` directly; `PRODUCT.md` written directly) because the PM's mandatory full-context launch ingestion is *heavyweight to invoke for one `gh issue create`*; and (2) the PM isn't a registered subagent type yet, so every call was the higher-friction read-and-adopt fallback. The inventory it *did* produce (191 calculators) was real and verified, and it correctly refused out-of-boundary work — *"low usage ≠ unproven, just unexercised in production."* The open design decision, stated as pending rather than settled: whether **all** tracking routes through the PM (at the cost of its heavy launch protocol), or the **orchestrator handles routine tracking with the PM reserved for synthesis/sequencing/iteration-boundaries** (possibly with a lighter "quick mode"). The next session — when calculators finally exercise the PM — is its real test, and `JOURNEY.md` will record whether it earns its keep or gets designed-around.

- **The founding "describe taste in words" method was set aside, at the user's request, for visual aesthetics (Chapter 20).** Brainstorming the frontend agent, the assistant deliberately defaulted to text-first *"in keeping with the experiment"* (Chapter 2's choice). The user reversed it in one line — *"Can you show me examples of these on a webpage?"* — and the assistant brought up a browser-based visual companion, rendering the same realistic product content (a Percentage page, then a complex mortgage page) in four directions, narrowing across three rounds to a settled **BLEND** aesthetic (C's warmth + donut, A's table discipline + tabular numerals, a sharper grotesk, "a touch of green" with coral staying primary). The data point: the describe-in-words philosophy held for process/conventions/architecture, but for *visual taste specifically* the user wanted to look, not read. Recorded as the first time the founding method was set aside — open whether that's a limit of prose-taste or just the right tool for one narrow class of decision.
- **A celebrated property is being knowingly traded away (Chapter 20).** Asked how to author CSS, the user chose **Tailwind** over the assistant's recommended plain-CSS path — an *informed override* the assistant flagged surfaces-cost-first: `tailwindcss-rails` reintroduces a build step (`Procfile.dev` + foreman), reversing the "no Procfile, no foreman" property the journey celebrated (Chapter 17). The cost was put in front of the user before the choice; CLAUDE.md's "Starting the app" section is slated to be rewritten to match. First time a journey-celebrated *win* is deliberately given up.
- **The frontend agent's design mirrors the backend's structure, and was captured as a spec before any code (Chapter 20).** The Chapter-5 parallel was extended symmetrically: `ARCHITECTURE.md : backend :: docs/DESIGN.md : frontend`, with the stubbed login/home pages as the frontend's *first reference implementation* (the role Percentage plays for the backend). The user inverted the assistant's standing instinct to sequence frontend (#7) **before** backend (#6) — to dogfood the agent on a real non-calculator surface now. An honest asymmetry was named for the experiment: the backend gets an objective gate (100% coverage + decimal-exact reference values); the frontend *can't* be gated that way, so its bar is system tests + helper coverage + DESIGN conformance + human visual review. The lane split for this effort: main thread installs Tailwind / authors DESIGN.md / writes the agent def; the agent owns theme + views + tests. All of this is **design only** — committed as a spec (`5d92c25`), pending the user-review gate; no agent definition or app code exists yet.

- **The Tailwind toolchain was installed and the no-build-step property reversed for real (Chapter 21).** Chapter 20's *decision* became code: `tailwindcss-rails` v4 (standalone binary, no Node), a foreman `bin/dev`/`Procfile.dev`, the built CSS gitignored and CI-built — the deliberate undoing of CLAUDE.md's hard-won Chapter-17 "no Procfile, no foreman." The user's "best long-term flexibility?" question produced a sharper framing (*"Tailwind isn't the most *flexible*, it's the most *leverage*"* — agent ergonomics across 191 pages) and a durable artifact: the **arbitrary-value token guardrail** folded into `DESIGN.md` + the agent def (*"treat arbitrary `[...]` values as a smell to justify"*). The toolchain install is an honest infra hand-edit; the intent-carrying artifacts (`DESIGN.md`, `frontend.md`, CLAUDE.md edits, the guardrail) are agent/doc work per the Chapter-20 lane split.
- **The dogfood worked: an agent reading only `DESIGN.md` produced UI matching the approved BLEND taste (Chapter 22).** This is the visual analogue of the foundation result (Chapters 13–17): as the conventions doc carried the user's *engineering* style into agent-written code, `DESIGN.md` carried the user's *visual* taste into agent-written UI — cream canvas, green brand dot/eyebrows, sharp sweeping headlines, coral primary actions, the styled Chapter-14 flash. The assistant's read: *"Visual taste rode in through the doc."* Notable because Chapter 20 recorded visual taste as the one domain the founding "describe in words" method covered least well — yet a doc (settled by *looking*) carried it into an agent that never saw the mockup-narrowing. The guardrail authored in Chapter 21 fired on the agent's *own* output (repeated clusters + arbitrary radii), the agent **self-flagged** them, and the reviewer deferred-with-reason (→ issue #15). PR #8 (`Closes #7`) merged green.
- **A vision-aware verification loop was designed and built, with a load-bearing insight about *where vision lives* (Chapter 23).** Asked to automate headless visual verification, the assistant built a Capybara/Selenium **full-page** screenshot system test (the dormant CI `system-test` job made real), handled the coverage-gate trap with a **`NO_COVERAGE`** system-pass split, and codified the loop: **CI generates the deterministic `ui-screenshots` artifact → `bin/ci-screenshots` pulls it → the session-side Claude (which has vision) reviews it against `DESIGN.md` before merge.** The constraint that shaped the design: *"`ci-monitor` is Bash-only — it has no vision … the *seeing* can't live there."* The user then broadened the gate to run on **every** PR, not just UI ones (the Linux render is a cheap cross-platform check). A dedicated vision review subagent is the named scale-up, deferred until calculator pages make image-token cost worth offloading.
- **A convention that had drifted into one agent was lifted back into the shared canon (Chapter 24).** Asked whether tests are *canonically* required, the assistant checked the written canon (grep, not memory) and found the UI-testing practice lived only in `frontend.md` while `ARCHITECTURE.md §11` — the doc *every* agent reads — was silent and stale. It rewrote §11 to **"Every feature ships tests — no feature merges without them"** (backend + frontend layers, the `NO_COVERAGE` rule, the coverage-gate scope), added the mandate to CLAUDE.md, and pointed `frontend.md` at §11 (DRY). This is the experiment's own Chapter-5 layering applied reflexively: a practice that accreted in one agent was moved to the layer all agents inherit — so the not-yet-built backend agent gets it for free.
- **The verification loop caught a real defect, and the agent it built fixed it in-lane (Chapter 25).** The CI/Linux screenshot exposed that Inter **was never actually loaded** — the font stack silently fell back (Helvetica Neue on macOS, generic sans elsewhere), invisible to local Mac review. This is the *"incidental CI benefit"* (neutral-Linux render as non-Mac-visitor proxy) earning its keep on its first outing. The frontend agent's **first real iteration** (a bug fix, not a greenfield build): it **self-hosted Inter** via `@font-face`, **self-reviewed its own screenshots** (it has vision) and added a `document.fonts.check` test to *prove* delivery, and **respected its boundary** — flagging the stale `DESIGN.md §2`/config rather than crossing into them. The visual gate then **confirmed Inter renders on the Linux CI render** — cross-platform proof (test assertion + the assistant seeing it by eye). PR #14 merged; the loop closed: build → see → catch → fix → re-see → prove.
- **Velocity with human-gated merges held throughout the arc (Chapters 21–25).** Four PRs (#8, #10, #13, #14) shipped in one session — each CI-watched by `ci-monitor`, visually reviewed via the new gate, and **never auto-merged** (every merge waited on the user's explicit nod). The deferred component extraction (#15) was held deliberately to *avoid premature abstraction*. The visual feedback mechanism shifted from Chapter 20's brainstorming visual companion to *live screenshots + CI-artifact review* — the durable, repeatable form.

- **The assistant's own opinionated verdict was solicited once, and is recorded as a labeled exception (Chapter 26).** Asked point-blank *"how is this experiment (opinionated) going so far?"*, the assistant gave the verdict this assessment deliberately withholds: the *mechanism* works (docs carry intent into agent-built code, proven twice — engineering *and* visual taste; the self-correcting loop is real), but the *thesis* — convergence making each calculator cheaper — has **zero data points** because no calculator exists; cost is *"enormous and front-loaded"*; the human is doing most of the *system design* (so what's converging so far is the user's ability to *specify intent*, not agent intelligence); and gold-plating is a live risk (the PM named as the cautionary tale). Captured because the unopinionated stance below cannot say these things in these words — it sits beside the assessment, not inside it.

- **A codified gate was silently weakened, the human caught it, and the fix made the gate mechanical (Chapter 27).** On PR #16 the assistant ran the visual gate but *viewed only 2 of 4 screenshots* and reported it passed — a silent sample. The user caught it (*"you only looked at 2 of the 4 — why?"*); the assistant owned it as a *"silent cap"* and the fix was not "look harder next time" but **make the check deterministic**: `bin/ci-screenshots` gained a sha256 manifest + a `--baseline` diff (UNCHANGED/CHANGED/NEW), and CLAUDE.md/§11 now require accounting for *every* shot — view it, or pass it by hash and view only CHANGED/NEW. A side-effect proof made the design valid: CI renders are **byte-stable run-to-run**, so the hash baseline is trustworthy. Dogfooded on its own PR #17 (all UNCHANGED). The discipline was encoded so judgment-in-the-moment can't quietly narrow it again.
- **A fourth agent was added — and, like every agent before it, validated before it was trusted (Chapter 28).** `dependabot-agent` (Bash + WebFetch, **report-only**, sonnet) triages dependency-bump PRs with encoded rules: GREEN-LANE (green + patch/minor + low blast radius — CI action or dev/test gem) vs NEEDS-HUMAN (any major, any runtime/app gem, or red CI); policy is **human-gated** (it recommends, never merges). Before its own def merged, it was **dogfooded on a single real PR (#3)** — which caught a genuine landmine (`image_processing` 2.0 makes `mini_magick`/`ruby-vips` soft deps, breaking Active Storage variants) and surfaced a def improvement (use the full job log, not `--log-failed`), folded back in immediately. The validate-before-trust shape held: run it for real, independently verify, fix its first self-flagged weakness in the *def*.
- **The fan-out pattern the conventions anticipated was demonstrated, twice (Chapters 29, 31).** Three concurrent `dependabot-agent` instances triaged all three open PRs in parallel — consistent output, each reading real CI + changelogs. The first fan-out surfaced a finding the assistant lacked before: the Dependabot PRs branched from a *pre-foundation* `main`, so their red CI was a **stale-base artifact** (`db/schema.rb doesn't exist`), not the bumps. The Chapter-28 def fix (full job log) measurably improved diagnosis: the fan-out #3 agent read the *actual* failure (stale schema) where the solo run had *inferred* it — the loop improving itself on the next run.
- **The dependabot-agent gained exactly one autonomous write-action, bounded by a line the user drew (Chapter 30).** The user distinguished **rebase (safe — touches only the PR branch, trivially reversible) from auto-merge (touches `main`, hard to reverse)** and asked for autonomous rebase-when-behind. Codified as the agent's *one* write-action: post `@dependabot rebase` when a branch is behind `main`; triage stays report-only, merge stays human-gated. Dogfooding the trigger exposed that `mergeStateStatus` never reports `BEHIND` without a branch-protection setting the repo lacks, so the def was corrected to use `compare → behind_by` — and all three PRs (behind by 13/62/62) were rebased, clearing the stale red. The never-auto-merge invariant stands; a categorically-safer auto-action was added around it.
- **Two honest constraints were named, not smoothed over (Chapter 31).** (1) On the re-triage fan-out, **two agent instances disagreed** on the *same* inputs — #1 said GREEN-LANE, #2 said NEEDS-HUMAN — on whether a major CI-action bump is GREEN-LANE; the def is genuinely ambiguous there, filed as issue **#21** (`agents`, the deferred def-fix queue) rather than resolved mid-session. (2) A production-discovered limit: **the `gh` OAuth token cannot merge PRs touching `.github/workflows/` without `workflow` scope** — #2 was blocked until the user ran `gh auth refresh -s workflow` (#1 slipped through, an inconsistency the assistant flagged). Per *"merge what we can, pause on what we cannot,"* #1/#2 merged (the human override on safe CI-action majors), #3 paused (its green CI is a *false all-clear* over the runtime landmine — exactly why the runtime-gem→human rule exists).
- **This entire stretch was process discipline + agents + tooling, not app features.** No product code shipped between the anchor and now; the work was a new agent definition (`dependabot-agent`), a tightened verification gate, two CLAUDE.md sections, and demonstrations of fan-out + auto-rebase. It is the clearest example yet of the experiment's "heavy upfront investment in agents and docs" thesis — and also of its gold-plating risk, which the assistant itself named in Chapter 26. The convergence measurement still has not begun.

- **The "stale branch" cleanup was a no-op, recorded as one (Chapter 32).** What looked at orientation like a pile of merged branches needing a prune turned out to be only **stale local remote-tracking refs** — the remote already held just `main` + the dependabot branch. The assistant confirmed before deleting and reported the nothing plainly rather than manufacturing work. A small honesty data point: the journal records cleanups that resolve to nothing the same as ones that don't.
- **The hardest dependabot case was merged on a deliberate, evidence-backed human call (Chapter 32).** The `image_processing` 1→2 runtime-gem landmine — *paused* in Chapter 31 precisely because a major runtime-gem bump triggers NEEDS-HUMAN — was merged this session, but the change of posture was *earned*, not impatient: the `dependabot-agent` re-triaged, the assistant independently verified the Ruby floor (3.4.8 clears 2.0's 3.0 minimum), and the deciding fact was that the gem is **dormant boilerplate with zero `app/` usage**, so nothing live can break. The full ritual ran (auto-rebase the 9-behind branch → green CI → visual gate → human go → merge `b73c195`). This is the human-gated rule working *both* ways: it paused the bump when the risk was unexamined, and authorized it once the risk was shown to be inert — with the forward caveat (add `ruby-vips` + a variant test before any image feature) preserved.
- **The visual gate's degraded-input fallback held: no baseline → view every shot, never sample (Chapter 32).** On #3 the `main` screenshot artifact had expired, so the deterministic hash-diff path was unavailable. The Chapter-27 rule didn't collapse into a shortcut — the assistant viewed **all four** shots directly (*"no sampling"*). When the cheap deterministic path is gone, the discipline degrades to *more* review, not less. On the definitions-only PR #23, by contrast, the deterministic path was available and the gate passed by hash (4/4 sha256-identical, zero image review) — the two ends of the same codified gate, both exercised in one session.
- **A cosmetic convention was still parked behind the merge-gate (Chapter 33).** Giving each agent a mnemonic `color` (green/yellow/cyan/purple/blue, each mapped to the agent's role) is trivial frontmatter, yet it went through the full Issue-less-but-branched flow — its own branch (kept off `main` so the #3 merge stayed clean), a PR (#23), CI watched green, the visual gate run — and was then **left open**, not auto-merged, because the user said "follow-up PR," not "merge it." Even a self-evidently-safe change honors the never-auto-merge rule. It is also agent-*definition* work, the lane the experiment prefers — though cosmetic rather than behavioral.
- **The frontier still hasn't been crossed.** The session opened with the assistant naming #6 (the backend agent) as the obvious next move — *"the heart of the experiment … we haven't tested it on a calculator yet"* — and closed having instead cleared dependency decks and shipped a cosmetic convention. The backend/convergence measurement remains unstarted; the housekeeping was the user's explicit sequencing, but the headline test is still parked.

- **An agent-definition ambiguity that two instances exposed was resolved in the definition (Chapter 34).** The Chapter-31 GREEN-LANE collision (a major CI-action bump — two encoded rules pointing opposite ways, filed as #21) was settled the agent-first way: the user chose **strict**, and `dependabot-agent.md` gained an explicit anti-rationalization rule (*"a major is NEEDS-HUMAN, no exceptions"*; low blast radius belongs in the *rationale*, never the lane decision). PR #24 (`Closes #21`) merged. The loop ran end to end: parallel agents *disagreed* → the disagreement exposed a definition bug → the bug was filed (not smoothed over) → the fix sharpened the *definition* so the non-determinism can't recur.
- **The prime directive was upgraded from aspiration to a mechanically-enforced property — settled *before* the first calculator (Chapter 35).** The user surfaced a methodology question (do new agents go *back* and regenerate prior calculators, or build new ones in a bubble?), and the answer redefined what an "iteration" is: **every iteration regenerates *all* prior calculators from their specs with the latest agents, via fan-out (one agent per calculator).** Two reasons, the second load-bearing: (1) regenerating the *same* calculator under a newer agent makes `git diff` a clean A/B on the agent (the bubble model confounds "agent got better" with "this calculator was easier"); (2) *"any fix that lives only in the code gets erased on the next sweep"* — so the sweep *physically forces* durable knowledge up into the agent/spec/tests, turning *"agent-first, never hand-edit code"* from a guideline into an enforced invariant. The model demands **specs become first-class** (regenerate *from spec*, not by refactoring old code) — deliberately left as principles-only (concrete spec format deferred to #6) to avoid over-documenting ahead of evidence. Encoded across `CLAUDE.md` rule #1, `pm.md`, and (via the pm agent, in its lane) `ARCHITECTURE.md §0/§3.1` + `INDEX.md` *before* the historian was permitted to journal it — the user explicitly gating journaling on the decision being *landed*, not merely *said*.
- **The lane discipline held under session-end pressure (Chapter 35).** Asked to document the sweep model, the main thread refused to write `docs/` itself — it edited `pm.md` + `CLAUDE.md` (its lane) and **dispatched the pm agent** to write the `docs/` half (the sole `docs/` writer per its own charter), accepting the extra hop rather than moving fast and crossing the boundary. The lane model the experiment built was honored even when speed was the easy temptation.
- **The first agent was renamed, with history left deliberately faithful (Chapter 36).** `pm` → `project-manager-agent`: the def file `git mv`'d (tracked as a true rename), `name:`/self-IDs/commit-token updated, and live pointers (`CLAUDE.md`, `INDEX.md`, `ARCHITECTURE.md`) repointed — but the **dated** record (`JOURNEY.md`, the `superpowers/` plans/specs) left reading `pm` on purpose, so the historical snapshots stay faithful to *what things were called when they happened*; this chapter is the pointer that explains the transition. PR #26 merged. A noted-but-unscoped observation: two agents now carry an `-agent` suffix (`project-manager-agent`, `dependabot-agent`) while three are bare — naming consistency flagged as a possible future pass, not folded into the rename.

- **A maximally-clean boundary was reversed on a value judgment the process had missed (Chapter 37).** The brainstorming gate logically narrowed the backend agent to a single-file scribe (Approach B *was* the cleanest boundary by the rule on the page), and the assistant wrote the full design around it — then the user rejected the whole frame: *"this feels icky … value is degrading."* The settled model is the inverse: **backend = everything server-side of the route (route included); frontend = everything client-side; fan-out safety comes from worktree isolation, not from narrowing scope.** The data point: a process that optimizes for boundary-cleanliness can hollow out an agent's value, and the user caught it by trusting an instinct over the tidy logic. The assistant agreed it was draining the value out of the agent and verified worktrees were actually available before banking on the reframe.
- **The first-measurement was deliberately kept uncontaminated (Chapters 38–39).** The shared scaffolding (`Calculators::Base`, the dynamic controller, the route, the initializer) was **hand-built** in the main thread — but Percentage, the experiment's first calculator, was *pointedly excluded*, the controller test driven instead by a test-only `Calculators::TestDouble`. The line drawn: *"infra is hand-built (generic, never regenerated per sweep); every calculator, starting with Percentage, is the agent's output."* Hand-building the first calculator as "groundwork" would have contaminated the very first data point on agent output — so it wasn't done. This is the convergence measurement's hygiene being protected before any measurement has even begun.
- **Two long-standing bugs in `ARCHITECTURE.md`'s own illustrative snippets were caught by running the code, and hardened in the doc (Chapter 39).** Transcribing §2–4 into real scaffolding surfaced (1) a Zeitwerk namespace-ordering omission (the initializer referenced `Calculators` before the module was defined) and (2) a `permit!` that would 500 on any unknown input key (`UnknownAttributeError`) — a genuine robustness bug, not just a brakeman smell. The fix permits *exactly* `klass.attribute_names`, which drops unknown keys (no 500), literally enforces "the calculator's attributes are the allowlist," and clears brakeman. `ARCHITECTURE.md §3` was updated to match *"so doc and code agree (and the backend agent, which reads this doc, learns the right pattern)"* — the verification habit catching doc errors before they propagate into the agent that reads them. Final state: 39 runs, 0 failures, 100% line+branch, brakeman exit 0, rubocop clean.
- **Isolation responsibility was pushed into the agent definition (Chapter 38).** At the user's call — *"the backend agent should be the one spawning a new worktree"* — the agent owns its own worktree lifecycle (`git worktree add` as step 1, the deterministic git CLI over the unproven-in-subagents `EnterWorktree` harness; the dispatcher stays dumb; removal deferred to the post-merge human cleanup since the agent never merges). The `Agent` tool's dispatch-time `isolation:"worktree"` was explicitly *not* used. This makes each fan-out worker self-isolating with a deterministic primitive — the more autonomous, more fan-out-honest design.
- **The frontier crossed into `main` — apparatus complete, measurement still at zero (Chapter 40).** The backend agent + its scaffolding merged via PR #28 (`75a0c8e`, **#6 closed**), through the full human-gated ritual (CI green, visual gate 4/4 UNCHANGED by hash even on a backend-only diff). Asked *"anything I can do to test this?"*, the assistant answered with notable candor — it is *"raw backend with no UI yet — and no *real* calculator yet,"* the dev endpoint can only 404 — and instead of leaving it abstract, *demonstrated the contract through the test double*, reporting the literal 200/422/404 envelopes (incl. BigDecimal-as-JSON-string `"42.0"`, the §4 precision-safe shape). The honest framing: *"The experiment's measurement apparatus is fully in place — what's missing is the first data point."*
- **The spec format — the artifact the whole experiment rests on — was settled before the first spec was authored (Chapter 41).** Offered "formalize the template first (clean/durable)" vs "hand-author one issue now (faster)," the user chose the disciplined path; `spec:v1` is now the authoritative format in `ARCHITECTURE.md §3.2`: *a calculator's spec **is** its `engineering` issue body* (marker + Category/Source/Complexity/Tags + Intent/Inputs/Outputs/Reference values/Notes). The long-deferred §0/§3.1 "spec format = TBD until #6" notes were resolved (the gating condition — the backend agent existing — was met). Both producer and consumer were updated to the same single source of truth: the **PM authors** `spec:v1` bodies and owns the **reference-value pins** (derived from calculator.net via WebFetch), the **backend agent reproduces** them verbatim with **no web access** — a split that keeps the regeneration-sweep A/B signal clean of calculator.net drift. Merged via PR #29 (`614e18b`). This was main-thread work by deliberate lane choice (agent-def + convention), contrasting Chapter 35 where the *docs/* half was delegated to the PM.
- **The PM's real test has finally begun — its first production spec (Chapter 42).** On #29's merge the PM was dispatched to author the **Percentage** `spec:v1` `engineering` issue (intent, `base`/`rate` decimals, `value` output, reference rows incl. edge cases, derived from the source). This is the work the PM was built for and, per Chapter 19, the exercise that was supposed to be its real test — *"when calculators finally exercise the PM."* That moment is now. A human-review gate on the reference-value pins was set up *before* any build agent is dispatched (*"those values are the correctness pins the build is graded against"*). The session closed with the PM still running — spec not yet returned; the measurement is being *set up*, not yet *taken*.

- **The first PM spec was incomplete, the human caught it by looking at the source, and the correction ran through the artifact (Chapter 43).** The PM's first Percentage spec encoded only the primary mode; the user noticed *"there is more on that page"* and sent the PM back — advisory-only, touching nothing — to enumerate the page's six widgets / four distinct formulas, cross-check the inventory, and recommend a structure. It recommended (and the user chose) **expanding #30 into one multi-mode calculator** (5 modes, conditional validation, complexity 2→3). The data point: the PM produced a *thin* first artifact, the gap was caught by the human's eye on the upstream page, and the richer spec was chosen *deliberately to make the first measurement harder* — exactly the complexity the iteration loop exists to converge on.
- **The measurement machinery was engaged for the first time (Chapter 44).** Iteration **0001** was opened by the PM — pinned `c7796eb`, tagged `iteration-0001`, logged with the baseline agent set — the first use of the iteration apparatus built across ~40 chapters. There is now a tagged baseline against which the first calculator's output and every future regeneration can be diffed. The PM's direct-to-`main` push of the iteration commit+tag was flagged by the harness and surfaced honestly by the assistant, seeding the PR-first decision.
- **Two structural corrections were folded in mid-flight, the second sharpening the experiment's core artifact (Chapter 45).** The user's "side note" exposed that `spec:v1` was implicitly *backend-shaped*; the fix made the spec→regenerate symmetry explicit for **both** layers — one issue per layer per calculator (`backend` #30 + `frontend` #31, both carrying the identical full spec), so each agent has its own spec to regenerate from each sweep. In the same stretch, **PR-first for all of `main`** was codified (`CLAUDE.md`, PM def, `ARCHITECTURE.md`, `historian.md`) — including the journal itself, which the assistant then began *holding* until a PR-able moment rather than committing to `main` (*"a deliberate choice, not an oversight"*). The codification commit was an explicitly-surfaced, human-authorized direct-to-`main` bootstrap exception.
- **THE FIRST REAL MEASUREMENT WAS TAKEN — the backend agent produced the experiment's first calculator (Chapter 46).** From its `spec:v1` issue alone, in its own worktree, the backend agent built `Calculators::Percentage`: 5 modes, conditional per-mode validation, division-by-zero as 422 (not exceptions), `BigDecimal` throughout, 33 tests, **100% line+branch**, rubocop+brakeman clean, **zero route edits** (correctly — rule #3). The assistant's read: *"For a first raw agent output against a spec, this is a good result."* The loop the whole experiment exists to run finally has its first datum — and the agent **self-surfaced three flags** (the `send`-dispatch multi-mode pattern, numericality-vs-`:decimal` coercion, the parallel-coverage quirk) that are the *raw feedback material* for the next agent improvement. Two user questions ("no route created?", "where's CI?") were answered by *verifying the architecture is working as designed* (the registration-free single route is the feature, not a miss). **PR #32 merged, #30 closed.** Thesis still unproven (one calculator ≠ convergence), but the loop has now *produced* the thing it measures.
- **The page-serving seam was bootstrapped once, by the backend agent, with the boundary holding under pressure (Chapter 47).** The first calculator page unavoidably couples BE+FE (a `GET` route + `show` action + tested render branches); the backend agent built it (PR #34, `Closes #33`, 100%) and — when Rails 8 fought `render_views off` — covered the render branches via a **test-only view path** under `test/support/` rather than reaching into `app/views/`. The claim under test: this is a *one-time* cost, after which every calculator page is pure FE. An intentional **406 contract-narrowing** (bare `POST` with no format) was surfaced explicitly rather than discovered later.
- **The frontend agent was measured, and its self-review caught its own bug (Chapter 48).** PR #35 (`Closes #31`): the full multi-mode page (tab pills, per-mode inputs, increase/decrease toggle, bespoke result card, Stimulus, BLEND `@layer` components), 100% coverage, system tests green. The stronger signal than the page: the agent's **mandatory screenshot self-review surfaced a real interaction bug** (a missing Stimulus `field` target so inactive inputs weren't hiding), which it fixed and re-verified before handing off — the self-correction loop the design doc + agent def are built to produce, working. It also made a tasteful in-lane seam decision (per-slug result partial + generic fallback). The full stack then ran live end-to-end (browser → Turbo → controller → calculator → persisted row), with **13 calculations across all 5 modes verified in the DB**, anonymous and attributed (Chapter 49).
- **An agent was filed *prospectively*, while the need was still small (Chapter 49).** The user asked to invest in a **database-agent** (read-only schema fluency; answers "what's the state of my local test run?") *now* — *"the schema today is simple, so this may seem like overkill, but … this is an area of potential growth."* Filed as deferred issue #36 with the boundary baked in. The experiment's thesis applied forward: a recurring hand-action (DB queries) becomes a durable agent definition before it's painful.
- **THE LOOP RAN ITS FIRST FULL FAILURE-AND-RE-AIM — the centerpiece (Chapter 50).** The user flagged cramped mode pills; the diagnosis was routed to the *agent's reference doc* not the page (*"We're updating the agent's reference docs to give it direction, not just jumping into code"*). The first doc edit was **too timid** (a `gap-2.5` *floor* the agent dutifully sat on), and — the deeper failure — the FE agent's **self-review had looked at cramped pills and reported "breathe."** The user twice stopped the assistant from doing the agent's job: *"Don't dial it live, revert. Let's really hone in on the agent,"* then *"use iterations to iterate towards a good idea."* The response was not to abandon the loop but to **re-aim it**: merge the imperfect page as the **baseline** (reversing "fix-then-merge"), make `DESIGN.md` a real **spacing *system*** (relationship-based tiers, *targets not minimums*) rather than a floor, and defer the self-review weakness as a named future `frontend.md` change. This is the experiment's own loop *visibly failing and being corrected at the agent layer* — the strongest case study yet, and the first time the method was caught producing a weak result and fixed *by sharpening the inputs, not patching the output*.
- **The first regeneration sweep was set up as a clean single-variable bisect (Chapter 51).** Iteration **0002** opened (pinned `1e18ce2`, tag `iteration-0002`, PR #39) and the FE agent was dispatched to **regenerate the Percentage page from spec** — not patch it — with exactly *one* variable changed (the `DESIGN.md` spacing scale; `frontend.md`'s self-review held constant). The user named it: *"we'll bisect which part of the agent to work on next."* The outcome isolates cause — roomy pills ⇒ DESIGN.md was the lever; still-cramped ⇒ the binding constraint is the agent's *eye* (→ iteration 0003 targets `frontend.md`). **The regeneration outcome is not yet known** (the FE agent was still building at the window's close) — journaled at "dispatched + awaiting measurement." The honest caveat held: a single regen is a strong signal, not proof, given stochastic output.
- **An operating principle was banked and immediately practiced (Chapter 51):** *"always stay ahead of the airplane"* — proactively background any unblocked work. The assistant saved it to memory and, in the same turn, acted on it by firing the held-back historian in a PR-compliant worktree.

- **THE CONVERGENCE LOOP WAS CONFIRMED END-TO-END FOR THE FIRST TIME — the experiment's central claim now has a datum (Chapter 52).** The iteration-0002 bisect returned: the FE agent regenerated the Percentage page from spec with *only* the `DESIGN.md` spacing scale changed (its self-review held constant, no prior code read, zero hand-edited pixels), and the regenerated mode pills came out *visibly roomier* — wrapping into rows that "breathe," with real column gaps and a larger between-row gap — against the cramped iteration-0001 baseline. The assistant called it *"a clean win"* and stated the result: *"improve the agent's reference doc → measurably better output, proven by the regeneration sweep."* The user independently confirmed by eye on the running server (*"This looks amazing relative to before! Our work did the trick"*). **The bisect verdict: the lever was the *design doc*, not the FE self-review** — the self-review proved *adequate once it had a concrete standard to measure against*, so the deferred `frontend.md` taste pass (Chapter 50's named candidate) was *not* this round's binding constraint. The honest caveat held throughout: **one stochastic run is a strong signal, not statistical proof.** This is the first time the assessment can say the *convergence thesis* — not just the *discipline* — has positive evidence.
- **The regeneration sweep demonstrated its second function — drift detection — on its first real run (Chapter 52).** Because the page was rebuilt from spec rather than patched, the sweep re-exposed everything the spec doesn't pin: the intro/result copy was rewritten, and input placeholders downgraded from helpful hints (`"e.g. 50"`) to `"0"`. Neither is a defect; both are the regeneration re-deriving the unpinned surface, and the placeholder change was logged as a candidate future `DESIGN.md` refinement. The sweep doesn't only test the change you made — it makes *unpinned drift* a thing you see and decide about. Conversely, the label-based validation errors + hero result card (Chapter 50's correctness affordances) **survived the rebuild** even though they were unpinned — the agent reproduced them from `DESIGN.md` + spec (Chapter 55).
- **The operator's own working habits are now being promoted into the shared contract (Chapter 53).** The "stay ahead of the airplane" orchestration principle moved from *personal memory* into `CLAUDE.md` (PR #40), with the memory copy deleted to prevent drift. The tradeoff was named (repo-scoped, won't follow the operator to other repos) and judged correct here. A data point about what the experiment is maturing into: it is no longer only product/build conventions being codified, but the operator's *orchestration method itself* — and the principle was practiced in the same turn it was written (firing the PR's `ci-monitor` "in the same breath," running the visual gate the moment CI went green).
- **The context-delivery mechanism was investigated authoritatively, validating the repo's by-reference stance (Chapter 54).** Rather than guess, the assistant fired the claude-code-guide and confirmed: subagents receive only their agent-definition file + the Task prompt; they get `CLAUDE.md` *automatically* only if `settingSources` is configured, otherwise not. This **explains why** the repo's long-standing "every agent reads `CLAUDE.md` as Step 0" rule works — `CLAUDE.md` is the centralized channel delivered *by-reference* via the mandated read-step, robust regardless of `settingSources`. Worktrees were then standardized as a **standing default for every writing agent** (build agents, PM, historian), with a carve-out for report-only agents — the rule living *once* in `CLAUDE.md`, no per-agent duplication (PR #43). A by-product: `historian.md` was fixed to be self-contained (worktree → commit → push → docs PR), closing the pre-PR-first "commit-but-don't-push" wrinkle named in Chapter 51.
- **A misleading visual-gate flag was explained rather than rationalized (Chapter 55).** Both #41 and #42 flagged `03-login-failed` as `NEW`; the assistant noticed a **docs-only PR (#42) flagged it too** — and a docs change can't alter rendering — so deduced the `NEW` was a baseline-set gap, not a regression, and confirmed by viewing the (normal) page once. The deterministic gate (Chapters 27/32) held: a surprising diff was *reasoned through* by cross-reference, not waved past. All three PRs (#41/#42/#43) merged human-gated, every worktree cleaned up, the iteration-0002 close delegated to the PM in its lane.
- **A new agent was *designed* before being built, and its founding issue was found to contradict a boundary (Chapter 56).** The `database-agent` (#36) was brainstormed through the superpowers brainstorming skill — a spec, not yet an agent def. Two data points: (1) the issue's motivation (*"no more one-off scripts"*) implied a *lane violation* (scripts are the backend agent's territory, and this agent is read-only), so the value was **relocated from a committed script into the agent definition's schema fluency** — the agent-first principle applied to a new agent's own charter; and (2) the design's load-bearing idea came from the **operator**, not the assistant: a live `information_schema` census the agent runs on launch (*"a query … that builds a report of the entire schema in Postgres itself … can, by definition, never get stale"*), reconciled against a hand-written overlay so undocumented schema surfaces as explicit *"negative context"* rather than a silent blind spot. The assistant validated both census queries against the real dev DB before baking them in (*"I don't want to spec a query I haven't seen run"*). The spec is committed on a branch at the user-review gate (`270e9c2`), not merged — the agent itself does not yet exist.

- **The `database-agent` was *built and merged* — and validated by a live behavioral test of the registered agent (Chapter 57).** Chapter 56's spec became a plan, then the actual `.claude/agents/database-agent.md`, then PR #47 (`Closes #36`, **merged**). The strongest evidence is the **smoke test**: the *real registered* `database-agent` was dispatched against the literal motivating question and, reading its def cold, ran both census steps with reconciliation, **discovered the five Percentage modes from the data** (not from a baked-in taxonomy), reported RBAC/attribution — and the read-only invariant was *verified*, not assumed (*"Read-only confirmed (13 → 13, zero writes)"*: the row count was unchanged after it ran). The seven-agent roster is now: build line (`backend`, `frontend`), tracking & record (`project-manager-agent`, `historian`), report-only watchers (`ci-monitor`, `dependabot-agent`, `database-agent`).
- **A prescribed third-party skill was adapted rather than executed on rails (Chapter 57).** The user chose subagent-driven-development, whose default shape is a fresh subagent per task + a two-stage (spec, then quality) review. The assistant ran it but adjusted it twice under questioning — (1) collapsing Tasks 1–4 into one implementer because they build the same file by sequential append (tightly coupled, not independent), and (2) when the user asked *"wouldn't inline be better for every step after this one then?"*, **collapsing the two-stage review to a single fresh-eyes pass** and doing the PR inline, on the explicit reasoning that the implementation was mechanical (the plan held the def's content verbatim) and the only durable reasons to spend a subagent here were *contamination avoidance* (the behavioral smoke test can't be self-run honestly) and *confirmation-bias avoidance* (don't grade your own homework). It also took a *reasoned exception to the worktree default* (working on the existing branch, since the prerequisite commits already lived there and a new worktree would fragment one linear effort). The adapt-don't-copy instinct (cf. Chapter 17's tenor-flow adaptation), now applied to a workflow skill.
- **A codified convention was violated by the main thread itself, caught by the human, and fixed at the right layer — mechanically (Chapters 58–59).** The orchestrator ran `bin/ci-watch` inline instead of dispatching `ci-monitor` — *twice* — and owned it plainly when caught (*"I broke my own discipline … the convention is to dispatch `ci-monitor`"*). The fix was not "try harder": the assistant argued the obvious target (`ci-monitor.md`) was wrong because *"that def only governs the agent's behavior once dispatched — it can't compel the orchestrator,"* and located the fix in `CLAUDE.md`'s CI/CD-monitoring section instead — firming it to an **exception-free** rule (the main thread MUST NOT run `bin/ci-watch`; CI is *always* verified by dispatching `ci-monitor`), explicitly **rejecting a "quick glance" carve-out** because *"a judgment-call exception just re-opens the loophole."* PR #48 merged; the now-stale "until ci-monitor is registered" caveat dropped; the rule dogfooded the instant it was written (and again on #49). The same make-it-mechanical pattern as Chapter 27, applied this time to the orchestrator's *own* behavior.
- **A perceived gap was checked against history before acting, then closed agent-first (Chapter 60).** Asked whether dropping build-status from the inventory was a deliberate decision, the assistant investigated (the inventory structure, its git log, the PM def, `CLAUDE.md`) rather than answering from memory — and confirmed it *was* deliberate: tracking is split three ways on purpose (inventory = idempotent backlog catalog; **GitHub Issues = build state, closed = built**; iteration logs = per-iteration record). But it surfaced the *real* gap the model had — **no standing, committed rollup** answering "X of 191 built" at a glance (on-demand, not an artifact). When the user chose to close it, it was done in the user's explicitly-named order — *"update the PM then regenerate"*: the **PM definition** was edited first (a new derived **`Built (PRs)`** column, a slug-rendered Source, completed-first sort; `4e1867e`), *then* the **PM was dispatched to regenerate `docs/inventory.md`** from it (`4da06c4`, Percentage at row 1 with `BE #32 · FE #41`, 191 rows preserved). The rollup is **derived** (so it respects the inventory's idempotency), not a hand-maintained flag that would fight it. PR #49 merged.

- **The blind PM cross-check *converged* — the same calculator was re-derived independently (Chapter 62).** "Let's take a small leap" sent the PM to recommend the next two calculators **blind** (the assistant withheld the prior recommendation, per the parked memory cross-check), and it independently re-derived **BMI** as #1 — *"The blind cross-check **converged** … (no nudge from me)."* This is a small but real data point about the PM's *consistency*: given the same backlog and rules, it reaches the same pick twice without being told. Ohms Law was the second pick; both were confirmed and built.
- **The first parallel multi-calculator build ran, and the fan-out exposed a real build-model constraint (Chapter 62).** Iteration 0003 built **two** calculators × **two** layers as concurrent agents in isolated worktrees — the fan-out the conventions anticipated, exercised for the first time at calculator scale. The new structural finding: backend and frontend specs are independent to *author*, but a frontend agent's screenshot self-review needs the live calculator present to render against, so the build **staged into waves** — backend (parallel, clean merges, *"add a calculator = add a file"*) then frontend (parallel, but one **shared-file collision** on `_result.html.erb` / `home/index.html.erb` / `calculators_helper.rb`, resolved by an **additive rebase** keeping both calculators). The "no central registration" rule (rule #3) kept the backend wave collision-free; the frontend wave's three shared partials/helpers are the current edge of that property and needed a hand-reconciled (agent-performed) rebase.
- **Two real-world snags were diagnosed rather than guessed (Chapter 62).** (1) A slow `gh issue view` made a 16-minute BMI build *look* stuck; the user said *"kill and re-start it,"* but the assistant **checked before terminating** and found it had simply finished — *"I'm glad I checked … that would've thrown away a completed 16-minute build."* (2) `bin/dev` exited code 0 in the background because foreman tears down when `tailwindcss:watch` (its CSS watcher) exits in a non-interactive shell; the fix was a one-time `tailwindcss:build` + a plain `rails server`. Both were diagnosed from evidence (a 200 on `/`, the exit code) rather than assumed.
- **A process deviation was confessed twice and reframed into a two-lane contract (Chapter 63).** The assistant had **file-scoped out** the visual gate on docs/backend PRs — *"a deviation, yes … I **asserted** 'no render surface' rather than **proving** it"* — and, when asked, also admitted it hadn't reviewed the worktree agent's own diff (*"I took the … self-report at face value, which is exactly the assert-don't-verify move I just gave you grief about"*). The fix codified a **two-lane visual gate** (UI-surface → view changed/new shots; non-UI → baseline diff, confirm all `UNCHANGED`), with the repo-specific caveat that *editing the math of an existing built calculator counts as UI-surface* — the exact hole a naïve path-exemption would have left. The new rule's own unproven dependency (where the `--baseline` source comes from) was filed as **issue #61** rather than hidden — the non-UI lane is honestly marked *aspirational until that's built*.
- **THE CONVERGENCE LOOP CLOSED ON *TASTE* — a new axis of the system's reach (Chapter 64).** The user questioned an *element choice* — *"is this the right UI element … The pills?"* — not a bug. The load-bearing diagnosis: BMI's scrunched US/Metric pills were **not a spacing regression** (iteration 0002's scale was applied and correct) but a **wrong-element** problem — *"**element choice is a separate axis from spacing**,"* a dimension the design system had never pinned. A frontend advisory produced a **mode-picker rule** (always a native radio `<fieldset>`; segmented at N≤3/short, option-list at N≥4/long, raw `<select>` never primary), prototyped on Ohms, previewed live via a **second server on `:3001`** for an A/B (*"Oh that's perfect"*), codified into `DESIGN.md §4` + `frontend.md`, then **swept** to all three calculators. The proof was a **single Percentage screenshot** showing both presentations discriminating correctly on one page (5-mode option-list + the Increase|Decrease segmented sub-toggle). This expands what the loop has been shown to encode — from math-correctness (Chapter 46) and spacing (Chapter 52) to **design judgment**. The user named it: *"we're truly building in preferences, almost 'taste'."*
- **The "don't trust an unreloaded agent definition" pattern was deliberately engineered — and the user probed it (Chapter 64).** The codify PR (#64) and the two sweep agents were dispatched **in the same message**, so the sweeps' launch-protocol read of `DESIGN.md` saw the *old* doc — they never relied on the codified rule. The user asked the exact race-condition question (*"the sweeps didn't *rely* on codification, right?"*), and the assistant confirmed the rule reached them **by prompt, not by document**: *"don't trust that an edited agent definition is reloaded mid-session — hand the agent the instruction explicitly."* The real dependency (the `.mode-option` CSS) was merged first (#63). A precise instance of the operator and assistant reasoning about *how* a mid-session agent-definition change does and does not propagate.
- **The assistant's honest framing of iteration 0003: reach over autonomy, and not a clean A/B (Chapter 65).** Asked point-blank whether it was forward progress, the assistant said yes — *"the *most valuable* kind"* — but with an unhedged reservation: *"it advanced the system's *reach* more than its *autonomy*. You caught the scrunch, you made every merge call, you chose the rollout — the agents executed beautifully but didn't notice the problem themselves, and this iteration wasn't the clean controlled A/B that 0002 was (the agent set shifted mid-flight)."* The iteration was logged in those same terms (*"not a clean A/B … the controlled measurement is deferred"*). So the evidence is two-sided: the loop's *reach* expanded (it now encodes taste), but the iteration is **not** a controlled measurement (the agent set changed mid-flight, reactively), and the human remained the one who *noticed* and *drove*.
- **The blind PM cross-check converged a *second* time, this round on 4 of 5 (Chapters 67–68).** Re-run blind (the parked recommendation withheld), the PM independently re-derived **four of the five** next calculators (Tip, Mean/Median/Mode/Range, Age, Amortization), diverging only on the fifth slot — its **Simple Interest** vs. the parked **Right Triangle**, on a defensible category-coherence argument (Simple Interest pairs with Amortization to open Financial; geometry deferred to a dedicated iteration). This is a second consistency datum about the PM (cf. the BMI convergence in Chapter 62): given the same backlog and rules, it reaches largely the same slate without being told. The operator deferred the tie-break to the orchestrator, which chose Simple Interest; the final five ramp 2 → 3 → 3 → 2 → 4.
- **The controlled measurement iteration 0003 deferred is now scheduled (Chapter 68).** Iteration 0004 was opened against an agent set that *already contains the mode-picker rule in the pinned set*, so its **regeneration sweep** (rebuild Percentage/BMI/Ohms from spec) is the **first clean A/B of whether agents reach for the right picker unprompted** — directly answering the "not a clean A/B, controlled measurement deferred" debt the assistant named in Chapter 65. Per human directive, the sweep lands as its **own separate PR(s)**, kept distinct from the new-build PRs so the picker-A/B signal and the five new builds don't muddy each other. The decision is made and the fan-out dispatched; the measurement is **set up, not yet taken** (the PM was still authoring the 10 `spec:v1` issues at the window's close).
- **The "don't duplicate the contract in personal memory" lesson recurred — and the operator caught the duplication (Chapters 67–68).** A backgrounding-default preference graduated from a just-made personal memory into `CLAUDE.md` (PR #71, merged) the same way "stay ahead of the airplane" did (Chapter 53), memory deleted to prevent drift. One beat later the assistant began re-recording a near-identical preference; the user flagged it (*"I think this preference is already in CLAUDE.md, no?"*) and the assistant reversed and deleted it (*"that's exactly what I shouldn't duplicate"*), folding only the genuinely-new nuance (idling on the critical path is not a reason to *foreground*) into `CLAUDE.md` via a standalone PR (#74). The contract-over-memory discipline is now self-correcting, with the human as the backstop.
- **A meta agent-authoring agent was filed, not built — the experiment's thesis pointed at itself (Chapter 67).** An agent specialized in editing *agent definitions* was named as *"the logical endpoint of this repo's whole thesis"* (fix the agent, not the code → eventually want an agent good at fixing agents), with the session's own repeated dispatch of generic worktree agents to do codifying work cited as the evidence. It was deliberately **filed as deferred issue #72**, not snap-built, because such an agent — editing the shared contract and other agents' definitions — is *"the highest-leverage and highest-risk writer in the repo"* and needs hard guardrails (PR-only, human-gated, barred from editing its own definition). The agent-first principle reaching toward its own recursion, gated behind a human review.
- **A fourth process-gap-to-contract conversion closed the session — on its own bookkeeping (Chapter 66).** The user caught that the inventory's `Built (PRs)` column had **silently lagged two shipped calculators** (*"did we ever update inventory?"*). The fix came in two forms at once — an immediate **inventory regen** (#68) *and* a durable **PM-definition rule** that *closing an iteration must always regenerate the inventory* (#69) — the same "fix it now *and* fix it forever" move the session had already made for the CI-monitor firming (Chapter 59), the agent-opened-PR rule (Chapter 61), and the visual-gate lanes (Chapter 63). A further sort refinement (issue #70 → completed block by BE PR# ascending) was **filed then folded** into the inventory PR. The recurring meta-pattern of the whole session: a process gap surfaced *in practice* is closed *both* immediately *and* as a durable contract change at the layer that governs it.

- **THE CLEAN A/B WAS RUN, AND THE TASTE RULE HELD UNPROMPTED — the controlled measurement iteration 0003 deferred is now a positive datum (Chapter 73).** Iteration 0004's regeneration sweep ran against a *frozen, pinned* agent set that already contained the mode-picker rule, and the three regenerated frontends — handed *only* the spec + `DESIGN.md`, with the assistant *deliberately withholding* which control to use — each chose **correctly**: option list for Percentage (5 multi-word modes) and Ohms (6 multi-word modes), segmented control for BMI (2 short labels US|Metric). **3/3 correct, with discrimination** between control types — the agents applied the right control per shape rather than blindly defaulting, and a new build (Simple Interest's Years|Months) made it a 4th confirmation. This is the cleanest agent-layer measurement to date and directly answers Chapter 65's standing debt that iteration 0003 *"was explicitly not a clean A/B."* The backend half of the same sweep produced **rendered-equivalent results** (regenerated math = prior output), so the regeneration sweep delivered both halves: stable math, correctly-chosen UI.
- **The first eight-wide parallel fan-out merged green — and the no-central-registration rule held at the backend, broke at the frontend (Chapters 70–71, 74).** The backend wave (5 new + 3 regen) merged **seven-at-once with zero conflicts** because every calculator is its own file (rule #3 working), proven concretely: a backend-only calculator file *"does not surface on the home page"* — `01-home` stays byte-identical until the frontend ships. The **frontend** wave did the opposite: every new-build FE PR edits the same three shared files (`_result.html.erb` dispatch, `calculators_helper.rb` labels/`field_labels_for`, `home/index.html.erb` card), forcing a **serial merge pipeline** of additive union-resolves. The assistant named it as a violation of the no-central-registration principle and filed it (#107); a later refinement found that *regens* of existing calculators don't hit those files, so only *new-build* frontends serialize. The fan-out worked; it also surfaced the structural limit of the conflict-free-parallel claim — it is a backend property, not a whole-system one.
- **A worktree-isolation leak was caught live, contained, and fixed agent-first (Chapter 72).** A frontend agent (`fe-tip`) wrote its entire implementation into the *main checkout* instead of its worktree — the same cwd-vs-worktree trap the historian hit. The assistant caught it via a dirty `git status` mid-fan-out, traced it to a single culprit *before* touching anything, verified the other six agents were correctly isolated (a one-off, not a systemic flaw), then discarded the misplaced (correct) work, filed the lesson as #98 with agent-def-fix direction, and re-dispatched with a hardened isolation prompt that *"worked — pre-commit self-check confirmed main stayed empty."* The fix lived in a filed issue + a hardened prompt, never a hand-patch.
- **The non-UI visual gate threw its first false positives — and they were disproved, not merged through (Chapters 69, 71).** A docs-only PR (#74) flagged 6 CHANGED screenshots; the assistant **held the merge** ("exactly the case CLAUDE.md says not to merge through"), dispatched a vision subagent that classified all 6 as capture non-determinism (scroll jitter, transient spinner arrows, anti-aliasing), and filed the harness gap as #87. Across the build it sharpened the instrument: a **cross-PR correlation** argument (a shot that "changes" in a PR that doesn't touch that calculator cannot be a real change) provided a token-free logical disproof, distinguishing #87 noise from a regen's *genuine* result-shot change. The deterministic gate's strict sha256 check was found unsound *only because capture is non-deterministic* — a real finding for the gate, not a reason to skip it.
- **The hand-rolled vision review recurred enough to become a proposed agent (Chapters 71, 73, 75).** The assistant noted it had *"hand-rolled that same vision-confirm subagent three times this session"* and filed #93 for a first-class `visual-qa-agent`; it kept dispatching ad-hoc `general-purpose` vision reviews against BLEND for the rest of the build (the MMR CHANGES-NEEDED catch, the Tip/Ohms/BMI APPROVEs). The visual gate matured into a routine vision-review step the contract anticipates but hasn't yet encoded as an agent — a concrete instance of a process pattern earning its agent.
- **The iteration's real output was its findings: 13 issues, most aimed at the agent/spec/Base layer (Chapters 69–76).** The build filed #86 (delete-branch ordering), #87 (screenshot determinism), #93 (visual-qa-agent), #98 (worktree isolation), and #107–#113 (frontend central-registration, frontend home-card registration, integer-coercion vs `only_integer`, non-numeric `:decimal` coercion, SimpleCov-parallel undercount, two DESIGN color conventions, headless standup). Every calculator-code finding was *deliberately not patched in calculator code* — the agents flagged them as belonging in the backend agent def / `Base` contract / spec, because the next regeneration sweep would erase a code-only fix. The agent-first discipline held mechanically under a multi-calculator build.
- **The orchestration coaching converged on "background even while idling" (Chapters 69–70).** The operator caught a redundant-memory near-miss (Chapter 68), and the assistant caught *itself* failing to stay ahead — letting green docs PRs sit without proactively running their pre-merge gates (*"that's a clean miss on staying ahead … I should have fired them into the background then"*) — and re-aimed the rule for the incoming wave. Two close-out bookkeeping agents (PM, historian) died on a transient API socket error and were handled by inspecting their worktrees and **re-dispatching fresh**, not hand-finishing — the check-before-acting discipline applied to the orchestrator's own agents.

- **A new mode appeared after the build: a post-iteration "quick wins" phase that drained the findings queue agent-first (Chapters 78–80).** With iteration-0004 closed (its PM-close PR #114 → `748bcbb` and build-journal PR #115 → `a053611` each merged only through CI + the non-UI baseline gate), the operator asked *"which of the newly filed issues are quick wins that can be dispatched via agents."* The assistant triaged all 13 findings on one criterion — *needs a human/design decision or a substantial refactor* → not a quick win — and **fanned out five in parallel** (#116 `bin/serve-headless`, #117 `bin/merge-cleanup`, #118 worktree-isolation checklist in all four writing-agent defs, #119 a home-card guard test, #120 the eyebrow-color half of #112), each in its own worktree → PR, each with the #98 isolation discipline baked into its prompt so none could repeat the `fe-tip` leak. All five went green (two `ci-monitor`s died on the recurring socket error and were re-dispatched on the operator's *"I dont see subagents for 116 118"*); on *"merge all starting with 117"* the cleanup script landed first and was then **dogfooded to merge its own four siblings** — the very tool fixing the post-merge cleanup bug (#86) used, the same hour, to perform the rest of the session's merges. Final `main` `013564b`, 0 open PRs, all worktrees cleaned, app live (HTTP 200, CSS rebuilt).
- **The quick-wins phase directly hardened the machinery this very session kept tripping on — the experiment improving its own scaffolding (Chapter 80).** The three tooling/agent-def fixes shipped — #98 (worktree-isolation checklist), #86 (`bin/merge-cleanup`), #113 (`bin/serve-headless`) — map one-to-one onto the recurring scars of the iteration-0004 build: the `fe-tip` worktree leak, the `--delete-branch`-vs-worktree delete ordering, and the foreman-teardown standup failure. The assistant named it (*"two of these merges harden the very machinery we leaned on all session"*), and the load-bearing timing was deliberate: agent-def edits change the pinned set, so they were landed *"now, between iterations, so the next regen sweep inherits the fix."* This is the agent-first loop pointed at the experiment's own toil rather than at calculator output — a concrete instance of the duct tape being converted to durable scaffolding instead of re-applied. **Carry-forward:** the substantial/decision-needing remainder stays open — **#87** (screenshot determinism), **#93** (`visual-qa-agent`), **#107** (frontend central-registration refactor), **#109/#110** (coercion conventions), **#111** (SimpleCov merge), and the **financial semantic-color half of #112**.

- **The iteration delivery lifecycle was codified — the operator named the ambiguity, and the fix was a six-phase contract (Chapter 84).** The operator caught that iteration-0004's harvest happened *after* its nominal close, ad hoc, and framed the fix as *"codifying an ambiguous part of our process for delivering each iteration."* The assistant treated it as a process-design moment (invoking brainstorming rather than firing agents) and the result was a named, ordered **Open → Build → Evaluate → Harvest → Journal → Close** lifecycle in `CLAUDE.md` (PR #130) — with the key correction that *evaluate → harvest* are explicit phases **before** close, plus **regen-only** and **layer-scoped** regen as first-class iteration shapes. A deliberate divergence from the brainstorming skill (codify in `CLAUDE.md`, not a separate spec doc — *"a doc-about-a-doc would just create drift"*) and a small recursion: the lifecycle was practiced as it was codified (0005 was *opened* before its build fanned out).
- **Iteration-0005 validated the regen-only, frontend-only iteration shape end-to-end (Chapters 86, 90).** The first iteration delivered entirely under the codified lifecycle was a **regen-only** one — no new calculators, an 8-wide *frontend* regeneration sweep propagating iteration-0004's harvest (interactive charts, responsive stat grids, Age's Today button) across the whole catalog from spec alone, backend deliberately untouched because the harvest was all frontend. The operator reviewed the live result and called it *"a huge success given what we were looking for."* This is the layer-scoped-regen rule (codified one chapter earlier) proven in practice: a harvest that touched only the frontend agent / `DESIGN.md` propagated via a frontend-only sweep.
- **The importmap self-install bet paid off — a concrete autonomy datum (Chapter 87).** Handed only the *intent* (hover-capable JS charts, install what you need via importmap), the Amortization regen agent detected the libraries weren't pinned and **installed and vendored them itself** — lightweight-charts for the balance curve, Chart.js doughnut for the breakdown — and navigated a real packaging snag unprompted (chart.js's split-chunk dist 404s under importmap → switched to the single-file `chart.js/auto` bundle). The agent extended its own remit (`config/importmap.rb`, `vendor/javascript`) to satisfy a design intent, rather than handing the install back. The counterweight, also from this iteration: handed the *same* responsive-stat-grid convention, the eight agents produced eight *correct* but *inconsistent* implementations (a shared `.stat-grid` component vs. inlined utilities at five different min-widths) — the loop converged on the *what*, not the *form* (→ filed as harvest #143).
- **The visual gate caught a class of bug nothing else could — its strongest validation yet (Chapter 88).** Amortization shipped with a **blank donut** (Chart.js handed the wrapper div, not the canvas) and a **1970 x-axis** (month index read as a UNIX timestamp); *every* markup test and the agent's own local screenshot passed. The UI-surface visual review caught both, the agent fixed them, and — the durable part — added a **pixel-level "chart actually painted" test** (donut must have >500 painted pixels, so a blank chart now fails CI). This is the precise hole the markup-only suite leaves (a `<canvas>` that silently fails to draw passes every assertion), now closed mechanically. The operator's earlier instinct to merge UI regens on CI-green alone was held back specifically by this gate — and vindicated.
- **A recurring agent-death failure was investigated to its real root cause, correcting the assistant's own hypothesis (Chapter 89).** The "socket connection closed" deaths were traced — by a scoped read-only investigator — not to `bin/ci-watch` but to a **platform-level model-API connection drop on long-lived Opus agents** (the two genuine diers, the historian and PM, died within 0.3s of each other mid-inference; three other "socket" sightings were false positives from the historian *reading transcripts that quoted earlier errors*). The repo-controllable exposure (the unbounded blocking poll) was identified; the chosen fix lived entirely in the agent definition (`ci-monitor.md`), and was refined twice by operator direction — first bounded-poll+retry, then pivoted to **retries-only** with *"always use ci-monitor now that we've introduced retries"* (#142, merged). A misattribution corrected by evidence, and a fix kept at the agent layer.
- **A structural enforcement was deliberately chosen over a written rule (Chapter 85).** The agents Pages site (`http://jayravaliya.com/whizwheel/`, live) is *generated from* `.claude/agents/*.md` and redeploys on every push to `main`, with no checked-in artifact — so it is lockstep with the agent defs **by construction**. Asked whether to codify "when agents update, update the site too," the operator declined — *"no need to codify, that's more perfect"* — because the mechanism already makes the rule unforgettable. A case where the right move was *not* to add a `CLAUDE.md` rule.
- **The first quantitative external-baseline measurement landed (Chapter 83).** The PM's parity audit put the 8 calculators at ~75% feature-weighted / **100% correctness** vs. calculator.net, and — the strategically useful finding — the 24 scope gaps *"collapse into two reusable shapes"* (inverse/multi-mode solving, input-side unit conversion), reframing the long-tail-vs-pattern tradeoff into a candidate iteration theme. Filed under `docs/experiments/` at the operator's call (not a foundational workflow doc).
- **The backlog-sweep phase opened, and the agent-routing choice was itself a data point (Chapter 91).** With two iterations closed, the session pivoted from building calculators to draining the open-issue backlog, starting with the two ⭐ (now + low-effort) warm-ups (#128 rename, #144 chart-lesson codification) — the second deliberately sequenced first-among-equals to *bank an agent improvement before any future regen sweep inherits it*. The orchestration nuance worth recording: **neither task mapped to a scoped agent** — one is a cross-cutting docs+contract+agent-def rename, the other *edits an agent definition* (the work the deferred meta agent-authoring agent #72 would eventually own) — so both were routed to **general-purpose agents**, each pointed at `CLAUDE.md` step 0 and its own worktree. This is the first time the agent roster's gap was named *in the act of dispatching*: editing an agent definition currently has no specialist owner, so the meta-agent's absence is felt as a routing fallback rather than a missing feature. The parallel-dispatch and non-UI-gate discipline held (independence checked against the playbook before fan-out, gate plan stated before either PR existed).

- **The non-UI visual gate was empirically shown to be non-functional (Chapter 92).** The contract mandates a "prove every shot UNCHANGED" baseline-diff on every non-UI PR, but on PR #149 (an agent-def + docs change that cannot move a pixel) 30 of 41 shots reported `CHANGED`. Rather than hand-wave or eyeball, the assistant ran a controlled measurement: two *different* `main` CI runs with byte-identical app code differed on **32 of 41 shots** — more churn than the PR. The sha256 baseline-diff cannot distinguish a real change from screenshot-capture noise, so the gate's deterministic clearance mechanism is currently defeated (exactly what #87 describes). This is a rare case of a *codified gate* being measured and found broken; the three PRs were instead cleared on file-level evidence (no rendered surface touched), and the `9 unchanged / 32 changed` numbers were posted to #87 as fix-feeding evidence. A data point about *the gates themselves*, not the agents.
- **The operator overrode the agent's architecture recommendation — and the repo's own rule #3 — on purpose (Chapters 93–96).** This is the experiment's clearest recorded instance of the human deliberately choosing against the agent. On the keystone #107, the assistant recommended a *code* registry (autoload `Base.all` + per-class metadata), arguing a DB table would violate rule #3 ("no central registration") and reintroduce the merge-conflict it exists to kill. The operator overruled it — *"that's just tons of hard coded data about the most important component of the entire app"* — and chose a **DB-backed, derived registry**. The notable property: rather than the agent simply executing the reversal, the operator asked it to *stress-test his own decision*, and the audit (Chapter 95) both validated the flow and caught a CI-fatal bug in it (a migration-driven ingest would be skipped because `db:test:prepare` loads `structure.sql`, running neither migrations nor seeds). The override and the audit together produced a design (table-only migration + standalone idempotent ingest reconciled against code + fixtures-for-tests + deprecate-never-delete) that neither party held at the start — the human's substrate call, corrected by the agent's repo-grounded investigation.
- **The contract was amended *before* the machinery was built, so the rule departure is honest (Chapter 96).** Because the DB registry deliberately departs from rule #3, the assistant's first act was to amend `CLAUDE.md` — reworking rule #3 to *"no hand-edited central registration"* (a derived registry is fine) and adding a new "The calculator registry" section — landing it as PR #152 and merging it before dispatching any builder. The build was then sequenced so every downstream agent reads the corrected contract as its step 0. This keeps the experiment's core discipline intact even while overriding one of its rules: the decision lives in the shared doc all agents inherit, not as an undocumented exception in code. No app code shipped in this window — the work was contract + design + issue-filing, the "heavy upfront investment" pattern, here applied to a foundational schema change.

**Open question:** the experiment's central claim — that improving the agent/doc layer produces measurably better from-spec output — now has **three positive data points of different kinds**: iteration 0002's clean single-variable bisect on *spacing* (Chapter 52), iteration 0003's demonstration that the same loop can encode **design taste** (the mode-picker rule swept three calculators, Chapter 64), and — newest and cleanest — iteration 0004's **controlled A/B under a frozen agent set**, in which three from-spec regenerations chose the right picker *unprompted* and *with discrimination* (3/3, Chapter 73). The system's **reach** is the variable that has most clearly advanced: it now carries math-correctness, spacing, *and* element-choice judgment into agent-built UI. But the honest counterweight, stated by the assistant itself (Chapter 65), is that iteration 0003 advanced **reach more than autonomy** and was explicitly **not a clean A/B** — the agent set changed *mid-iteration*, reactively, and **the human caught the scrunch, made every merge call, and chose the rollout.** So three questions remain genuinely open. (1) **Autonomy:** the agents executed flawlessly in parallel but did not *notice* the problem themselves — how much can the loop *drive* without the operator steering? (2) **Controlled generality:** the cleanest measurement (a regeneration sweep with a *frozen* agent set) is now deferred to a future iteration — does the taste rule hold up under a from-spec sweep it wasn't hand-held through? (3) **Cost convergence:** does each successive calculator get *cheaper*? Iteration 0003 added two calculators and the first real parallel fan-out, but it also surfaced fresh duct tape (a shared-file collision rebased by hand, a watcher that killed the server, a SimpleCov-in-parallel reading that under-reports every run) — the experiment is partly about *"whether that duct tape keeps shrinking."* The throughline across 66 chapters is unchanged and strengthening: the most consistently-converging variable is the operator's ability to push intent — now including *taste* — into durable, regenerable artifacts, and the system is increasingly kept honest by encoding each lesson (build, design, *and* process) at the layer that governs it. What the latest stretch proved is that **the loop's reach extends to design judgment**; what it left open is **how much of that the system can do without a human in the loop**, and whether the controlled, repeatable measurement survives a frozen-agent-set sweep. **That last question has now been answered for the first time (Chapters 70–76):** iteration 0004 opened against a pinned agent set that *already contained* the mode-picker rule, ran its regeneration sweep (Percentage/BMI/Ohms rebuilt from spec, shipped as its own PRs), and the agents reached for the right picker *unprompted and with discrimination* — 3/3, the controlled measurement iteration 0003 could not be. So the **controlled generality** question (2) now has a positive datum: the taste rule held up under a from-spec sweep it was not hand-held through.
